# Tasks

The shared ledger. Every collaborator — human or Claude — reads this before
starting work and updates it as work moves. It lives on `main` so that all
clones see the same picture.

`ROADMAP.md` says *what the project is trying to become* and why, in prose.
This file says *who is doing what right now*. When they disagree, this one is
current and the roadmap is the intent.

**Working on this file:** see `.claude/skills/ledger/SKILL.md` for the protocol
— how to claim a task without colliding with another session, and what counts
as verified. The short version: `git pull --rebase` first, change only the rows
you own, push immediately.

---

## Status vocabulary

| Status | Means |
|---|---|
| `open` | Described well enough to start. Nobody is on it. |
| `claimed` | Somebody is working on it now. Owner and branch are filled in. |
| `blocked` | Cannot proceed. The reason is in the notes, naming what would unblock it. |
| `done` | Written, tested, pushed. **Not yet independently checked.** |
| `verified` | Somebody other than the author ran it and confirmed the claim. |

`done` and `verified` are deliberately different columns. This project's
standard is that a claim is only closed by evidence, not by an assertion that
the work happened — the same reason `ROADMAP.md` records where Wave 0's
estimates were wrong. **Anything in `done` is unverified until someone else
says otherwise.**

The `Evidence` column is what makes `verified` mean anything: name the test, the
command, or the measurement. "Looks right" is not evidence.

| T016 | Spatial Interaction: the camera, and the two failures that would end the feature's credibility | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | `lib/spatial/{camera,tracker,session}.ts`; `apps/web/tests/spatial-session.test.ts` — 17 tests, the whole chain (permission → tracking → intent → chart) driven with no hardware via a mocked `getUserMedia` and a scripted tracker. **§12's architecture is the easy half** — frames never leave the browser because nothing on this path has a network call to send them with. The half that decides whether anybody trusts it is two mundane failures, and both are now asserted rather than intended: **a camera that stays on** (`stop()` stops *every* track, not the first; survives a track that throws; idempotent, because teardown runs from unmount, from failure and from the user's own control) and **a scene that moves after the light goes out** (`onFrame` drops any frame arriving when the session is not running — tracker callbacks are asynchronous and one can be in flight at switch-off). A failed tracker load releases the camera it had already opened, since a failed start that leaves the light lit is indistinguishable from the feature running. Mutation-checked, **8 for 8**: stopping only the first track, acting on frames after teardown, leaking the camera on a load failure, reopening a second stream, requesting audio, collapsing every refusal to one message, tearing the camera down on pause, and starting the tracker after a refusal — each caught. Telemetry is a bare tally of counts (`SpatialTelemetry`), deliberately not an event stream: a log of timestamps and confidences is a behavioural trace of somebody at their desk, and a feature cannot ask for a camera while quietly building one; totals still answer whether gestures are completed or abandoned. **The MediaPipe tracker is deliberately not imported** — Hand Landmarker fetches its WASM bundle and `.task` model from a CDN by default, and a silent network fetch on enabling a *privacy* feature is the wrong default for a product whose first claim is that nothing leaves the machine. The seam is defined and honoured; the implementation lands when the model is served from this installation. **Still open in Prototype 1:** calibration, the on-screen overlay, the settings panel and the feature flag. |
| T017 | Spatial Interaction: thresholds that mean the same thing for a hand that is not mine | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | `lib/spatial/calibration.ts`; `apps/web/tests/spatial-calibration.test.ts` — 17 tests. **`pinchOn: 0.035` was an unstated assumption about one hand at one distance from one camera**, and it fails two people in opposite directions. Sitting back from a large display, a *resting* hand — fingers loosely curled, thumb near the index — is already inside the threshold, so the machine reads a grab nobody made and the scene follows idle movement (Rule 2, in one number). Leaning close to a laptop, a genuine pinch never gets small enough and the feature does nothing, with no explanation on screen. **Both look like bad tracking**, and neither would ever be reported as a threshold problem. The fix is that every hand carries its own ruler: wrist-to-index-base is a bone length, so it varies with distance from the camera and not with the pose, and thresholds expressed as a fraction of it mean the same thing at any size — no setup at all, which is why `adaptiveThresholds` defaults on. The ratios are the absolute defaults over the 0.10 span they were written against, so the two paths agree for a reference hand instead of quietly redefining a pinch. Explicit calibration (§17) is two poses, not a wizard, and **refuses rather than returning a number it cannot justify**: too few samples, poses that did not separate by a margin, or a hand that moved while it was measured — a threshold placed inside the noise leaves the researcher believing they are set up and blaming the tracking for the rest of the session. Samples are reduced with a median because one lost fingertip moves a mean past the separation being measured. Mutation-checked **12 of 13 caught**; the survivor was the finding: **a `span <= 0` guard that deleting changed nothing**, because a zero span already makes the ratio non-finite and the next line rejects it — defensive-looking dead code, now removed rather than covered by a test written to justify it. **Two of my own errors, both found by running it:** the test fixture placed wrist and index base 2x apart, so every ratio it measured was half what the test said, and two assertions passed anyway because halving both poses preserves their separation; and I wrote that a *wide open* hand falls inside the fixed threshold at distance — the arithmetic says it does not at any plausible span, and the true claim is the narrower one about a resting hand. Existing fixtures in `spatial-machine` and `spatial-session` were resized to `REFERENCE_SPAN`, since the absolute numbers quoted in their comments were never that hand's thresholds. |
| T018 | Spatial Interaction: what it costs to leave switched on | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §13 requires that the vision work never degrade the rendering it exists to serve, and that was the one claim in this feature nothing had measured. **Measured first: the intent layer is not the risk.** `SpatialInteractionMachine.step` costs ~1–2µs per frame and stays flat across 50,000 frames — 0.005% of a 33ms budget. Everything wired *around* it at frame rate is the risk, and three real costs were there. **(1) State published every frame.** `onState` fired on all ~30 frames a second even though a held grab reports `GRABBED` unchanged throughout; observers are React setters in practice, so this asked the workspace to re-render at tracker rate, continuously, for the entire time the feature was on. Now published only on change. **(2) No rate governor.** A tracker delivering at camera rate (60 Hz is common) would drive machine and chart at twice the rate the smoothing was tuned for, and since One Euro's cutoffs are expressed in time, that changes how much it smooths — not merely wasted work. Capped at ~33 Hz, and **deliberately not 1000/30**: a nominally-30 Hz tracker delivers ~33.3ms apart with jitter, so a floor at exactly 33.3 would reject every frame arriving a millisecond early and intermittently halve the rate of the stream it was sized for. Dropping is legitimate rather than lossy for a specific reason — every command is a *delta* from the last processed position, so a skipped frame makes the next delta larger instead of losing motion, which is asserted by comparing total rotation at 60 Hz against 30 Hz. **(3) Work while the tab is hidden.** `rAF` stops itself; a timer-driven tracker does not, so frames were processed to produce commands that repainted nothing. Now dropped — but the camera is deliberately **not** stopped, since someone who switched windows for ten seconds did not ask to be logged out, and re-requesting the stream can re-prompt. **A real bug found in the governor's own design:** frame timestamps belong to the tracker, and a fresh tracker's clock may restart near zero, so carrying `lastProcessed` across a restart would reject every new frame until the new clock passed the old one's final value — a session stopped after ten minutes would appear to start and then do nothing for ten minutes, camera light on, no error. `stop()` now forgets the clock. **`Volume`'s paint path had never been executed by any test** — `volume-controller` stubs `getContext` to null, so `draw()` returned on its second line and the depth sort, occlusion count and per-frame work were entirely uncovered. `apps/web/tests/volume-paint.test.tsx` supplies a recording 2D context instead: 5 tests covering that the cloud draws, that many commands still produce one paint, that an idle chart paints nothing, and the React-churn property. **Three of my own errors, each caught by measuring rather than reasoning.** I first 'fixed' the occlusion churn with a value-equality check — worthless, because on a dense cloud the count genuinely changes almost every frame (419, 422, 420, 425), which is also why React's own bail-out cannot help; the real fix is to stop asking at frame rate and publish once the view settles (120ms), which is both cheaper and more readable than a caption cycling through eight values a second. My first version of the test then **passed against the broken code** because it wrapped 60 frames in a single `act()`, so React batched away the very thing it measured — committed one frame at a time it showed 45 commits per 60 frames. And the fixture used 120 points, at which occlusion sat at a constant 2, so both the dedupe *and* the counting itself could be deleted with everything still green; it takes ~900 points before marks actually slide behind one another. A fourth: the settle test used fake timers, which also fake the scheduler React flushes state through — it passed alone and failed in the suite. It now uses real timers and asserts the caption text, because a commit count would still pass if the component re-rendered without ever saying anything. |
| T019 | Spatial Interaction: the part a researcher can actually reach | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | Everything before this was an engine nothing in the product could reach. `lib/spatial/preferences.ts` + `components/spatial/SpatialControl.tsx`, wired to the P13 scatter in the gallery — the only genuinely 3D primitive, since §3 excludes enabling gesture for ordinary 2D charts. `apps/web/tests/spatial-preferences.test.ts` (17) and `apps/web/tests/spatial-control.test.tsx` (15). **The tests are almost entirely about ordering, because that is what trust is here.** No camera is requested on mount; opting in does not start one; the explanation is a step that precedes the browser's prompt (§4); `getUserMedia` is called only after a press on a button that says so; and 'Not now' is a real answer. Remembering that someone enabled the feature never resumes their camera on a later visit — enabled means *offered*, and starting is an explicit act every session. Unmounting releases the stream, because a component that goes away with a camera open leaves the light on and no control on screen to explain it. **Stored settings are treated as untrusted input**, since they come from disk, can be hand-edited, and are read during a render: `enabled` must be strictly `true` (a stored `"yes"` or `1` must not switch on a webcam feature), a blob from another version is discarded rather than migrated, malformed JSON returns defaults instead of failing a render, and every number is clamped — a stored `rotationSensitivity` of 1e9 would otherwise make the scene unrecoverable without clearing site data. Crossed pinch thresholds are repaired as a pair, because a release threshold below the engage threshold makes the gesture impossible and reads as broken tracking rather than a bad setting. A test asserts the stored blob contains no timings, confidences or history — §12 checked rather than claimed. **Two defects found by building it.** Attaching the stream to the `<video>` element was unguarded, so a failed assignment threw out of `start()` with the camera already open and no session to switch it off with — the camera-left-on failure by the one route that leaves nothing on screen to fix it; found because happy-dom type-checks `srcObject` exactly as a browser does, which also meant the first version of the test exercised the failure path while claiming to test the happy one. And the panel read **"Not running."** at the moment the camera light came on, because no state is published until a frame is processed — false, and the fastest way to convince someone the feature is broken. **I reintroduced D025's exact defect** — `className="linkish"`, a class no stylesheet defines — in the file whose whole subject is being careful. CSS fails silently, so nothing caught it. `apps/web/tests/css-classes.test.ts` now fails on any literal `className` with no rule, verified by reintroducing `linkish` and watching it name the file. Interpolated `className={...}` is deliberately skipped: a static reading would produce false positives, which is how a guard gets suppressed rather than fixed (D021). Thirteen pre-existing unstyled classes are in an explicit allowlist rather than fixed, because inventing styles for chart internals blind would change how those figures look with nothing to check against; a second test fails if an allowlisted class later gains a rule, so the list cannot quietly stop describing anything. **Still not wired: MediaPipe.** The control constructs a `ScriptedHandTracker`, so the camera turns on and no hand is tracked. That is the honest state — Hand Landmarker fetches its model from a CDN by default, and a silent network request on enabling a *privacy* feature contradicts the product's first claim. **Not verified in a browser:** CSS layout is unchecked, because the port the harness attaches to is held by another process. |
| T020 | The container could not be built on the machines most likely to build it | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | Installed Docker (Colima, userspace — Docker Desktop needs admin rights) and ran CI's image job locally for the first time. **It failed, and CI cannot see why it would.** `pgserver` publishes no linux-aarch64 wheel in any release and no sdist, so on an ARM host `docker build` dies with `No matching distribution found for pgserver`. CI runs `ubuntu-latest`, which is amd64, so the job is green and would stay green forever while the documented container route was broken for the people most likely to take it — Docker on Apple Silicon requests linux/arm64 by default. The runtime stage is now pinned to `linux/amd64` (the web stage stays native: it emits JS and CSS, identical on any architecture), with `platform: linux/amd64` in compose so the mismatch cannot resurface at run time. `tests/test_packaging.py::test_the_runtime_stage_is_pinned_to_an_architecture_pgserver_supports` fails if the pin is removed. **The pin fixes the build and not the run**, which took a second round of digging to establish honestly. With it, the image builds on ARM and then PostgreSQL crashes during migration 0001. Bisected to the exact statement: plain DDL fine, `SELECT` fine, `CREATE EXTENSION vector` kills the backend — pgvector is compiled C using SIMD instructions and does not survive qemu's x86-64 emulation. So the container is **x86-64 only**, as a hard limit rather than a default, and Apple Silicon users must use `scripts/bootstrap.sh`. `throughline_domain/preflight.py` now checks the extension before migrations and, on failure, prints an explanation naming the cause, saying the volume is fine, and pointing at the native install — then exits. It uses `os._exit` on that path only, because pgserver's atexit handler otherwise spends a minute failing to stop a server that already crashed and buries the message under twenty lines of connection errors. The check and the migrations share one process, since each process that touches the database starts and stops its own cluster. `tests/test_preflight.py` — 8 tests. Documented in the README, explicitly noting that CI cannot catch it. **One correction to my own verification.** I first reported the container's API healthy. It was not: `curl 127.0.0.1:8080` was answered by the user's own API already running on the host, and the container had already exited. Re-run on a free port, the container genuinely failed — the health I reported was another process's. |
| T021 | Spatial Interaction: an actual hand | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | The gap that made everything before it an engine rather than a feature: the camera turned on and nothing was tracked. `lib/spatial/mediapipe.ts` + `scripts/vendor-hand-model.mjs`; `apps/web/tests/spatial-mediapipe.test.ts` — 15 tests. **The objection that held this up is resolved rather than waived.** MediaPipe's documented example passes a jsdelivr URL to `FilesetResolver`, so enabling the feature would fetch the runtime and a 7.5MB model over the network at the exact moment a researcher switches on something whose entire claim is that nothing leaves their machine — and it would break on a plane, on a locked-down university network, and the day the CDN path moves. Both halves are now vendored into `public/mediapipe/` at *install* time (the WASM copied from the pinned npm package, the model downloaded once and checked against a pinned SHA-256 that the script refuses to install without) and served by this application. Installing software fetches things; the promise is about what happens once it is running. **Verified end to end against the running server:** all four assets return 200 from the app itself, `application/wasm` on the wasm (browsers require it for streaming compilation), and the served model hashes to exactly the pinned digest. A test fails on any `http(s)://` literal in the tracker. **The landmark mapping is where the real risk was**, and it is the only part testable without a camera: every line is an array index, and a thumb read from the ring finger's slot still yields a number, a pinch distance, and a scene that rotates — just never when the researcher meant it. Fixtures place landmark `i` at `x = i/100`, so a mis-index names the slot it was actually read from. Pinned: all seven mapped indices, that a partial hand is dropped rather than yielding NaN rotations, that handedness is reported from the researcher's point of view rather than the camera's (MediaPipe inverts it), and that a missing confidence defaults to 1 rather than 0 — 0 would leave the confidence gate silently blocking everything, so the feature would appear dead with the camera on. **Palm centre is the wrist-and-knuckles centroid**, and the choice is asserted: a fingertip moves as the hand closes, so rotation measured from one would drift every time the researcher pinched; the wrist alone sits at the edge, so rotating it swings further than the hand travelled. A test closes the fingers and asserts the palm does not move. Inference is rAF-driven and rate-limited to ~33 Hz (rAF also stops when the tab is hidden, which is the right behaviour for a camera loop), the model is dynamically imported so a workspace whose owner never enables this does not carry it — **measured: the workspace bundle grew 1 kB, 106→107 kB** — and the tracker is closed on stop to free tens of megabytes of WASM heap. A load failure names the fix (`npm run vendor:hand-model`) rather than surfacing a 404 on a `.task` file. **Not verified: a real hand.** That needs a camera, a person, and lighting, none of which exist in this environment — §38's test matrix is untouched. **Also unverified: the panel's CSS**, because the workspace is behind first-run account creation, which I do not do. |
| T022 | Spatial Interaction: seeing your hand, and telling the system about it | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §18 and §19. `components/spatial/HandPreview.tsx` + the calibration flow in `SpatialControl`; `apps/web/tests/spatial-control.test.tsx` grows to 22. **`CalibrationManager` had been dead code for two commits** — computing thresholds nothing consumed — which is the same defect class as D022 with the arrow reversed, so one test asserts the derived thresholds actually persist and are applied to the live session. The preview earns its place through the skeleton rather than the video: a raw feed answers "am I in shot", the landmarks answer "does the tracker agree with what I think my hand is doing", which is the question behind every complaint this feature will ever get. Mirrored, because an unmirrored self-view makes every correction backwards. Drawn from a ref on its own animation frame — **never React state**, since a frame is 21 points arriving 30 times a second and routing that through `useState` would reinstate the per-frame re-render cost T018 removed, in a more expensive form. Calibration refuses rather than returning a number it cannot justify, and the refusal is tested end to end: two poses 0.30 and 0.29 apart produce the actionable message and **persist nothing**, then the researcher is returned to the first pose rather than stranded. **Three real defects found by writing the tests, not by reading the code.** (1) **Frames were gated on a mounted controller**, so the preview and calibration were dead whenever the chart ref had not attached — a researcher could hold a pose perfectly and watch the bar sit at zero, camera on, nothing explaining it. Seeing your own hand must not depend on the chart existing; the observer now gets every frame, and only the *gesture* path needs a controller. (2) **The progress throttle dropped its final update**: rounding to quarters put 11-of-12 and 12-of-12 in the same bucket, so the bar stopped at 92% and the button stayed disabled on a pose that had already been measured. Throttling that drops the last update drops the only one that unblocks anything. (3) In my own test helper, `feed()` restarted its clock at zero for the second pose, so the session's rate governor rejected every frame — the identical mistake the session guards against on restart, made one layer up. Toggles for the preview and the outline (§19); the preview stays mounted when hidden so toggling back does not restart its animation frame. ~~Still unbuilt: sensitivity sliders and the automatic minimise.~~ **Closed by T023.** |
| T023 | Spatial Interaction: settings that take effect, and a way home | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | Completes §29's settings and §19's minimise. `apps/web/tests/spatial-control.test.tsx` grows to 27. **The slider range is deliberately narrower than the range storage accepts**, because they answer different questions: `LIMITS` is "what is not insane" and stays wide so a hand-edited or future value survives rather than being silently rewritten, while `SLIDER_RANGE` is "what is worth offering" — a slider spanning 0.2 to 8 would put every usable setting in the first fifth of the track and feel broken while working perfectly. **Sensitivity is asserted at the chart, not at storage.** Persistence is easy to test and proves nothing a researcher cares about; the test drives an identical pinch-and-drag through the whole chain at 0.8 and at 3.5 and compares what the controller was actually told. **Mutation-checked:** removing the `configure` call so settings never reach the running session fails it. That matters because a settings panel whose effect is deferred until restart teaches people it does not work, and they stop touching it. *Reset the view* is a button rather than a gesture, which is the whole point: a researcher whose tracking has gone wrong is exactly the one who cannot gesture their way home (§31). The preview minimises itself once calibration succeeds — it existed to answer "does the tracker see my hand", that question has been answered, and the visualization is what the researcher came for. The toggle brings it back. |
| T024 | Spatial Interaction: a selection the assistant can be told about honestly | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §26, which §49 names as the actual value proposition — not "control graphs with your webcam" but pointing at something and reasoning about it. `throughline_domain/selection.py`, `0033_note_selection.sql`, `journal.ask(selection=…)`; `tests/test_selection.py` (19) + `tests/test_journal.py` (+5) + `tests/test_api.py` (+2). **The mechanism is trivial; what the model is allowed to believe is not.** A selection is points somebody circled on screen — nothing fitted, no test run — and the likeliest wrong outcome is an answer that reads like analysis and rests on a hand movement. Three guards, each mutation-checked: **(1) the wording never calls it a cluster, group, population or sample**, and says outright that nothing was fitted and any difference is unquantified, because a model told "cluster 4" reasons about between-group differences as though clustering had happened; **(2) every statistic is computed here from the coordinates, never accepted** — a mean the interface asserted is indistinguishable, to the model and to the researcher reading the note next week, from one somebody calculated, and `validate` rebuilds the payload from known keys so a supplied summary is unreachable by construction; **(3) labels are untrusted text** — bounded and fenced, since a point named "ignore previous instructions" is a string to report. Deliberately no standard deviation or correlation: either invites the reading that a hand-picked set is a sample of something. **The selection is recorded on the note** (`0033`), because a note saying "these points" with no record of which points is D018 in miniature — a chain legible in the moment and broken a week later. Refusal happens before the model is called, so a malformed selection costs nothing and stores nothing. **A pre-existing bug found while writing the boundary test:** `ask` mapped *every* `JournalError` to 503, so asking about a non-existent object returned **Service Unavailable** — telling the researcher the assistant was down and sending them to check a model configuration that was working perfectly. `NoSuchObject` is now its own type and its own 404; a malformed selection is 400. **Mutation-checked 7/7**, after one mutation had to be rewritten: the first version read a caller summary from the *validated* dict, where the whitelist means it can never appear, so it was inert. Modelling the real mechanism — validate passing the payload through — is caught. **Not wired to the interface**, and the reason is worth stating: the only 3D scatter is the gallery's demo cloud, which belongs to no project and no dataset, so there is nothing a question about it could be recorded against. The UI lands when a project-data 3D view exists. |
| T025 | The corpus in three dimensions, with the loss stated | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | The prerequisite T024 named: a 3D view of *real project data*, so a selection can be recorded against something. `throughline_domain/embedding_space.py` projects passage embeddings (256-d) to three via PCA; `GET /api/projects/{id}/embedding-space`; `tests/test_embedding_space.py` (13) + `tests/test_api.py` (+2). Each point is a passage, so it resolves to a source and a locator — which is what makes a question about a selection answerable. **A projection always looks like structure whether or not it contains any**, so explained variance travels with the coordinates rather than being available on request: three components carrying 60% and three carrying 6% produce visually identical pictures. Refuses rather than draws when it cannot be honest — fewer than 4 points (three points in 256-d lie exactly on a plane, so the picture would show perfect structure that is a property of arithmetic), zero variance (almost always a broken embedding step, and it says so), and **vectors from more than one model**, which the schema comment had anticipated in prose and nothing had enforced. Truncation is reported rather than silent. **Mutation-checked 7/8, and the survivor is recorded rather than hidden.** The orientation pin cannot be failed by any local test: the `ORDER BY` already hands SVD an identical matrix and numpy is deterministic for identical input, so on one machine it is redundant. What it covers is two machines — Accelerate and OpenBLAS can return opposite signs, so the same corpus exported on a Mac and on a Linux server would be mirror images. Unreachable from here, so it is a known gap in the evidence, the way D020 is. **Three of my own errors, each caught by measuring.** (1) I claimed the sign convention keeps the view stable as the corpus changes; it cannot, and a test caught it — when the leading eigenvalues are close the components genuinely *rotate* (measured |cos| 0.5), which is PCA working. The docstring now promises only reproducibility for the same points. (2) The first fixture, `sin(seed + i*0.37)`, spans a **2-dimensional** subspace, so the third component explained 0.000 and pointed in a noise direction — the tests read as covering a 3-D case while exercising a degenerate one. (3) Replacing it with near-random values was the opposite failure: eight points in 256-d have near-equal eigenvalues (0.178/0.163/0.146), so components rotate on any change. The fixture now has separated scales, like a real embedding. numpy is **declared** in `research-domain` rather than borrowed from `throughline-ingestion` — the accident the two lines above it in that file were written about. |
| T026 | The loop closed: point at a passage, ask about it, and the answer is recorded | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §26 end to end, and the first screen where the gesture work stops being an engine. `components/embeddingspace.tsx` + a workspace section; `apps/web/tests/embeddingspace.test.tsx` — 11 tests. Points are passages, so selecting one and asking about it produces a model note recorded against the source's research object. **One backend gap had to close first:** the projection returned `source_id`, but the journal anchors on *research objects*, so a selection had nothing for an answer to attach to — D018's defect, arriving by a new route. Points now carry `object_id`, null when the source has no research object yet, and the interface **says why it cannot ask** rather than greying out a control in silence. **The screen's two obligations are both refusals to flatter the picture.** Explained variance is stated *above* the chart, not below: read underneath, a number is a correction to an impression already formed rather than a frame for it. Below 25% the caption stops reporting a decimal and says what it means — that most of what separates these passages is not visible, and apparent groupings may not survive in the full space — because "9%" is a number most readers cannot convert into "this is close to noise". And it never draws an empty chart: a projection that could not be made shows its reason, since an empty scatter is indistinguishable from a corpus with no structure and a reader takes the second meaning when the truth is the first. **The selection travels as coordinates only**, asserted at the boundary that could break it: a test parses the request body and fails if anything resembling a statistic appears. Every number the model sees is computed server-side from those coordinates. The answer is attributed to the model on screen, never to the researcher. **One test error worth recording:** the first version clicked the canvas centre to select a point and selected nothing — the origin of the *data* is rarely where a point is. It now sweeps, which is robust to the camera, the zoom and the data all changing, and is also what a researcher does. |
| T027 | Asking about a region, without calling it a cluster | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §25, implemented against the tension in it. The section asks for *cluster* selection; this product refuses the word, because nothing was fitted and no test was run — so what it offers is the passages **near where you pointed**, said in those terms on screen and in the prompt. A test asserts the string "cluster" appears nowhere in the rendered page. `selectRegion` on the controller seam, `within()` in `Volume`, a reach control in the embedding view; +6 `volume-controller`, +4 `embeddingspace`. **Deliberately not a new gesture.** §9 and Rule 6 both say a gesture must earn its place, and one that only changed *how many* points the same point-and-pinch gathers would not. Reach is a setting, defaults to zero (one passage), and the mouse honours it identically — a capability reachable by hand and not by pointer would make the camera mandatory for part of the chart, which is Rule 5's failure in different clothes. **Adding the command to `IntentCommand` produced five type errors, one per implementer**, which is the exhaustiveness check in `apply` doing exactly what it was written for: a new command cannot be silently unhandled. **A real bug I introduced, caught by a test rather than by reading:** `within()` put the *normalised* point in `datum` where `nearest()` puts the original. The normalised copy exists to be drawn — coordinates rescaled to a unit cube, none of the caller's own fields — so a region selection would have sent the model **coordinates in the wrong space** and dropped the `object_id` the answer needs to be recorded against. **And a second:** selecting a region set no anchor, so the region appeared with no question box and nothing to do with it. The nearest point is now the anchor, which is also where the note lands. |
| T028 | Runnable, and testable, by somebody with nobody to ask | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | `docs/TRY_IT.md` — a walkthrough for exercising the whole product solo, including what each startup failure means and exactly what to judge about the gestures. `tests/test_try_it_guide.py` (13) checks its claims against the code: every script it names exists, every npm command is defined, every button label it tells you to press is on screen, every sidebar item exists, and the startup strings it quotes are the ones printed. A guide that drifts is worse than none, because it is followed while unattended. **Three real bugs, all found by running the thing rather than reading it.** (1) **The port check did not work.** It bound `127.0.0.1` with `SO_REUSEADDR` and reported a free port while a server was plainly serving on it — servers listen on the wildcard address, and a loopback bind with address reuse is allowed alongside one. So `dev` started three processes and then died on an address-in-use traceback, which is precisely the situation the check existed to prevent. It now asks by *connecting*, and the test that would have caught it binds `0.0.0.0` the way a server does — verified by reverting to the old implementation and watching it fail. (2) **The startup banner never appeared under redirection.** Python block-buffers stdout when it is not a terminal, so the one message telling somebody where to go and whether hand tracking is installed sat in a buffer for the lifetime of a process that never exits. Visible in a terminal purely by luck. (3) **My own fix broke startup entirely** — a regex meant for `print(` matched a multi-line `subprocess.run(` and passed it `flush=True`; caught within a minute by running it again, which is the argument for running it again. Also: the hand-tracking model's presence is reported at startup rather than discovered in the browser as a 404 on a `.task` file at the moment somebody switches a camera on. |
| T029 | A page for finding out whether the tracking works here | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | `/gesture-check` — no account, no project data, a synthetic cloud and the tracker's own numbers. It exists because every reliability claim in the gesture layer rests on synthetic landmarks, and behind a sign-in the first thing anyone testing tracking must do is remember a password; on a colleague's laptop they must be given an account. `apps/web/tests/gesture-check.test.tsx` (7), session frame-rate reporting +4. Frames-per-second is published about **once a second**, on the tracker's own clock — per frame would be a measurement disturbing what it measures, and wall-clock would answer how fast the test ran. **It also unblocked verification I had never been able to do**, because it is the first page in this product reachable without an account — and looking at it in a browser found two real bugs that no test in this suite could have. **(1) A hydration mismatch on every page carrying `SpatialControl`.** The support check reads `navigator`, which does not exist on the server, so the server rendered nothing and the browser rendered a button — React threw *"server rendered HTML didn't match"* on the workspace, the gallery and the embedding view. Invisible to the suite by construction: happy-dom **has** a `navigator`, so both renders agree in tests and disagree in production. Fixed by rendering nothing until mounted. **(2) The 3D chart was geometrically wrong in any narrow container.** The canvas pinned `width` and `height` in CSS with `maxWidth: 100%`, so a container narrower than the chart scaled the width and left the height alone — the bounding cube drew as a tall trapezoid and every mark sat somewhere it did not belong. A projection wrong by a scale factor is the most convincing kind of wrong, because it still looks like data. Fixed with `aspectRatio` and an automatic height; confirmed by eye at 608px, where the cube is now a cube. happy-dom lays nothing out, so no test here could have seen it. The page states what a bad number means — around 30 frames a second is healthy; many more grabs started than completed means the pinch threshold is wrong for that hand — because a diagnostic nobody can interpret is decoration. |
| T030 | Haptics that are real, and honest about where they can be felt | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | `lib/spatial/feedback.ts`, `throughline_domain/haptics.py`, `GET /api/haptics`, `POST /api/haptics/tap`; `tests/test_haptics.py` (7) + `apps/web/tests/feedback.test.ts` (17) + volume detents (4). **The easy version would have been a lie.** `navigator.vibrate` is Android-only and Safari does not implement it, so on a laptop it is a no-op wearing the label "haptics". But the constraint is the browser's, not the machine's, and this product already runs native code beside it — so the local API fires a real macOS trackpad tap. **Verified end to end: all three Apple patterns return `performed: true`.** **The limit decides whether the feature is honest, so it is stated everywhere it could mislead.** The actuator is in the trackpad; a hand held in mid-air has nothing near it and cannot feel the pinch. The capability carries a `felt_where` string rather than a boolean, the panel prints it beside the switch, and the guide says it before anybody judges the feature. Where it genuinely earns its place is the pointer path — Rule 5's co-equal input — which is a hand *on* the actuator. **So the pointer path is now wired**, which the first version argued for and then did not do: a light detent on landing on a point, the sharper pattern on a selection, nothing when a click chose empty space (a tap would confirm a selection that did not happen). Rate-limited at 70ms **in the feedback layer rather than in each caller**, because it is a property of what a person can distinguish, not of any one chart — sweeping a dense scatter crosses dozens of points a second, and unlimited that is a rattle rather than a detent. `onDetent` is an opt-in callback rather than the chart emitting feedback itself: a primitive drawn into a report has no business making a machine tap, and a chart that owned its own policy would be one every host had to remember to silence. One shared `Feedback` for the machine, because one trackpad and one audio graph — two instances would rate-limit independently and the limit would stop meaning anything. Security and latency: the tap takes a JSON body, which forces a CORS preflight — without it any page in any tab could buzz somebody's trackpad — and it is fire-and-forget, since awaiting even a local round trip on the frame a gesture engages puts the confirmation behind the thing it confirms. Sound defaults off; `pyobjc` is declared with a `darwin` marker rather than borrowed. |
| T031 | The keyboard reaches everything the pointer does | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §30 and Rule 5, audited rather than assumed — and the audit found a real lock-out. The 3D chart could **rotate** from a keyboard and nothing else: no zoom, no reset, and **no way to select a point at all**. Selection is what feeds a question to the assistant, so a researcher who cannot use a mouse was excluded from the product's headline capability rather than merely inconvenienced. The model is aim-and-press: there is no cursor in a 3D scene and inventing one would be a second thing to learn, so the target is the centre of the view — rotate a point there, then `Enter`. A crosshair is drawn **only while the chart has focus**, because "the centre" is not a place anybody can see otherwise, and a permanent one would be a meaningless mark on a figure in a report. The aim radius is deliberately generous (60px against the pointer's 28): requiring pixel accuracy from the one input that cannot be precise would make the feature technically present and practically unusable. `+`/`-` zoom, `Home` resets, `Escape` clears. All three share the same clamp the pointer and the controller use — one `zoomBy`, because three callers clamping independently is three chances to disagree about the bounds, and the bounds are what stop the scene being lost (§32). Keys it does not handle are left to the browser, so `Tab` still escapes the chart. The keys are named in the `aria-label`, since a control that is reachable and undiscoverable is reachable in the same sense a door with no handle is a door. Also this round: detents wired on the gallery's P13, which was the one interactive chart without them — and the screen I had told the researcher to test gestures on. And the native capability probe is memoised: **three requests were going out for one immutable fact** (page, panel, and React's development double-invocation), with every new consumer adding another. The promise is cached rather than the result, so components mounting together share one request instead of racing several. |
| T032 | One command that says what is wrong with this installation | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | `python scripts/manage.py doctor` — Python version, virtualenv, Node, the hand-tracking model **and its hash**, both ports, the database and migrations, and haptic hardware. `tests/test_startup_checks.py` grows to 11. Written for somebody testing alone: the alternative, which is what happened before it existed, is meeting each problem one at a time, mid-task, through an error that names a symptom several layers from its cause. **Every failing check carries the command that fixes it** — a diagnosis without a next step is a better-worded complaint. The model check verifies the **hash**, not merely existence: a truncated download sits on disk and fails in the browser with a message about WASM, which points nowhere near the cause. **A bug in my own first version, caught by running it.** It reported *two failures* whenever the stack was running — because the ports were held, which is the normal state and precisely the one somebody is in when they run this. A check that calls the working case a failure is one people learn to ignore, and then it is worth nothing on the day it matters. Each port is now asked whether *Throughline* is answering there; if it is, that is reported as "already serving — nothing to do". The closing line was wrong the same way, telling somebody to start what was plainly already running; it now prints the URLs instead. |
| T033 | The video the tracker reads, wrong twice with the same symptom | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | Reported as "the gesture is not working". The strongest candidate, found by reading the element the tracker samples rather than by guessing at thresholds: the `<video>` sat at **`left: -9999`** — laid out, but entirely off-screen. A browser is entitled to stop compositing a video nobody can see, which leaves MediaPipe sampling a stale or blank texture on every frame: **camera light on, zero hands, forever, no error anywhere.** **This is the second time this element has been wrong in exactly this way.** It was `display: none` first, which a browser need not decode at all. Both produce an identical, silent symptom, and neither can be caught by this suite — happy-dom lays nothing out. So it is now guarded by what *can* be asserted: never `display: none`, never a negative offset, still `pointer-events: none`, near-zero opacity and `aria-hidden`. It sits at the origin at 2px, on screen by every definition the browser uses and invisible by every definition the researcher does. **And the page now says what is wrong rather than showing numbers that imply it.** The counters were already there and still required interpretation, which is a diagnostic that has offloaded the diagnosis. One sentence, ordered so the innermost failure is the one reported — otherwise somebody is told to improve their lighting while the real problem is that no frame has been read, and they go and change the lighting. When a pinch is not closing it prints the measured distance *and* the threshold it must beat, because "0.041" means nothing without "and it has to be under 0.035". When the pinch **is** closing and nothing moves, it says that is a bug rather than a setting — the one case where sending somebody to Calibrate would be wrong. |
| T034 | Two bugs that each made the gesture do nothing, silently | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | Still reported as not working, so I stopped guessing at thresholds and re-read the pipeline. Two real defects, both of which produce *exactly* the reported symptom — camera on, nothing responds, no error. **(1) A second hand in frame disabled one-handed gestures entirely.** MediaPipe reports a second hand whenever any part of one is visible — resting on the desk, holding a pen, half out of shot — and every such frame went down the two-handed path, where zoom requires **both** pinched. Not both pinched meant the machine set `READY` and returned, doing nothing: pinch-and-rotate silently dead, with no way to tell the reason was a hand you were not using. §17's point is that visibility is not intent, and the code applied that in one direction only. It now falls through to one-hand handling, choosing the pinched hand (nobody pinches by accident — that is the premise of the clutch) or otherwise the nearer one by its own span. Four tests, including that two *open* hands still do nothing, so Rule 2 survives the fix. **(2) I had raised MediaPipe's detection thresholds to 0.6**, reasoning from §31 that low-confidence detections must not act. That conflated two jobs: those thresholds decide whether a hand is **reported at all**, while the machine decides whether to act on one. Raising them does not make the system careful, it makes it blind — in ordinary room lighting the hand is never reported, the diagnostics read zero hands seen, and that is indistinguishable from no hand being present. Back to MediaPipe's 0.5 defaults; §31 is enforced where it belongs and where it is tested. Diagnostics now also carry `bestConfidence`, so "never seen" and "seen and discarded" stop reading the same. Also verified this round by driving the real UI in a browser: the three-step camera flow works and a declined permission produces the correct actionable message. |
| T035 | Rotation never worked, and the seam is why | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | **A units mismatch across the controller seam, which made rotation impossible from the first commit.** The machine emitted deltas in *normalised image coordinates* — a deliberate hand movement between two 30 Hz samples is about 0.01 — and `Volume.rotate` multiplies by 0.008 because it is written for a **pointer drag in pixels**. So a movement the researcher could plainly see themselves make arrived as 0.00008 radians: five thousandths of a degree. **Both sides were tested and both passed.** The machine's tests asserted a rotate command was *emitted*; the chart's asserted `rotate()` did not throw. Nobody looked at the number crossing between them, and the contract said only *"radians-equivalent deltas; the adapter decides the mapping"* — that vagueness **was** the defect. The command now carries screen pixels, stated explicitly, matching `hover` and `select`, which already used viewport pixels via `toScreen`. The machine converts using the viewport it already receives every frame, so it stays correct at any chart size. `rotationSensitivity` drops 2.2 → 1.0, because 2.2 was tuned against a quantity that never reached the chart and was therefore never a measurement of anything. Three new tests drive the **real machine into a real chart** and assert the scene moved more than 20px, that the delta is in pixel magnitudes, and that it scales with the viewport. All three fail against the old code. **And the lag, which was a separate fault and also a tuning one.** One Euro's `beta` was 0.03, so the cutoff barely rose with speed and a deliberate movement was smoothed as heavily as a tremor — the scene trailed the hand *by design*, and no amount of extra frame rate would have helped. Raised to 0.4, within the range the authors suggest for a limb rather than a mouse; stillness is unaffected because `minCutoff` governs that. Separately, the session's rate gate sat at the same 1000/33 the tracker uses, so it rejected frames the tracker had deliberately produced whenever one arrived a millisecond early; loosened to 1000/45, since it is a backstop rather than a second opinion. |
| T036 | A second chart where three dimensions are honest | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | **P15 — a fitted response surface over two predictors**, plus `lib/charts/scene3d.ts`; `apps/web/tests/surface.test.tsx` (14). Asked for "all graphs in 3D", and that request was declined in one direction and met in another. A bar chart in 3D is *worse* than a bar chart — perspective enlarges near bars, occlusion hides far ones, and reading a value off a rotated axis is measurably less accurate — and nothing is gained, because the data has one dimension and the picture has three. **`z = f(x, y)` is different in kind:** it *is* a 2-manifold in 3-space, so the third dimension is in the data rather than added to it, and the shape a researcher wants (ridge, saddle, plateau, optimum) is exactly what a contour plot destroys and rotation restores. §3 lists mathematical surfaces and fitted responses for this reason and does not list bar charts. It also closes a **claim-versus-reality gap**: P13's own docstring has said "fitted response surfaces over two predictors" since it was written, and it only ever drew points. **Three honesty obligations, each a test:** a fitted surface is a *model*, so the caption says so and the observations are drawn over it and never merged into it; a surface occludes far more completely than a cloud, so hidden observations are counted; and cells with no observation near them are drawn faintly, because the smoothest part of a fit is usually the part with no data under it. The data table holds the observations and **not** the surface — tabulating a fit would invent measurements. **The projection is now shared** (`scene3d`) rather than copied. Two implementations of the same arithmetic is exactly how the rotation units diverged: both sides stayed self-consistent and disagreed with each other. Volume was moved onto it in the same commit, which removed a fourth hand-written copy of the zoom clamp. Gestures, haptics, the keyboard path and region selection all work on the surface for free, through the one seam. **Prototypes 1–6 of §39–§44 are complete**: the interaction loop, smoothing and calibration, two-hand zoom, pointing and hit-testing, point-and-pinch selection wired to the data model, and selections becoming AI context. |
| T037 | Air Ink: the core, where it can be judged without a hand | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | First slice of §137–§235. `lib/ink/{stroke,machine,predict,select}.ts`; `apps/web/tests/ink.test.ts` — 22 tests. Deliberately the parts that can be *evidenced* rather than demonstrated: the two locks, the honesty of prediction, and a circle becoming a set of observations. **Ink takes two deliberate acts, not one** (§139). A stray rotation is undone by rotating back; a stray mark on a colleague's figure is something somebody has to notice and delete — so the tool must be armed *and* the pinch used as pen contact. A pointing finger produces no ink at any time, because pointing is what people do while they talk. A pinch shorter than two frames is refused as a mark: one frame cannot be told from a hand closing on its way somewhere else, and a dot is the most common piece of unwanted ink. **Prediction may change how a line looks and never what it claims** (§148, Rule 4/10). The visible stroke is extended about one frame along the measured velocity — abandoned at a turn sharper than ~40°, because corners are usually the part drawn deliberately; abandoned below a speed floor, because a still hand's velocity is tracker noise and extrapolated noise is a twitching tail; and clamped, so a tracking spike cannot throw the line across the frame. Predicted points are **marked**, and `observedPoints` strips them before any selection or measurement — a region bounded partly by a guess would contain observations the hand never enclosed. **A circle becomes a selection** (§160), resolved through the chart's own hit-testing rather than a second copy of the projection — the same lesson as the rotation units: when drawing and picking use different arithmetic they disagree at the edges. Each observation is counted once however many samples hit it, because the count is the number a researcher quotes. A stroke that is not closed is refused as a region and says so (a line through a scatter is a proposed trend, not a selection), and a region catching nothing says *that* rather than returning silence — an empty selection and an empty cluster are different findings. **Not built:** the renderer, the coordinate mappers beyond screen space, erasing, shape and handwriting recognition, and the ink→AI context path. Recorded rather than implied. |
| T038 | Region selection was approximate, and a count gets quoted | 1 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | **A defect in code committed an hour earlier, found by asking where accuracy is merely probable.** `selectWithinStroke` resolved a drawn region by *sampling*: it walked a six-pixel grid inside the loop and asked the chart what was at each step. That is an approximation, and it fails in the worst direction — a mark whose hit radius is small, or which falls between two samples, is **missed**, and the selection comes back short with nothing whatsoever to indicate it. The number that comes back is the number a researcher reads aloud, writes into a paper, and hands to the assistant as "these observations". An approximate selection is not a smaller feature than an exact one; it is a wrong answer delivered confidently, which is the specific failure this project exists to prevent. It also had the grid step hard-coded at a value tuned against nothing. **The fix moves the question to the side that knows the answer.** `withinPolygon(polygon)` joins the controller seam beside `hover` and `select`, and each chart tests *its own marks* against the region — exact by construction, cheaper (one test per observation rather than one per pixel of area), and guaranteed to use the same projection the marks were painted with, so picking cannot drift from drawing. `Volume` and `Surface` implement it against the projected positions they already compute. **Six new tests**: a mark deliberately placed between former grid samples and previously missed; a tight loop that must exclude neighbours; the datum carried through so a region can become AI context; an empty region; and — locating each mark by the *centroid* of its catchment rather than the first hover hit — that a region drawn after a rotation still finds what is inside it, which is the rotation-units failure mode restated for selection. |
| T039 | The FDR correction was wrong in the direction that hides itself | 1 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | **`benjamini_hochberg` took `m` from the length of its input, and the input is not the family.** The worker corrects across every *completed* run (`handlers.py:381`) and a run can complete without producing a p-value — a descriptive method, or a result that simply lacks the key. Those `None`s were counted in the multiplier, inflating every q-value in the sweep by `(m + k) / m`. Measured on the failing case: four untested entries among eight doubled every q-value, and at an FDR of 0.05 two of the four real findings flipped from surviving to withheld. **The error is conservative, which is exactly why it survived.** It never produces a spurious discovery; it silently withholds real ones, and the researcher sees a shorter list with nothing to indicate that anything was removed. A q-value is a number people quote, it has one definition, and being wrong in the safe direction is still being wrong. Non-finite p-values got the same treatment: a NaN sorted unpredictably, poisoned the monotonicity walk for everything after it, and then failed `q <= fdr` quietly — the old code returned q = 0.003 where the correct answer was 0.002, again by counting it in the family. **Seven tests, pinned against statsmodels' `multipletests(method="fdr_bh")` rather than against arithmetic this project computed**, because a self-consistent wrong q-value is precisely the failure mode. They cover a 25-test family matched to 1e-12, tied p-values receiving equal q (the input order must not decide which survives), monotonicity, the untested-candidate defect itself, missing and non-finite entries, and a wholly untested family. The ties behaviour was checked rather than assumed — walking down from the largest rank means the first of a tied group reached yields the smallest `p*m/rank` and the rest inherit it, so it was already correct, and is now pinned. |
| T040 | Air Ink becomes something a person can actually use | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | The renderer, the recorder and the page — §143, §146–147 — plus the wiring that makes the existing core reachable by a hand. `lib/ink/recorder.ts`, `components/spatial/InkLayer.tsx`, `app/air-ink/page.tsx`; 35 new tests across `ink-recorder`, `ink-layer` and `spatial-control`. **The recorder writes strokes in viewport pixels, mirrored, before anything can go wrong.** This is T035 applied in advance rather than in hindsight: ink has the same units risk as rotation and a worse consequence, because a stroke in normalised coordinates would still *draw* — a renderer scales whatever it is handed — and `withinPolygon` would then return the wrong observations with no visible symptom. Prediction is computed in normalised units *before* that mapping, so the speed floor and step ceiling keep meaning fractions of the camera frame rather than something different on every display. **Two canvases, not one** (§146–147). Repainting every stroke each frame is correct and gets slower the longer somebody draws, so the line lags more at minute ten than at minute one — degradation people blame on their machine rather than report. Finished strokes sit on a layer touched only when that set changes; the open stroke has its own. Two tests hold the line: one asserts a single stroke is painted per frame with twenty already on the canvas, the other counts React commits during a stroke and requires zero. **Two bugs found by mutation testing, not by writing tests that pass.** The prediction-history reset survived deletion because `commit()` cleared it too, so the common path never reached the guard and `clear()` skipped both — consolidated to one reset at the start of a stroke, which is the only place the meaning holds. Chasing that revealed the real defect: `clear()` mid-stroke left the machine in DRAWING with nothing to draw into, so every later frame reported `drawing: true`, `extend` found no stroke, and ink was silently dead until the researcher happened to release the pinch — after clearing a canvas, the natural thing to do is keep drawing. `InkStateMachine.cancelStroke()` fixes it. Eleven mutants killed across the recorder, the layer and the wiring. **A coordinate drift caught in a browser rather than a test.** The ink measures the element it sits in and the chart reasons in its own logical pixels; a 1px border under `box-sizing: border-box` made those 718×518 against 720×520. Measured live, moved the border to an outer element, and re-measured: 720×520, zero offset. happy-dom lays nothing out, so no test in this suite could have found it — the same blind spot that hid the hydration mismatch and the hidden video element. **Frames come from the one tracker.** `SpatialControl` gained `onFrame`, read through a ref because the session captures its observer once at camera start — a directly-read prop would freeze that closure, and a host that re-rendered would feed frames into a stale one for ever. Both the never-called and stale-closure mutants are killed. A second tracker would have meant two inferences per frame and two readings of one hand, putting the pen a few pixels from the pointer on the same screen. **`/air-ink` and `docs/TRY_IT.md` §3b** state the five things that remain genuinely unknown — whether the line feels attached, whether stray dots appear, whether loops close, whether the count matches a hand count, whether it stays fast — because every threshold in this subsystem came from reasoning rather than from a hand, and none of them can be settled by a test. **Still not built:** erasing (§175–178), coordinate spaces beyond screen (object, data, world, surface), shape and handwriting recognition (§166–169), and the ink→AI context path (§198). |
| T041 | A granular pass over the whole thing, and what it turned up | 1 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | Asked to check everything in a granular manner rather than build something new. **Green across the board**: 1116 backend and 523 web tests, 6 skips all carrying the allowlisted `no Neo4j configured` reason, `tsc` clean, eslint at **0 errors and 0 warnings** (from 7), all four routes returning 200 with clean consoles, the API healthy, `doctor` reporting every check ok, and a production build succeeding with all four routes prerendered. The one remaining warning anywhere is a `StarletteDeprecationWarning` from FastAPI's own `testclient`, not this codebase. **The lint warnings were read rather than suppressed, and one was a real defect.** `Binned`'s colour bar took the cells' `colour` closure and never used it — which read as a legend that had forgotten its own palette. It is in fact correct: the bar is painted in *ramp space* while the tick labels are placed through the same `transform` the cells use, so a value's label sits exactly at the ramp position its cell is painted with under any of the three scales. The dead parameter was removed and the reasoning written down, because the next person to notice it would have 'fixed' a correct legend. `Surface`'s unused `project` import was checked for the worse possibility — a second copy of the projection, which is how the rotation units diverged — and it is not: `Surface` goes through `toCanvas` everywhere. `KnowledgeGraph`'s `themeTick` dependency is genuinely redundant with `dirtyRef`, but **nothing in the suite covers the theme repaint**, so removing it would have been an unverified change to working code; the intent is recorded in a targeted disable instead. **Two gaps closed.** `doctor` and `TRY_IT.md` now both point at `/air-ink`, and three tests keep every localhost path either document names in sync with the pages that actually exist — a route that 404s leaves somebody testing alone unable to tell a missing page from a broken install. The route matcher was checked against real data rather than trusted to pass, which caught it rendering the root page as `/.`. **`distDir` is now overridable by environment.** `next build` against the `.next` a dev server is reading corrupted it once and turned every page into a 500 — a symptom that looks like a broken application and has nothing to do with the code. `NEXT_DIST_DIR=.next-check npm run build` verifies a build without touching what is being served; used here, and the running stack was confirmed still serving all four routes afterwards. Next's habit of writing the chosen directory into `tsconfig.json` is documented as the one thing to discard. |
| T042 | Air Ink was unwritable, and the fix was the opposite of the obvious one | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | **The first real-hand report: the line moved too fast to write with.** Two causes, both mine. The pen was using `DEFAULT_ONE_EURO` — the gesture layer's tuning, whose `beta` I raised to 0.4 to stop the *scene* trailing the hand during rotation. Rotation is a gross movement where lag is the complaint; writing is a fine movement where steadiness is. And the ink path had **no spike rejection and no dead zone at all**, though §153 names both and the gesture layer has had spike rejection since T035. **The obvious fix was wrong, and measuring is what caught it.** Reading §154 — "when moving slowly, more stabilization" — I first lowered the filter's cutoff hard. Measured against a 60px letter drawn by a hand with tremor, that made it *worse*: the unfiltered hand has an RMS shape error of 1.6px, and `minCutoff: 0.3` turns it into **11.4px**. A low cutoff is lag, and lag on a shape drawn in a second is a phase shift that reads as the shape being drawn wrongly. Across the whole sweep every smoothing setting cost shape accuracy and none bought any back — §153's "avoid over-smoothing" as a number rather than a caution. **What removes tremor is a very small dead zone**, which costs no lag: a still hand moves under a millimetre between frames, a writing hand moves several times that, so a threshold between them separates the two almost perfectly. My first dead zone was 0.004–0.006, five to eight times too large — at that size only 56% of a letter survived. At 0.0015 it removes the tremor and moves the letter's shape error by 0.05px. **Result, measured both ways rather than asserted:** still-hand drift 75.1px → 3.8px (20x steadier) *and* letter shape error 6.19px → 3.52px (43% more accurate). The raw tracker drifts 625px. **Gain**, because a fingertip cannot be placed to the millimetre: below 1 it compresses travel measured from where the stroke began, so the line starts exactly under the finger and only later distance is scaled — reach is kept by re-pinching, the way lifting a mouse works. **The record follows the pen, not the raw landmark**, and that is an accuracy fix rather than a cosmetic one: once gain is applied the ink is no longer the raw fingertip path, and recording the raw landmark would resolve a drawn loop against a different, larger loop than the one on screen — the selection would silently disagree with the picture. Three levels are exposed on `/air-ink` and in `TRY_IT.md`, because the right amount depends on a hand, a camera and a room, none of which the code can see. 15 tests, including one pinning the counter-intuitive finding and one that stops a future reader from "fixing" the handwriting level by lowering its cutoff. |
| T043 | The 3D scene never fit on its own canvas | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | **Reported as "I am not able to do anything with this graph", with a screenshot of the fitted surface torn into a fan of slivers.** Not a gesture fault and not user error: the scene was clipped by construction, at the default camera, with no zoom applied. Two faults in `scene3d.ts`, the projection every 3D chart shares. **Perspective was violent.** `FOCAL = 2.6` against a unit cube whose corner sits 1.73 units out left the divisor at 0.87, enlarging a near corner 2.98x while shrinking a far one to 0.60x — a **4.96x swing inside one scene**. A point cloud survives that and reads it as depth, which is why the 3D scatter looked fine for months; a mesh does not, because its near cells balloon past the frame while its far cells collapse. `DEPTH_RANGE` gave no protection: it damps perspective when *sizing a mark* and nothing damped the projected positions, which is exactly why the observations in that figure looked reasonable while the surface under them came apart. **And the frame never fit the cube.** `toCanvas` allowed 0.30 of the half-canvas per scene unit, which accommodates a radius of 1.67 — but a unit cube at an arbitrary rotation has a projected radius of at least √3 ≈ 1.73 before any perspective at all. Every 3D scene here had its corners outside the canvas from the moment it loaded. `FOCAL` is now 6 (swing 1.81x, still plainly a depth cue) and the fit factor 0.26, derived by sweeping the cube over the full yaw range and the clamped pitch range rather than chosen. **`tests/scene3d.test.ts` — 12 tests, and the shared projection had none before.** Both charts tested what they drew; neither tested whether it fit, because that property belongs to the projection rather than to either chart's data. The sweep is recomputed in the test, so changing one constant without the other fails: the old `FOCAL` breaks 3 tests and the old fit factor breaks 2, independently. **A visible way back.** `Home` reset the view and nothing on screen said so, which for anybody using a pointer is the same as no recovery at all — the other half of the report. Both charts now carry a *Reset the view* control, tested by asserting a pixel resolves to the same mark after rotating, zooming and pressing it. The token guard caught two invented CSS variables in the new styles before they shipped. **Not verified visually**: the browser screenshot tool returns black in this environment, so the geometry is evidenced by test and by DOM inspection, and the figure itself still wants a human eye. |
| T044 | The pinch was moving the pen, and no filter could have fixed it | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | **Second real-hand report: Air Ink still unstable, for highlighting on a chart.** Measuring against the actual task said the filters were not the problem — on a 144px highlighting loop every preset already sat at ~2px of shape error, and heavier smoothing made it *worse* (4.1px). So the instability was somewhere else, and it was: **the pen was tracking the index fingertip, and pinching is a movement of the index fingertip.** Closing thumb and index from an open hand travels the tip about half the pinch distance — **65 pixels** on a 720-wide canvas. Every stroke was displaced at pen-down, displaced back at pen-up, and pulled around throughout by unconscious variation in how hard the pinch was held. None of it is noise, which is why the whole of T042's tuning never touched it: **a systematic error was being treated as a random one.** The pen is now the midpoint of thumb and index tips, which is very nearly invariant under pinching — the fingers converge *on it* — while still tracking the hand as a whole, and is also where a pen would physically be held, so it is where the mark is expected rather than merely where it measures well. **The first three tests for this were worthless and the mutation caught it**: they computed the midpoint and compared it with the midpoint, so reverting the pen to the fingertip left them all passing. Rewritten to drive the real recorder with a hand that holds position while its pinch tightens and loosens mid-stroke — the mutant now dies. A second test bug surfaced on the way: the "still follows the hand" case moved 0.10 of the frame per step, exactly the spike threshold, so every frame was correctly rejected as a glitch and the test failed for the right reason. **`tests/chart-conformance.test.tsx` — 24 tests, both spatial charts, one contract.** `Volume` and `Surface` each had their own suite written at a different time against its own fixture, so they could drift apart while both stayed green — the hazard `scene3d`'s own docstring names. Now every assertion runs against both: the complete seam listed rather than sampled, rotation moving a mark by more than 20px, zoom clamped at both ends, `resetView` returning to the exact starting pixel, exact region resolution, and picking still agreeing with painting after the scene has moved. Writing it found that the seam is `resetView`/`deselect`/`pan`/`focus`/`viewport` — three of which no test had ever called on either chart. **The other twelve charts are 2D and stay that way** (§10, DO NOT FORCE 3D). |
| T045 | The specification stops depending on anybody remembering it | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | "Remember each and every line" is a thing I cannot honestly promise — this conversation has been compacted twice while building against the document — so the promise is replaced with a mechanism. `docs/MASTER_BUILD_PROMPT.md` holds all 236 sections verbatim with its `sha256` pinned; `docs/REQUIREMENTS.md` gives every section a row; `tests/test_requirements_ledger.py` (12 tests) fails the build when the two stop agreeing. **`unreviewed` is the load-bearing status.** Filling all 236 rows with plausible judgements would have taken minutes, and every wrong one would be a false claim of coverage inside the document whose only purpose is to prevent false claims of coverage. 37 sections are `built`, 3 `partial`, 22 `not-built` — each naming the code and the test that back it — and **174 are `unreviewed`, meaning no claim has been made**. The test caps that count so it can fall and never rise. **Six ways of corrupting the ledger were tried and all six are caught**: dropping a section, inventing a status, naming a file that no longer exists, claiming `built` with no test, letting the summary table drift from the rows, and a `not-built` row still pointing at code. A seventh guard is the one that matters most — editing the specification fails the build until the ledger is revisited, because an edited specification is precisely when a requirement disappears without anybody noticing. Verified by inserting a line into the spec and watching it fail. The extraction bug is also pinned: the body is checked for leaked `<w:` markup, scoped to the body because the header quotes the offending pattern while explaining it and a whole-file scan reported the explanation as the disease. |
| T046 | Reviewing the ledger, and the gap it found | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | First pass at turning `unreviewed` rows into assessed ones: §36–§47 and §85 read against the code, **174 → 161**. Most came back `partial`, which is the point of having the status — ingestion reads six formats and not SQL or APIs; profiling reports row counts, types, missing fractions and ranges but not duplicates, categories, candidate identifiers or units; large datasets are sampled and the sample is declared, with no aggregation, tiling or level of detail. `§40` and `§41` and `§44` and `§85` are genuinely built. **The find is §42.** The specification calls undo/redo *mandatory* and gives the reason — "spatial interaction dramatically increases accidental-action risk" — and there is no application-level undo at all. Air Ink can undo a stroke, which is §179 and a different thing. Recorded as `not-built` rather than quietly implemented as a camera-history stack, which would have been theatre: the actions §42 is worried about are deletions, filters and transformations, and those do not exist in the gesture layer yet. **§198 built in the same pass**, because reviewing §36 and §37 showed why they are only `partial` — the assistant is told almost nothing. `lib/ink/context.ts` turns a drawn region into the ask endpoint's selection payload, which the API has accepted since it was written. Nothing is summarised on the way across: the count, the mean and the range are computed in `throughline_domain.selection`, so they are calculated rather than asserted by the interface — a mean sent from the browser would be indistinguishable, to the model and to whoever reads the answer later, from one somebody worked out. A mark whose datum has no usable position is dropped rather than defaulted to zero, because a zero is a position and inventing one puts an observation somewhere nobody saw it. 13 tests. **The 500-point limit now exists in two languages and is pinned across the boundary** by `test_selection.py`, verified by changing one side and watching it fail — the duplication is deliberate, so a researcher who circles half the chart is told by the interface that watched them draw it rather than by a failed request with no circle attached. |
| T047 | Speech and gesture on one clock | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §37, §38, §39 and §199. `lib/voice/{timeline,deixis,intent,source}.ts`; 29 tests. **The hard part is not speech recognition.** Turning audio into words is somebody else's solved problem; deciding *what a word referred to* is the feature. The specification's own diagram is the trap it sets — gesture begins at t0, "these" is spoken at t0+400ms, the gesture closes at t0+900ms. **The word arrives while the circle is still being drawn**, so an implementation that resolves against whatever is selected at that instant binds it to nothing, silently. A referent is therefore an *interval*, and a word falling inside one binds to it however late the gesture finishes. Three ordered rules — a gesture in progress wins, then the most recent completed one, then one about to begin — with the forward window deliberately shorter, because binding forward guesses what somebody is about to do rather than describing what they just did. **Each word resolves at its own timestamp, not the sentence's**, which is the only reason "compare this with this" works: the two words are seconds apart over two gestures, and resolving both at the end of the sentence compares a cluster with itself. **An unresolved reference is refused, never defaulted** — not to the current selection, the last thing touched, or the dataset, each of which produces a fluent answer about something nobody pointed at. **Mutation testing caught that this exact guarantee was untested.** Replacing the refusal with "return the most recent entry" passed all 27 tests, because the only refusal case used a gesture old enough to have been forgotten — the timeline was empty, so every implementation returned null.
