The same class as D011: a read with no writer, where the empty answer looks like a complete one. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | A completed claim test now asserts an edge between the dataset object and the paper object, typed by the verdict family (supports/contradicts/qualifies/inconclusive), carrying the adjudication's confidence and pointing at the evidence row that backs it. Idempotent on the unique key, so re-testing updates rather than accumulating. `tests/test_claim_test.py::test_a_tested_claim_asserts_a_research_edge`, `::test_the_asserted_edge_reaches_the_neighbourhood_query` (asserted through `graphs.knowledge_graph`, the query the view runs, not the table) and `::test_re_testing_updates_the_edge_rather_than_adding_one`. |
| D014 | **Fixed.** `evidence` was read by `findings.py`, `graphs.py` and `objects.py`, and written **only by tests**. A finding's supporting evidence therefore comes back empty in production for every finding, which reads as "nothing supports this" rather than "nothing was ever recorded". | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | The cause was one layer up: `ClaimPayload` had no `claim_id` field, so Pydantic dropped the id the interface was already sending, and `evidence.claim_id` is NOT NULL. A completed claim test now records evidence with a direction taken from the verdict family. **A refusal records none** — `::test_a_refusal_records_no_evidence`. That test caught a real bug in the first version: `D14 not_yet_analysed` is `Family.UNDETERMINED`, the same family as a genuinely unstable result, so scoring on family alone filed neutral evidence for a pair that had never been analysed. `RunState` is the discriminator and the taxonomy says so — it is "deliberately *not* a `Family`". `::test_the_evidence_summary_a_finding_shows_stops_being_empty` asserts through `findings.evidence_summary`, the number a researcher reads. `strength` is deliberately left null: it means how strongly the evidence bears on the claim, and `verdict.confidence` is confidence in the adjudication, which is a different quantity. |

Its docstring's promise that a disagreement between two readings is attributable rather than arguable had nothing behind it. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Now persisted, and a re-read replaces the previous reading. **`test_a_re_read_replaces_the_previous_reading` had asserted that invariant since the table was added while performing the delete itself, commented "the real function deletes first" — which it did not.** The invariant was documented, asserted and unimplemented at once. `locate_claims` had never been driven by any test (only its no-passages refusal), so the whole extraction body was unexercised; there is now a model stub and three tests over it. |

| D018 | **Fixed. A finding could not be walked back to its evidence by clicking.** Found while measuring T005c. A finding created from connections now gets a research object derived from the objects of the analysis runs behind them, which is the same shape `analysis.py` already uses to record a run as `calculated_from` its dataset version — the last link, not a new mechanism. `tests/test_findings.py` +5, **asserted through `evidence_graph` rather than the lineage table**, because checking the edge alone would leave the thing a researcher opens unproven, which is how this went unnoticed. Separately, two sandbox tests imported POSIX-only `resource` at function scope and died on the Windows runner with `ModuleNotFoundError`, which reads as the sandbox being broken on Windows when what is broken is the test's assumption. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | The key test now stubs `sys.modules["anthropic"]` so the import always succeeds and only the key check is exercised; a second test forces the opposite branch with `sys.modules["anthropic"] = None` and asserts the install instructions. **Verified both ways locally** — the SDK is installed here (0.122.0), and the file also passes with it hidden behind a `sitecustomize` stub, which is the CI condition. | D020 | **The Windows equivalent of "an unapplied limit is admitted in the run" is untested.** D019 skipped the two POSIX tests on Windows rather than porting them, because the mechanism genuinely differs — POSIX refuses a `setrlimit` call, Windows applies ceilings through a Job Object. Whether a Job Object limit that cannot be applied produces the same admission in the recorded run is now unknown rather than covered. | medium | done | Claude (this session) | **The question had no answer, because it assumed a symmetry that is not there.** POSIX continues without the ceiling and admits it in the record, since refusing would make the platform unusable wherever `setrlimit` is refused. Windows **refuses outright** — `process.kill()`, then re-raise — because there it is avoidable: *"a run that silently lost its memory and CPU ceilings would still be recorded against a policy report claiming it had them."* So there is no Windows admission to test; there is a refusal, and that is what is now pinned. **The stated blocker no longer held.** The row said this needed a Windows machine and that writing it blind would be *"guesswork pushed into CI"* — but D051 established Windows is reachable from this machine through WSL interop, so the real `jobobject.py` was **run there**: it creates a job carrying a genuine 512MB/5s ceiling, and a bogus `assign` raises `JobObjectError` with *"AssignProcessToJobObject failed (Windows error 6)"*. That is the half that cannot be faked, and it is what the contract test then builds on. Evidence: `tests/test_sandbox.py` +2, running on every platform rather than skipping off Windows — one driving the executor's Windows branch through a job that refuses assignment, asserting the child is killed and the error propagates; one asserting both platforms still state *why* they differ, so a reader finds a decision rather than an oversight. **Mutation-checked three ways**: continuing unprotected fails it, leaving the child running fails it, and deleting the reasoning fails the second. Suite 1952 passed, 19 skipped. |

The first fix — excluding names followed by `(` — then broke every `INSERT INTO t(col, ...)`, where the parenthesis is a column list. The second fix still failed, because the engine backtracked to the shorter name `no` so the next character was `w` rather than `(`. | low | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `FROM`/`JOIN` and `INTO`/`UPDATE` are now separate patterns, and a `\b` before the lookahead forces the whole name to be consumed before it applies. Verified both ways: a genuinely missing table still fails the guard, and `FROM generate_series(1, 2)` no longer does. **A false positive is the failure mode that gets a guard suppressed rather than fixed** — which is what its own allowlist comment warns about. |

| D026 | **The figure renderer had D010's bug too.** `visuals.render_visual` wrote to `figures/{visual_id}/{visual_id}.{fmt}` while the row was keyed on (visual_id, format, spec_hash) — so editing a figure and re-rendering produced a second row pointing at the *same* file, and the earlier row's `content_hash` and `bytes` described bytes that were gone. Worse, `stale_renders` exists to say "this file is out of date" and was pointing at the file that had already replaced it. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Filename now carries the spec hash and the size; `0030_visual_render_size.sql` widens the unique key to include `height_px` (via `COALESCE(..., -1)`, since null is not equal to null in a unique index and two vector renders would otherwise both be allowed). Found while adding sizes, which would have collided a third way — 720px and 1080px were the same filename. `tests/test_visuals.py::test_two_sizes_of_one_figure_are_two_files` and `::test_every_render_row_describes_bytes_that_are_there`, mutation-checked. |

| D029 | **Spike rejection was operating on smoothed coordinates**, which made it dead code for the case it exists to catch. `rotate()` filtered the palm position and then compared *filtered* points against the spike threshold — but One Euro damps a single large jump so thoroughly that the jump never exceeded it. Cost: renders are no longer overwritten, so exports accumulate on disk. That is the right trade, because `artifact_staleness` exists to answer what a given export said. |

Reapplied by line number it fails correctly, naming `reports.tsx:109`. The guess recorded here was right — the unused `hit` was a draft, not dead code: `SetRegions.tsx:103` now carries a comment saying it *was* `const hit = null` with the tooltip mounted below it and given nothing, which is the unfinished state the warning was pointing at. **Verified rather than assumed**, because a linter that silently skips a file reports the same zero as a clean one: `npx eslint` on those three paths reports no problems, and appending `const neverUsed = 1;` to `Matrix.tsx` makes it report `'neverUsed' is assigned a value but never used` at 215:7 — so the rule is live on exactly these files. `npx next lint` across the app is also clean. `npx eslint` in `apps/web`: 4 problems, 0 errors, 4 warnings, after my two were removed. **Gotcha for whoever picks this up:** the lint run must not happen with a stray `.next-*` directory present — eslint walks it and reports hundreds of `require()` errors from compiled output, which buries the four real warnings.|
| D043 | **preflight reported a permissions problem as a stopped daemon, and the fix it named could not work.** `docker info` exits non-zero both when the daemon is down and when the socket cannot be opened by this user, and `_container_check` treated the two as one — printing "The docker daemon is not running. Start it with: sudo service docker start". Observed live: the daemon had been up for 24 hours (pid 317, `service docker status` active), the real error was `permission denied while trying to connect to the docker API at unix:///var/run/docker.sock`, and the suggested command correctly did nothing — which reads as the instruction failing rather than as the wrong instruction, and costs the reader far more time. Same shape as **D041**, where preflight told the reader their virtualenv was stale and to run bootstrap, which could never have helped. **Fixed:** the two are branched on, and the permissions case names `sudo usermod -aG docker $USER` plus the new-shell requirement. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | `/var/run/docker.sock` is `srw-rw---- root docker`; `id -nG` does not include `docker` on this machine. `_container_check()` now prints the group advice here and the start-the-daemon advice only when the daemon is genuinely absent. |

Installed, it is not: in the image the module is at `/usr/local/lib/python3.12/site-packages/throughline_api/`, four parents up is `/usr/local`, and the API looked in `/usr/local/apps/web/out` while the files were at `/app/apps/web/out`. The API answered normally throughout, so from outside the image looked healthy. **Nothing local caught it and nothing local could have** — every test either set `THROUGHLINE_INTERFACE_DIR` or ran from the source tree, and both make the wrong anchor look right. T013 says the container job has the best catch record in this repository; this is the second time that has been literally true. A second, smaller regression surfaced in the same session: `HEAD` returned 405, where the Node server it replaced answered it. **Both fixed.** | high | done | Claude (Fenil) | Found by building the image and running it, after the docker permissions problem in D043 was cleared. | D045 | **Both halves of the safety net were broken, and nothing tested either.** `backup.sh` and `restore.sh` pointed `pg_dump` and `pg_restore` at the data directory with `-h pgdata`, but `pgserver` puts its unix socket in a per-user runtime directory (`/run/user/1000/python_PostgresServer/…`), so that host has never been right. Worse, they only ever worked against an **already-running** server, while `update` backs up *before* restarting — so an update on a stopped installation could never take a backup, correctly refused to proceed, and read as the updater being broken rather than the backup. Every other test in the suite missed it because everything else reaches the database through `db.py`, which asks `pgserver` where the server is instead of guessing; only the shell scripts guessed. The dump now happens inside one Python process that holds the server open, because `pgserver` ties a server's lifetime to the process that started it — `restore.sh` already documented that and was built around it, and `backup.sh` was not. **Fixed, and now tested.** | high | done | Claude (Fenil) | Found by running a real update against a stopped stand-in installation. `tests/test_backup_script.py` — 4 tests that run the real script against a real database and assert the dump begins with `PGDMP`, not merely that a file exists, because the failure produced a plausible empty archive. Checked against the original script: 3 of the 4 fail. Full suite 1396 passed, 13 skipped, 0 failed. |
| D046 | **`bootstrap` fetches an interpreter when the version is wrong, but not when the version is right and `venv` is broken.** On a machine with `python3.12` but without `python3.12-venv` — the unbundled-`ensurepip` case `_venv_has_pip` already documents — bootstrap fails with "Could not create a virtualenv with pip in it" and tells the reader to `apt install python3.12-venv`. That advice works, but it asks for root on a machine where T070 already has everything it needs to sidestep the problem: the fetched `python-build-standalone` build ships pip and creates virtualenvs fine, and was verified doing so on this machine. The fetch is currently gated on a version mismatch alone, so the one case it cannot help with is the one where the version is correct. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | Hit for real while bootstrapping a stand-in installation with `/usr/bin/python3.12` on this WSL machine; the same bootstrap succeeded immediately when pointed at the fetched `3.12.14` interpreter. The fix is to treat "cannot build a venv" as a fetch trigger alongside "wrong version", which is a small change to `bootstrap()` and wants its own test. **Fixed.** Cannot-build-a-virtualenv is now a fetch trigger in its own right, alongside wrong-version. The re-exec sentinel still guards it — an interpreter we fetched that also cannot build one is a dead end, not a loop — and the distribution's own package survives as the *second* answer rather than the first, because offline or on a platform with no pinned build it really is the way out. **Verified against the exact failure on the machine that produced it**: a fresh clone bootstrapped with `/usr/bin/python3.12`, which has no ensurepip; the venv module printed its usual complaint and bootstrap carried on regardless. `pyvenv.cfg` proves which interpreter finished the job — `home` points at the fetched `python-3.12.14`, not the system one. **This stopped being theoretical when it broke a repair on this machine**, which is why it became a fix rather than a documented limitation. Two tests, one mutant killed: reverting to demanding root fails the first. |
| D047 | **The paper search told researchers it queried four databases while querying ten.** "Searches OpenAlex, Crossref, arXiv and PubMed at once" was true when it was written; bioRxiv, Europe PMC, Semantic Scholar, DOAJ, OpenAIRE and Zotero were added underneath it and the sentence never moved. So somebody looking for recent preprints, or for their own Zotero library, would read that line and conclude neither was being searched — the opposite of true, and a reason to go and search somewhere else. **Fixed structurally rather than by correcting the list**: the count is now taken from the same capabilities the source chips beside it are built from, so adding a connector moves the sentence and the chips together or neither. A list in prose is exactly what looks fine in review, which is how it got there. The same stale four had also reached a doc comment in `PaperReader.tsx`. Found while looking at the search header on request. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | `throughline_connectors.capabilities()` returns 10 sources; the lede named 4. `apps/web/tests/literature-header.test.tsx` — 4 tests, and **all four fail against the previous header**, including one asserting the lede contains no source names at all. Web 1273 across 87 files. **Also fixed in the same pass:** the search box had no `type` attribute, so it was a plain text input — no clear control, the wrong on-screen keyboard and Enter key on a phone, and none of the semantics that announce a search to assistive technology. |
| D048 | **The web suite can fail one test in a way that leaves no name behind.** On `feat/specialist-viz`, one full run of the 1430-test suite reported `1 failed` and the four runs immediately after were green — the failing run's output was not captured, so which test flaked is unknown, which is itself the defect worth recording: a suite that is green four times out of five is not green, and D037 already showed this suite can be order-dependent (vitest schedules files in parallel, so ordering varies run to run). Until it recurs under logging, every "N passed" claim on this branch names how many consecutive runs produced it. | low | done | Claude (this session) | **Seen again on 2026-08-30, and this time it left names.** A full web run reported 2 failed / 2339 passed: `chart-conformance.test.tsx > 'Volume' honours the spatial contract > rotates by an amount somebody can see` and `embeddingspace.test.tsx > asking about what was selected > sends the selected point as coordinates, not as a summary`. Both passed in isolation immediately afterwards, and two consecutive full runs went 2341/2341. Both touch `Volume` and its spatial controller, which is the first common thread this defect has had — the earlier sighting recorded nothing. Not chased further here; recorded so the next occurrence has something to match against. **Root cause found, and it reduces to a two-second reproduction:** `pytest tests/test_observability.py tests/test_workflow.py tests/test_worker_durability.py` fails five tests without the fix and passes 44 with it — the same five the shuffle seeds found, in 2s rather than 13 minutes. Three tests in `test_observability.py` must call `cur.connection.commit()`, because `observability.health()` opens its own connection and cannot see an open transaction; the commit defeats the rollback that isolates every other test. What survives is a `system.echo` run in state `running` with an expired lease and **`project_id` NULL** — the worst shape available: `claim_next` treats an expired lease as a worker that died and hands the run over, and because the column is nullable, the tidy-up every other file does by deleting its user cascades to nothing. The next file to call `claim_next` gets that row instead of its own, and it is older, so `ORDER BY created_at` prefers it. It stayed hidden because in declaration order a file in between drains the queue with a real worker; shuffle and nothing does. **Fixed at both ends** — the three tests now clean up what they commit, and `empty_queue` states the precondition the readers were assuming. Either fix alone is sufficient, verified separately; both are kept because the first removes today's contamination and the second stops the next one. **The same instrument then found order dependence in the *backend* suite, which is a separate defect and a worse one.** pytest honours the order of file arguments, so shuffling that list needs no plugin. Two seeds, both green in declaration order: seed 1 failed `test_worker_durability.py::test_worker_completes_a_queued_run`; seed 2 failed four tests in `test_workflow.py` — `test_claim_is_exclusive`, `test_expired_lease_is_reclaimed_after_a_worker_dies`, `test_heartbeat_only_extends_your_own_lease`, `test_retries_are_bounded_then_the_run_fails`. One cause: `claim_next` and `Worker.run_once` take the next claimable run in the *whole table*, and each of these asserts that what came back is the run it just enqueued. That is only true while nothing else is queued, and nothing made it true — it held by accident of declaration order. Fixed with an `empty_queue` fixture that states the precondition and guarantees it; both seeds pass with it. **The fixture is the right fix even at zero leaks**: owning the queue is a precondition these tests need, not a property of the system, and the next test that legitimately leaves a run queued would break them again. **Which file leaks is still unidentified** — every file that commits a project also deletes its user, and `workflow_runs.project_id` cascades from `projects`, so the obvious route is closed; a leak simulated by hand proves nothing because the session fixture empties every table before the first test. **Hunted under logging, not reproduced — and the hunt found a different, real one.** Six consecutive default `npx vitest run` invocations: green, 2010/2010 each. Shuffling *file* order (`--sequence.shuffle.files`) across four seeds: green. So the original sighting is still unidentified and this stays open. **What did reproduce** is within-*file* order dependence, which a default run never varies and so is not the same defect: `--sequence.shuffle.tests --sequence.seed=3` failed three tests in `tests/claimtest.test.tsx`, deterministically, on that file alone. Cause: the stored-claims block set `api.get` with `mockResolvedValue` — persistent, not `Once` — and reset only in its own `beforeEach`. Reorder the blocks and that mock survives into "located claims", whose tests then take the *read the record* path instead of the *re-read the paper* path, render the stored claim, and never post at all. Those are two different acts and the block exists to tell them apart, so it was silently exercising the wrong one. Fixed with a file-level `beforeEach` reset; verified green across twelve seeds, and removing the reset makes seed 3 fail again. Full suite green across eight `--sequence.shuffle` seeds afterwards. **Recipe for the next sighting**, since it is now known to work: `npx vitest run --sequence.shuffle --sequence.seed=N` bisects order dependence far faster than repetition does. Observed once on 2026-08-25 (first full run after the T076–T080 build; 94 files, 1429/1430). Not reproduced in four immediately consecutive `npx vitest run` invocations. No identity captured — the run was not logged. If seen again: `npx vitest run 2>&1 \| tee` first, then bisect with `--sequence.shuffle`. **The titled defect is now fixed, though the original sighting was never identified.** The remedy recorded here was to remember `vitest run 2>&1 | tee` next time; a step somebody has to remember is one that eventually gets skipped, which is the reasoning that put `_headers` into the release build rather than an upload checklist (D057). So capture is configuration: `vitest.config.mts` gains a `json` reporter writing `.vitest-last-run.json` on **every** run, success included — a green run is the baseline the next red one is compared against, and comparing needs both. `default` stays first so the terminal is unchanged. **It paid for itself on the first run.** That run reported `Test Files 4 failed | 160 passed` and, on the very next line, **`Tests 2485 passed (2485)`** — a headline anybody would read as green while four files had not executed at all. The record named them immediately: `destructive`, `projectmenu-keyboard`, `shell-nav`, `shell-panels`, all failing to resolve `@radix-ui/react-dropdown-menu` or `react-resizable-panels`. Cause was local rather than repository — both are declared in `package.json` and the lockfile, and `node_modules` had gone stale across the 84-commit merge; `npm install` restored 164/164 files and 2521/2521 tests. **The lesson is the headline, not the dependency:** a suite can report every test passing while whole files never ran, which is the same shape as this defect and would have been just as anonymous. Evidence: `tests/test_web_suite_leaves_a_record.py`, 4 tests, **mutation-checked** three ways — removing the reporter, dropping `default` in favour of the file, and taking away `outputFile` each fail it. Proved end to end by writing a deliberately failing test and reading its full name back out of the JSON. |
| D049 | **The launchers started Throughline without opening it, and told installed copies their interface was missing.** Two faults in the same path, both found by asking what actually happens when somebody double-clicks. First: `start` brought the stack up and printed a URL. For the person T071 names — *"somebody who has never opened a terminal"* — that is a window of log output and nothing visibly happening; they must notice an address in the text and know to paste it into a browser, which is the step the launcher existed to remove. Second, and worse: `dev()` printed *"Web interface unavailable — Node 20+ is not installed"* whenever Node was absent, but since **T072** the API serves the exported interface itself — so it claimed an absence on exactly the installation where the interface *is* available, at the API's own port. §123 in reverse: an unavailability that is not real is as wrong as a capability that is not. **Both fixed.** `start` opens a browser once the app answers rather than when the port accepts — the socket listens seconds before anything is served, and a browser opened on the accept shows a connection error. `dev` deliberately does not open one, which is the difference between the two commands. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | `tests/test_launcher_reporting.py` and `tests/test_launchers.py` — 7 tests. **Three mutants killed**: the headless opt-out removed, `dev` opening by default, and opening after nothing ever answered. The "start opens one" assertion is behavioural rather than a source grep and fails when `start` stops passing it. `THROUGHLINE_NO_BROWSER=1` declines it, for a machine with no browser. **Renumbered from D048.** Another session took that number for a different finding at 17:29 while this one was being written, and both reached `main` — the exact collision the ledger protocol warns about, and the second time it has happened here (D010/D011 were the first). Theirs was on `main` first, so this one moved, per the rule. Commit `ac237eb`'s message still says D048 and cannot be corrected without rewriting pushed history; this row is the correction. |
| D050 | **The advertised install line does not work for anybody outside the repository.** `README.md` and `scripts/install.sh` both publish `curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/.../scripts/install.sh` — and the repository is private, so that URL returns **404** before a byte runs. Even handed the script, its `git clone` of `https://github.com/SarthakPattnaik1/throughline-os.git` fails with *could not read Username*. Both tested as an unauthenticated stranger, the second with credential helpers explicitly disabled, because git had silently used a cached token on the first attempt and made it look like it worked. **T073 inherits it**: `updates.check()` runs `git fetch origin`, so an installed copy can only check for updates if its clone still has working credentials — reported honestly as *not knowing* rather than as up to date, but it cannot update. The reasoning behind `curl \| sh` is untouched (quarantine is set by the downloading browser, so a terminal line carries no Gatekeeper or SmartScreen warning); what is missing is that nothing can be downloaded at all. **Not a defect in the launchers** — T081 made them the right artifact — but there is nowhere to host one. | done | fixed | — | **Fixed, and measured end to end as an unauthenticated stranger on 2026-09-03.** The release is hosted rather than served from the private repository: `install.sh` returns 200 at 10,619 bytes, `latest.json` is a signed manifest, and the tarball it names returns 200 at 19,100,269 bytes — exactly the size the manifest declares. The decision this row was waiting on was taken, and the row had not caught up. **Guarded offline** by `test_the_install_line_is_one_line`: the README and `install.sh` must advertise the same URL, and neither may point at `raw.githubusercontent.com`, which is the exact shape of the original defect and looks fine to whoever writes it because their git credentials are cached. A test that fetched the live URL would fail on a plane and be deleted, so what is checked is the drift rather than the network. **Worth knowing separately:** the published build is 0df5994 from 2026-08-28, so a stranger installing today gets a release from before this week's work. That is a release-cadence matter, not this defect. Measured: `curl` of the raw URL returns HTTP 404; `git -c credential.helper= ls-remote` returns `fatal: could not read Username for 'https://github.com'`. `gh repo view` reports `isPrivate: true`. The decision is a product one and not mine: tagged **release assets** carrying the prebuilt 35 MB interface is the option that fits a private product and is where T073 was already heading; making the repository public would also un-meter Actions and resolve D034, but that is a different product. **State of play, 2026-08-26 — everything below is done except one decision.** The release *signing key now exists*: `keys/release.pub` is committed (`22bea19`) and the private half is saved outside the repo at `~/throughline-release-key.pem`, verified as a matching pair by signing a manifest and refusing a tampered one. **The only thing blocking distribution is choosing the host name.** It must be wired into two places that have to agree — `TL_RELEASES` in `frontend/assemble.mjs` (the landing page's download links) and `RELEASE_URL` in `throughline_domain/updates.py` (where an installed copy checks). Both currently hold the placeholder `releases.throughline.tools`. If they ever drift, downloads keep working while updates silently find nothing — with no error, because a copy that cannot reach its server reports *could not check* rather than *up to date*. **Decided:** a Cloudflare Pages project is enough to start; no custom domain is needed, `*.pages.dev` survives adding one later, and the 19 MB tarball fits under the per-file cap for now (R2 when it grows). **The name is permanent in practice**, because the URL is baked into every release and installed copies poll it forever. Remaining sequence once the name is chosen: wire both places in one commit, run `THROUGHLINE_RELEASE_KEY=~/throughline-release-key.pem ./.venv/bin/python scripts/manage.py release` and confirm it prints *signed*, `TL_RELEASES=https://<host> node frontend/assemble.mjs`, upload `dist/` plus `Main.dc.html`, then fetch a download link unauthenticated and confirm it is not a 404 — that last check is what actually closes this. **Update, 2026-08-26 (later the same day).** The name is chosen and wired: **`throughline-research.pages.dev`**, hyphenated to match `throughline-os` and because the run-together form misreads. Both spellings were free; the choice was made before the Pages project existed, which is the only window in which it is free to make. `throughline.pages.dev` was checked first and is **taken by an unrelated product** (*Throughline Health*, prior-authorization software) — verified by probing a name that certainly does not exist, which returns NXDOMAIN, against `throughline` which resolves and serves HTTP 200. `throughline-app` is taken too. Baking the requested name in would have pointed every installed copy's update check at a stranger's server, permanently. **Two things this row got wrong.** First, it called the host name the only thing blocking distribution; it was not — `install.sh` and `Throughline.bat` both still reached into the private repository, so hosting alone would have produced a download that 404s nowhere and then fails at the clone. That is now **T087**. Second, it named two places that must agree; there are **five** (the page, the updater, `install.py`, `install.sh`, and the `.bat`), and nothing tested that any of them agreed — the comment in `updates.py` claiming the host “lives in one place” was false. `test_every_front_door_names_the_same_host` now holds all five together and is mutation-checked against each drifting on its own. **What is left is hosting only, and it is not mine to do**: create the Cloudflare Pages project named `throughline-research`, upload `dist/` plus the landing page as `index.html` at the site root, and run the unauthenticated 404 check above. **R2 was considered and declined for now, deliberately.** The question was whether to move to R2 immediately rather than wait for the tarball to outgrow Cloudflare Pages' 25 MiB per-file limit (it is at 18.2 MiB, ~73%). Declined because R2 without a custom domain means `pub-<hash>.r2.dev`, which Cloudflare's own documentation calls *"rate-limited and should only be used for development purposes"*, *"intended for non-production traffic"*, with CNAMEs to it *"an unsupported access path"* — a worse thing to bake into every release forever than the cap it avoids. **Pages does not trap us**, which is the load-bearing fact: adding a custom domain later does not retire the `.pages.dev` address, so copies installed today keep updating while new releases advertise a domain, and R2 can then sit behind that same domain through a Pages Function with an R2 binding. Going to `r2.dev` now is the option that forces a hard hostname break later. **`throughline.research.pages.dev` is not available as a shape** — `*.pages.dev` grants one label; the nested form is reserved for preview deployments under a project you own, and it 404s. **The trigger for revisiting is now measured rather than remembered**: `release.build` reports remaining headroom past 85% of the limit and says plainly when an archive cannot be uploaded at all, so the wall is met with warning rather than by a failed upload weeks after the commit that caused it. The real fix whenever it comes is a **custom domain**, not R2 by itself.  **CLOSED 2026-08-27.** `dist-upload` at `0df5994` is live on `throughline-research.pages.dev`, and the closing check ran against it unauthenticated: the page and all nine published files return **200**, the three launchers carry `Content-Disposition: attachment` so they download rather than display, and `install.sh` and `README.txt` stay `text/plain` so they can be read first. A full stranger install was then driven from the live host on Linux — `curl -fsSL .../install.sh | sh` with no credentials anywhere: downloaded, checksum verified, worked around Debian's missing `venv` by fetching a Python that could, bootstrapped, migrated, and came up **serving the interface from the API itself with zero npm errors**, `/`, `/docs` and `/api/health` all 200, still running twenty seconds later. **Not yet confirmed on macOS at this build** — a Mac reached *installed and running* earlier at `0bd64d6`, before thirteen further commits. |

Nothing needed unblocking; the option was there the whole time and nobody looked. **What that verified, all of it previously unrun anywhere:** the `.bat` launcher parses and executes on real Windows, finds Python through `py -3`, resolves its install directory and invokes `manage.py start` — T071's row said it "cannot be verified from WSL" and that was true only of running it *inside* WSL.
