| D052 | **The landing page went live as a blank black screen, and every network request returned 200.** `frontend/Main.dc.html` is a Claude Design *canvas artboard*, not a web page: it opens with `<script src="./support.js">` and wraps its content in `<x-dc>` and `<helmet>`, all of which resolve only inside the canvas editor. Uploaded to Cloudflare Pages it renders nothing. **The reason it is hard to diagnose is the 200s.** Pages falls back to `index.html` for unknown paths, so the request for the missing `support.js` is answered with HTML; the browser fails on a syntax error rather than a 404, and a network tab shows success throughout. Nothing 404s, nothing logs, the page is simply empty. **Not a new defect so much as an unverified instruction.** `frontend/README.md` said *"Upload `Main.dc.html` as the page"* and D050's closing sequence said *"upload `dist/` plus `Main.dc.html`"*. Both were followed. `frontend/tools/mkharness.mjs` already produced the correct standalone page and was described as a screenshot rig, so nobody read it as the deploy step — it lifts `<helmet>` into a real `<head>`, `<x-dc>` into `<body>`, and supplies the component base class and props the canvas runtime would have. | high | done | Claude (Fenil) | Diagnosed against the live host: `curl` of `/` returns the artboard, and `/support.js` returns **HTTP 200 carrying HTML**. Fixed by publishing the port instead, and the deploy note rewritten to name it. Evidence: `tests/test_landing_downloads.py` +4, **mutation-checked** — making `mkharness` emit the artboard unchanged fails `test_the_published_page_stands_on_its_own`, and the old README sentence fails `test_the_deploy_note_names_the_page_that_works`. The built page is also asserted to keep every download link, so the port cannot silently drop the thing the page is for. **Verified visually rather than only structurally**: rendered headless through the repository's own Playwright rig at two scroll positions — the shader, the headline, the badges and *Skip to installer* all paint. That check existed (`frontend/tools/shot.mjs`) and had never been pointed at the question of whether the *published* artifact renders. |
| D053 | **The only Download button on the landing page was on screen for about two wheel notches.** The finale's `content` value — the name, the tagline and the Download button — was `ss(0.73, 0.78, p) * (1 - ss(0.86, 0.9, p))`: full opacity from p=0.78 to p=0.86, roughly **300px of a 3,800px runway**. It began fading while a reader was still reaching for it, which is a landing page failing at the one thing it exists for. **The comment directly above it claimed the hold was 0.70-0.86 and the code did not agree**, which is how the window got short without anyone noticing — the same defect class as a column written by one part of the system and read by none, in prose instead of SQL. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | **Measured, not estimated.** A Playwright rig samples the button's computed opacity across the runway: the hold was 302px and is now **842px, 2.8x longer** — `content` widened to `ss(0.71, 0.76) * (1 - ss(0.945, 0.975))` and the runway lengthened 520vh → 620vh. Every part of the reverse (`back`, `expand`, `pull`, and the two un-draw tails) moved back with it, because a hold the mark expands away through is not a hold. Rendered at both ends of the window to confirm the composition is identical rather than merely non-zero. Evidence: `tests/test_landing_downloads.py` +3, **mutation-checked** — restoring the old window fails 2, letting `expand` creep back under the hold fails 1, and drifting the comment from the code fails 1. The floor is set below the shipped value on purpose: it pins the property, not the design, so the finale can be retimed but not quietly halved. **Separately, and not a defect:** the page falls back to `tl-static` when WebGL2 is unavailable or `prefers-reduced-motion` is set, which collapses the runway from 4,680px to 1,384px and reads as *the animation is gone*. Confirmed by driving both paths headlessly. Worth knowing because it looks like breakage and is the accessibility fallback working. |
| D054 | **The landing page's animation ran slower on slower machines — not fewer frames of the same motion, but genuinely reduced motion.** The render loop advanced its clock with `this.t += 0.016 * speed`: sixty frames a second written as a constant. Any machine that could not sustain 60fps ran the animation clock slow in exact proportion, so the black hole really did spin slower and the page really did look more static. Reported as *"somehow the frontend degraded, it is not smooth, the motion is reduced and it appears static"* — three descriptions of one bug. The same fixed-per-frame assumption was in the pointer easing and the rebirth decay. **Invisible to whoever writes it**, because it only appears on hardware weaker than theirs — the same shape as `bootstrap.sh` installing four of nine packages and passing on the machine it was written on. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | **Measured before and after, same page, same viewport, in a browser.** At ~50fps the old clock advanced at **83% of real time**; the new one at **100%**. At 30fps the old one would have been 48%, at 20fps 32%. Fixed by advancing on real elapsed `dt`, clamped at 0.1s — generous on purpose, because the obvious 0.05 silently reimposes a 20fps ceiling on exactly the machines the fix is for, which was caught by measuring rather than by reading. Per-frame eases corrected with `1 - 0.94^(dt*60)`. **Also added: the shader now adapts its resolution to sustained frame time**, floor 0.4, climbing back when the machine can afford it — the honest lever for a ray-marched shader is pixels, not motion. The first version counted thirty consecutive slow frames, which at 2fps is fifteen seconds of stuttering before it reacts; counting frames to notice frames are slow is circular, so it is a timer now. Both faults found by instrumenting rather than by inspection. Evidence: `tests/test_landing_motion.py` — 6 tests, **mutation-checked**: restoring the 60fps constant fails 1, tightening the clamp fails 2, reverting to frame-counting fails 1, removing quality recovery fails 1, and disconnecting `renderGL` from the quality it computes fails 1. **`prefers-reduced-motion` and WebGL2 were ruled out first** — the reporter's console showed `static:false, reducedMotion:false, webgl2:true`, which is what redirected this from the accessibility fallback to the clock. |
| D055 | **The landing page called the product open source. It is not.** The hero carried an `Open Source` badge beside `Local First` and `Cross-Platform`. There is **no `LICENSE` file**, `gh repo view` reports `isPrivate: true`, and `README.md` never claims it — the badge existed only on the landing page, which is the one surface strangers actually read. This project's own named defect class in marketing clothes: a displayed claim nothing can support, exactly like the Contradictions meter in D011. **Also fixed in the same pass:** the beats were positioned at 62% with a headline tall enough to run up into the accretion disc, so the words were printed over the brightest part of the image and were hard to read. Lowered to 71% with a smaller headline, which puts both beats on sky. And the copy was rewritten to lead on what the product actually is — local-first — replacing metaphor with the README's own claims. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | Badge removed; the row is now two badges, both plainly true. Copy grounded line by line in `README.md`: *"The database, the queue and the sandbox all run here"* against *"The database, the job queue and the scientific sandbox all run locally"*, and *"Connect something and it says so first"* against *"where one can be connected, the interface says so before it is used"*. Guarded by `test_the_page_does_not_claim_to_be_open_source_while_it_is_not`, **mutation-checked** by putting the badge back. The test is deliberately conditional on a `LICENSE` existing rather than absolute — it refuses the claim arriving before the thing it describes, not the claim itself. Layout verified by rendering both beats headlessly at 1440x900. **Caveat worth stating: the hero now reads *"Your research never leaves this machine"* without qualification.** It is true by default and the very next beat names the exception, but a reader who does not scroll sees an absolute. Left as chosen rather than softened unilaterally, and recorded here so it is a decision rather than an oversight. |
| D056 | **Cloudflare answers Python's default User-Agent with 403, which made the whole product undeliverable — and only the live host could show it.** `curl` of a release URL returns 200; `urllib.request.urlopen` of the *same URL* returns **403 Forbidden**. Any named agent returns 200. Nothing local reproduces it, because nothing local sits behind a bot filter, so every test in this repository passed while the product could not be installed by anybody. **Four call sites, covering every path:** `install.sh` could not fetch `install.py`, so the advertised one-liner died at its last step; `install.py` could not read `latest.json` or the tarball, so nothing installed; `runtimes.download` fetches the release tarball during an update, so applying one would have failed; and worst, `updates.check_releases` could not reach the manifest, so **every installed copy would have reported *could not check* forever** — not an error anybody reports, just silence where updates should be. | high | done | Claude (Fenil) | **Found by running step 5 against the live host**, which is the only place it exists. Fixed by naming the product in the agent on all four callers — honestly, not by impersonating a browser, and there is a test refusing a `Mozilla/...` string. Verified against live Cloudflare, not a fixture: the fixed `install.py` fetched the manifest, downloaded the 19 MB tarball, verified the checksum and installed `b2dd184`; `updates.check_releases` verified the signature and answered *update available* to an older version and *up to date* to the current one. Evidence: `tests/test_release_requests.py` — 11 tests, **mutation-checked**: a bare `urlopen` fails 1, an impersonating agent fails 1, and an apostrophe in the embedded Python fails 2. **That last one is its own small lesson.** `install.sh` embeds Python inside a single-quoted shell string, so an apostrophe anywhere in it — including in the English comment *"Python's default agent"*, which is what happened — ends the string and breaks the file. Invisible on inspection, immediate when run. `sh -n` is now a test. |
| D057 | **Clicking Download printed the launcher into the browser instead of downloading it.** Cloudflare Pages serves `Throughline.bat` and `Throughline.command` with **no content type at all** and `throughline.sh` as `text/x-sh`, and no `Content-Disposition` on any of them — so the browser rendered each one as a page of text. Every download button on the site appeared to work and handed back a wall of script: a dead download wearing a working link, which is worse than a 404 because nothing looks wrong. **The page had been correct about this all along** — it links the launchers, the release publishes them, and `test_every_file_the_page_links_to_is_published` proved the file was there. What nothing checked was what the host *does* with a file once it is there, which is a different question from whether it exists. | high | done | Claude (Fenil) | Measured with `curl -I` against the live host: no `Content-Disposition` on any of the seven published files. Fixed with a Cloudflare `_headers` file giving the three launchers `Content-Disposition: attachment`, and **`_publish_launchers` now ships it with every release** rather than it being an upload step — a checklist step is one somebody eventually skips. **`install.sh` is deliberately exempt** and pinned to `text/plain` instead: the page offers it as *Read install.sh first — trust, then pipe*, which is the one honest thing a page suggesting `curl | sh` can do, and forcing it to save would break exactly that. Evidence: `tests/test_landing_downloads.py` +4, **mutation-checked**: dropping a launcher's attachment rule fails 1, forcing `install.sh` to download fails 1, and removing `_headers` from the publish list fails 1. That last mutation **passed on the first attempt** — `published_names()` was matching every quoted path in the function, including the table of reasons beside the list, so a file could be dropped from the publish tuple while its explanation kept the test green. It now parses only the tuple. |
| D058 | **A `.command` file can never be made Gatekeeper-clean, so the macOS download is a dead end by construction.** Apple DTS, on the developer forums: *"Notarization does not apply to shell scripts — it is technically not possible to notarize a shell script."* `codesign` can attach a signature to a script, but it lives in an extended attribute, is described as brittle, and earns no notarization ticket. **Stapling only works on `.app`, `.pkg` or `.dmg`.** So no amount of paying Apple fixes `Throughline.command` — the artifact itself has to change. **Why it blocks now:** since Catalina, Gatekeeper checks any *quarantined* executable, scripts included, when it is launched from Finder. A browser download sets `com.apple.quarantine`; double-clicking then triggers the check. The same script run from Terminal is not checked at all — which is exactly why the one-liner works. **macOS 15 made it worse**, from Apple's own release note: *"users will no longer be able to Control-click to override Gatekeeper... They will need to visit System Settings > Privacy & Security."* The page's old claim that macOS *"asks once"* was written for a bypass that no longer exists. **A `.dmg` does not help** — quarantine propagates to the contents, and the signature of what is executed is what gets checked. Confirmed, and it was worth confirming because `.dmg` is the intuitive answer. | high | open | — | **The half that needed no certificate is fixed.** The product was telling Mac users to right-click and choose Open, which is the override Apple removed — so a researcher on a current Mac followed the instruction, nothing happened, and the reasonable conclusion is that the download is broken. A stale instruction is worse than none: absent, they search; wrong, they trust it and stop. All three places that carried it now name System Settings > Privacy & Security > Open Anyway, and offer the Terminal one-liner, which never meets Gatekeeper because curl sets no quarantine attribute. Guarded by `test_the_mac_instruction_still_works`. **What remains open is unchanged and cannot be closed from here**: a `.command` cannot be notarised at all, and the fix is a signed `.pkg`, which needs an Apple Developer Program membership at $99/year. **The route that works today is the one-liner**, because `curl` does not set the quarantine attribute. That is corroborated by every security source but **never stated by Apple in writing**, so it is an implementation detail that could close in any release — it is a working route, not a durable one. **What proper macOS costs:** Apple Developer Program at **$99/year**, which an individual can join with no D-U-N-S number; a *Developer ID Installer* certificate; and a **`.pkg`** rather than a `.command` — the conventional artifact for a CLI/local-server tool, where `.app`-in-`.dmg` is the GUI convention. Then `pkgbuild --sign`, `xcrun notarytool submit --wait`, `xcrun stapler staple`. Automatable in CI with an App Store Connect API key. **Homebrew is not the free way round it any more:** Homebrew 5.0.0 deprecated unsigned casks and disables casks failing Gatekeeper in September 2026 — next month. A plain *formula* may still need only ad-hoc signing, which is the one avenue left worth checking before paying. **This is a product decision and not mine**: $99/year, or ship the one-liner as the macOS route and be honest on the page that the double-click download asks for a trip to System Settings. The page now says exactly that. **Update — a third option is confirmed and it is free: a Homebrew tap.** The September 2026 enforcement applies to **casks only**; Homebrew's own supply-chain doc says it *"applies macOS quarantine attributes to Cask downloads so Gatekeeper performs these checks"*, and there is no formula equivalent — quarantine handling lives in a `Cask::Quarantine` namespace with no counterpart. **A formula install is never quarantined, so nothing it puts on disk meets Gatekeeper at all.** No Developer ID, no notarisation, no fee. A tap needs no review and no notability — homebrew-core's *30 forks / 75 stars* bar is core's rule, not a tap's. The requirements are a **public** repository named `homebrew-throughline` holding `Formula/throughline.rb`, and nothing else; the recipe points at the tarball already published on the release host, so no source is exposed. A Python app installed into a virtualenv is the officially documented formula shape (`Language::Python::Virtualenv`), and a tap — unlike core — may fetch a prebuilt tarball rather than build from source. **`--no-quarantine` is not available to us and never was**: it was a cask-only flag typed by the *installing user*, not something a publisher sets, and it was deprecated in 5.0.0 and removed in 5.1.0 because Homebrew *"does not wish to easily provide circumvention to macOS security features"*. It is also moot, since formulae are not quarantined in the first place. `manage.py release` now generates `dist/throughline.rb` with this build's URL and digest, for the same reason `_headers` is published rather than remembered — a formula carrying last release's sha256 fails on the user's machine with a checksum mismatch. **Correction, same day: a tap is NOT recommended, and the case for it was overstated above.** Asked why a separate repository was needed, three things came back that change the answer. **(a) There is no way to avoid one.** `brew install <https URL to a .rb>` is hard-blocked — `Formulary::FromURILoader` sets `ALLOWED_URL_SCHEMES = %w[file]` and raises *"Non-checksummed download of formula file from an arbitrary URL is unsupported"*. And `Tap#install` runs a **full `git clone`** with no `--depth` and no sparse checkout, so a tap cannot be an existing large repo either; it must be a small dedicated one. **(b) Homebrew 6.0 (June 2026) added Tap Trust**: taps *"must be explicitly trusted before their code is evaluated or run"*, and auto-tapping of untrusted taps is gone. So the real user journey is `brew tap` → `brew trust` → `brew install` — **more ceremony than the one-liner, not less** — and Homebrew's own docs warn users against casually trusting third-party taps, which undercuts the familiarity argument that was the main reason to want it. **(c) It solves nothing about Gatekeeper.** The one-liner already installs on macOS with no prompt. What a tap actually adds is `brew upgrade` and `brew uninstall` semantics, against a recurring cost of a second repository and a `sha256` to keep in sync every release. **Better value for the same goal:** `manage.py` already has `update`, so self-update exists; it has **no `uninstall`**, and adding one would deliver the real half of what a tap offers to every platform at once, without Homebrew's trust machinery. Recorded rather than done. The formula generator is kept because it is written, tested and costs nothing — it makes a tap a short job if anybody ever asks for `brew install`. **Nobody has, so the repository is not being created.** **Rechecked 2026-09-06 in a ledger reconciliation pass.** **Half of this row is closed and half is not, so read it as two claims.** Closed: the stale right-click instruction is gone everywhere, replaced by *System Settings > Privacy & Security > Open Anyway*, and `tests/test_the_mac_instruction_still_works.py` holds it — 5 tests, all passing. Still true: a `.command` file cannot be notarized or stapled, and the repository contains no `pkgbuild`, `codesign`, `notarytool` or `stapler` step, no `.pkg` or `.dmg`, and no Developer ID certificate. The Homebrew formula generator in `scripts/release.py` exists and is unused, as the row says. |
| D061 | **The macOS download button was removed rather than explained.** It was the only route on the page whose first impression was a *"could not verify... free of malware"* dialog, and unlike every other fault fixed around it, that one cannot be repaired: a shell script can never be notarised (D058), so it was a permanent state and not a temporary one. Four steps to System Settings were written for it first; deleting the button was better than documenting the wall. **macOS is not left without a route** — the one-liner installs with no warning at all, because `curl` fetches it rather than the browser and nothing is quarantined. **Confirmed that the same line also opens it**: on a second run `install.sh` prints *already installed*, downloads nothing and hands straight to `manage.py start`, which is the launchers' own contract from T081. The PowerShell half behaves identically. So macOS gets one line that installs the first time and launches every time after, which is what the double-click was for. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | `Throughline.command` is still published for anyone who wants it — it is simply not what a stranger is pointed at. The four Gatekeeper steps came back out again in the same pass, since nothing on the page now leads there, leaving the menu shorter than before any of this started. Evidence: `test_every_platform_has_a_route_on_the_page` replaces the old *one door per platform* assertion — it now requires a **route** rather than a file, asserts macOS is still named and the one-liner is still present, and **fails if the `.command` link returns**. Re-running the one-liner over an existing install was driven against the live host rather than reasoned about. |
| D062 | **The download menu was carrying text that belonged somewhere else.** Three rows and a three-line footnote, where the page's job is to get somebody installed. Cut in one pass: the *Read install.sh first* row went (**the URL is already in the copyable command below it**, and `_headers` pins the script to `text/plain`, so the affordance survives without a row restating a link the reader already has); the **macOS row** went with it, since macOS has no download and is named in the one-liner's own label; and the footnote lost everything the read me now carries. The menu is three rows shorter than it was this morning and says more. | low | done | Claude (Fenil) | **`README.txt` is the overflow** — published with every release, linked from the page as a small *read me*, and served as `text/plain` so a click reads it rather than saving it. It carries what the menu cannot: both one-liners, what the first run actually does and how long it takes, where the install and the database live, how updating verifies a signature, and `manage.py doctor`. **A terms-of-use draft was written first and then withdrawn** — asked for terms, what was actually wanted was instructions. Worth recording rather than quietly deleting: there is still **no LICENSE and no terms** in this repository, so the legal position of the software is unstated, and a line saying *by installing you accept our terms* would have pointed at a document that did not exist — the Open Source badge from D055 wearing a suit. If terms are ever wanted, they need a real jurisdiction and a real entity, neither of which can be invented. Evidence: `tests/test_landing_downloads.py` — `test_install_sh_can_still_be_read_before_it_is_piped` now asserts the *capability* rather than the row (the URL is on the page and the host serves it as text), `test_the_page_links_a_read_me_that_the_release_publishes`, `test_the_read_me_is_shown_rather_than_downloaded`, and `test_the_read_me_covers_both_one_liners`. |
| D063 | **A release install could not run at all on any machine that had Node — it started the stack and then shut itself down.** `manage.py start` asked `if node:` and ran `npm run dev` inside `apps/web`. A release ships the **exported** interface and no npm project, so npm exited immediately with `ENOENT ... apps/web/package.json`; the supervisor exits as soon as any child does — deliberately, because a dead worker beside a live API looks like a working stack that never finishes anything — so it took down the API and worker that had just started correctly. **The branch asked the wrong question.** Whether Node is installed on the machine says nothing about whether *this installation* has a project to build; most machines carry Node for some unrelated reason. The design was already right and already documented — `_ensure_interface` says an already-built interface needs no Node at all, *"which is the case a release should arrive in"* — and only this one branch disagreed with it. **Invisible everywhere it was tested.** On a source checkout `package.json` exists and the dev server is correct, so the defect lives only on the installation nobody develops on. Every earlier verification (Linux, Windows, macOS) stopped at *installed*, because the next step takes minutes — so three platforms confirmed the install and none confirmed the run. | high | done | Claude (Fenil) | **Found by the first person to let a release run to the end**, on macOS. Then **reproduced on Linux against the shipped `367bc72`** — identical `ENOENT` and teardown — and fixed on that same install, before and after in one sitting: after the change it prints *Served by the API itself — no Node process is involved*, `/` and `/docs` both return **200**, the page served is the real Next export, there are **zero** npm errors, and it is still running twenty seconds later. Fixed by branching on `_can_run_dev_server()` — the presence of `apps/web/package.json` — rather than on Node. Evidence: `tests/test_release_runs.py` — 4 tests, **mutation-checked** by restoring `if node:`. **Second defect, fixed as a consequence:** the *Hand tracking model not installed* hint told a release install to run `npm --prefix apps/web run vendor:hand-model`, which cannot work there for the same missing-`package.json` reason. It now prints only on a checkout. **That note was wrong and is corrected in D069:** hand tracking is *not* unavailable on a release install. It works, and the code said otherwise. |
| D064 | **A broken release cannot heal itself, and the read me had the data directory wrong.** Two things, both surfaced by the same attempt to recover from D063. **(a) The one-liner does not update.** Re-running `curl ... | sh` over an existing install prints *already installed*, downloads nothing and runs the copy that is there — correct behaviour for a launcher (T081: install or open, never update on its own), but it means somebody holding a broken build re-runs the advertised line and gets the identical failure, which reads as *the fix did not work*. Worse, D063's failure kills the interface, so **the update button in the UI is unreachable on exactly the install that needs it**. The only way out is `manage.py update` from a terminal, or deleting the program directory. **Now fixed.** Both installers compare the installed `VERSION` against the live manifest, say what they found, and hand over to `manage.py update` — T073's job, which knows how to back up, verify the signature and roll back. The installer only *notices*; it does not do the swap itself. A failed update is **not fatal**: the copy already present starts anyway with the reinstall command printed, because refusing to run on account of a newer version existing would be a worse failure than the one being fixed. Driven against the live manifest in both directions, update succeeding and update failing. **(b) `README.txt` said the database lives inside the program directory. It does not** — `db.py` resolves the data root to `~/.throughline-os` (note the dot) from `THROUGHLINE_HOME`, while the program is `~/throughline-os`. Getting that backwards tells a researcher either that reinstalling destroys their work, or that backing up the program directory preserves it. Both are false and only one of them is discovered safely. Written by me the same day, in the file published as the read me. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | The read me now names both directories, points out the dot, and says plainly that removing the program directory is safe. Guarded by `test_the_read_me_names_the_data_directory_correctly`, which asserts the claim **against `db.py` itself** rather than against a second copy of the path written into the test — so if the data root ever moves, the read me is what fails. |
| D065 | **When the stack died on startup it said nothing, so the person it happened to had no way in.** The supervisor exits as soon as any child does — correct, because a dead worker beside a live API looks like a working stack that never finishes anything — but it returned in **silence**. A child that failed at startup produced a wall of its own error output and then the stack tearing itself down with no explanation. In D063 that was raw `npm error ENOENT` lines followed by nothing: something started, something stopped, and there was nothing to act on. **A supervisor that knows why it is exiting and does not say so is throwing away the one piece of information nobody else has.** | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | Each child is now labelled — *the API*, *the background worker*, *the web dev server* — and its death reported by name and status. A death **within twenty seconds** is treated as a startup failure rather than a crash under load and says so: that is almost always the environment rather than the code. It prints both `manage.py doctor` and `manage.py update` **in full, with absolute paths**, because by definition the reader is on an installation that does not work and cannot be assumed to know either command. Evidence: `tests/test_release_runs.py` +4, asserting the labels exist, that a startup death is still distinguished from a later one, and that both commands are named. Recorded beside D064's installer fix because they are the same problem from two ends — one lets a stranger get current, the other tells them why they need to. |
| D066 | **A busy port told a stranger to run a file that was not there, under a name they had never heard.** With port 8080 held by an earlier run that had not shut down, `start` refused — correctly, before touching a database or spawning a child — and then advised: *`PORT=8081 WEB_PORT=3001 ./scripts/dev.sh`*. **Three things wrong with that one line.** It is a **relative path**, and somebody who installed with the one-liner is standing in their home directory, where `./scripts/` does not exist. It names **`dev.sh`**, a developer script nobody who used the advertised install has ever seen. And it never says **how to stop the process it just named** — even though `port_owner` had the pid in its hand and threw it away, having gone to the trouble of looking it up precisely so the message would be actionable. The same defect as the hand-tracking hint in D063: advice a release install cannot follow, which reads as the product being broken rather than the port being busy. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | `port_owner` now returns the pid alongside the description, and both `start` and `doctor` use it. A blocked port prints *Most often that is an earlier Throughline that did not shut down*, then `kill <pid>`, then a **fully absolute** `manage.py start --api-port N --web-port N` — runnable from anywhere, naming the same entry point the person already used. Evidence: `tests/test_startup_checks.py`, whose assertions were themselves pinning the bad advice — they asserted `WEB_PORT` and `PORT=` appeared, so the unfollowable form was *held in place by a test*. They now require the pid, an absolute `manage.py start`, and that `./scripts/dev.sh` is **absent**. Verified by driving `dev` against a genuinely held port and reading what it printed. |
| D067 | **An install cut halfway through left a tree that every later branch trusted.** A download interrupted after `scripts/manage.py` lands, but before the rest does, is taken by the *already installed* branch — so `update` runs out of a partial tree and `start` imports from one, and both fail in ways that read as the product being broken rather than the install having been stopped. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | Both installers now judge completeness **positively**, against what the release builder actually writes: a `VERSION` file, the `packages/` tree bootstrap installs from, and the exported `apps/web/out/index.html`. Anything short of that is treated as unfinished and the install is redone. **Moved, not deleted — and that was the whole design question.** The suggestion was an automatic `rm -rf`. That is not something an installer may do: `$DEST` comes from `THROUGHLINE_INSTALL_DIR`, so one bad value removes a directory nobody asked it to touch, and the failure is unrecoverable at exactly the moment somebody is already having a bad time. Renaming to `<dest>.broken-<timestamp>` reaches the same outcome, costs a folder nobody has to keep, and can be undone by somebody who disagrees with the diagnosis. A second guard refuses to move `$HOME`, `/` or an empty path at all, so it only ever touches something it has identified. Driven against the live host: a tree with `manage.py` and nothing else was detected, moved to `/tmp/tl-half.broken-20260826-174128`, reinstalled clean to `0bd64d6`, **and the moved copy was still there afterwards**. The guard was driven too, with `THROUGHLINE_INSTALL_DIR=$HOME`, and refused by name. Evidence: `tests/test_release_runs.py` +3, including one that fails if `rm -rf "$DEST"` ever appears in `install.sh`. |
| D068 | **Windows double-click was the one door that skipped the installer, so it missed both of the day's recovery fixes.** Asked whether the work covered all three platforms, and the honest answer was no. `throughline.sh` and `Throughline.command` hand to `install.sh`; both one-liners are installers themselves; **`Throughline.bat` fetched `install.py` directly**. That worked — but `install.py` only installs. The *installer* is where *already here, so update it* (D064) and *half-installed, so redo it* (D067) live, so the Windows launcher reached neither. Two lists in two languages, in a third language. The audit that found it took one command and should have been run before claiming the fixes were platform-wide. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | `Throughline.bat` now hands to `install.ps1` — PowerShell rather than more batch, because it is always present and `irm | iex` runs from a string, so no execution-policy prompt applies. `install.ps1` gained `THROUGHLINE_NO_START`, set only by the launcher, so the installer can install without starting and the launcher keeps ownership of the window the app runs in. **Driven on real Windows**, where it installed `0bd64d6`, detected Python 3.14, fetched the pinned 3.12 and bootstrapped. That run also caught a defect introduced in the same change: a `|| echo "the release server could not be reached"` fires on **any** non-zero exit from PowerShell, so a port already in use during startup was reported as a network failure — sending somebody to check their connection over a busy port. Removed; the existing *is it installed now* check answers the only question the file can actually answer. Naming a cause you have not established is worse than naming none. Evidence: `tests/test_launchers.py` +2 — one asserting **every** door installs through an installer, mapping each launcher to the one it uses, and one refusing the invented diagnosis. `Throughline.bat` also left `test_release_requests.py`'s caller list, since it no longer makes HTTP requests; a new test pins that it delegates and fails if it starts fetching again, so the removal is a file leaving the list by not making requests rather than by going unchecked. |
| D069 | **Hand tracking was reported missing on every release install while it was working.** The release ships the model at `apps/web/out/mediapipe/hand_landmarker.task` — inside the exported interface, which is exactly what the API serves — and both checks looked only in `apps/web/public/mediapipe/`, the *source* location, which is not in the archive at all. So `doctor` said *not installed*, startup said *not installed*, and the browser could load it perfectly well the whole time. **A false negative rather than a false positive**, which is the rarer direction and no better: it is still a claim that does not match what the machine can do, and it sends somebody to fix something that is not broken. **I recorded the opposite of the truth in D063** — that hand tracking was unavailable on a release and nothing said so — having reasoned about it instead of looking in the tarball. Corrected there. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | Both checks now look in `out/` as well as `public/`. Confirmed by listing the published archive: the model and its four WASM files are all under `out/mediapipe/`. **And the advice was wrong even when the model really is missing:** it said `npm --prefix apps/web run vendor:hand-model`, which needs an npm project a release does not have — the same unfollowable-advice defect as D063's hint and D066's port message. A checkout is now told the vendor command; a release is told to reinstall, because a release should have shipped it. Evidence: `tests/test_startup_checks.py` — one test asserting the model is found where a release keeps it, and one asserting each kind of installation is given advice it can actually carry out. |
| D070 | **Thirty-seven server routes cannot be reached from the interface, and the suite was green on every one of them.** This project's named recurring defect — finished, tested, unreachable — measured for the first time rather than noticed one instance at a time: of 115 `/api` routes, 80 are called by `apps/web` and 35 are called by nothing a person can operate. Whole subsystems are inside that number. **The visuals subsystem has four routes and no page at all.** Workflow nodes wait for an approval nothing can give, which is the step this product is built around. Graph analytics have five routes and no interface. The board can add a card and bring one to the front but has no way to delete one, and `DELETE .../board/{id}` is the half nobody calls. **A backend test is not a client, and that is why this stayed invisible**: `test_every_route_answers.py` proves a route does not fall over when called, which says nothing about whether anyone can call it — so the more thoroughly an unreachable route is tested, the healthier it looks. **— the number is now 17, and three of the four examples above are out of date.** Checked while looking for something to wire: the ledger in `tests/test_routes_are_reachable.py` holds 17 entries, not 37. The board gained its delete, the visuals subsystem gained `savedfigures.tsx` and is down to one unreached route rather than four with no page at all, and workflow approvals are reachable — `approvals.tsx` calls them and handles the 409. Graph analytics, five routes and no interface, is the one example that still holds. **Recorded rather than quietly edited**, because a row that overstated the current state by more than twice would send somebody reading this file to work that is already done, and over-reporting a known defect is the same failure as under-reporting one. The prose number is a snapshot and will decay again; the ledger is the live count, and the guard checks it in both directions so the ledger itself cannot. | high | recorded | Claude (Sarthak) | `tests/test_routes_are_reachable.py`, 48 tests. Routes are discovered from the app and callers scanned from interface *sources only*; every one of the 37 now carries a written reason, so a new route with no client and no reason fails. The ledger is checked in both directions — an entry for a route that has since gained a client fails too, which is the direction these lists normally rot in. **Mutation-checked, 9 of 9 killed, and the first pass killed only 4**: the orphan, stale and deleted checks could each be deleted outright with the suite still green, because against a healthy system all three find nothing. Fixed by making the comparison a pure function and testing it against a fabricated broken system. The rule that every entry gives a reason had the same defect and the same fix. |
| D071 | **LAW 4 was recorded as enforced while being enforceable by nothing.** The approval gate — "an irreversible step is released by a visible human decision" — has been in the engine since the first migration, and no part of the system could produce one or give one. Nothing outside `workflow.py` ever called `start_node`, no `enqueue()` call in the codebase passed `nodes=`, so **no run has ever had a single node**, and the approve route needed a run id that nothing handed out. `docs/PHASE_0.md` named the law satisfied by `start_node` throughout. The same absence made two further claims false: `workflow.py`'s "a restart resumes after the last completed node" and `runner.py`'s "no work is abandoned mid-node" described machinery no run used. **And `approve_node` was unguarded** — it approved any node of the run by name, so approving a step that had already completed reset it to `queued` and un-finished work that was done, while approving a name no node had matched nothing and answered as though it had. Both were unreachable only because nothing could call it. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by D070's reachability guard, which is what it was built for. `workflow.gate()` stops a run and **requires a description of what it is asking to release** — an approval screen saying "approve step 3" is a rubber stamp, so the sentence is the control and a gate without one is refused. `workflow.once()` makes a gate affordable: a resumed handler re-enters from the top, so without it every sandboxed analysis before the gate is paid for twice. The runner catches `AwaitingApproval` **inside** the transaction — letting it escape rolls back both the completed steps and the record that the run is waiting, so it would retry forever and never appear on the screen. `GET /api/projects/{id}/workflows/awaiting-approval` is new; without it an approval could only be given by someone who already knew the run id. Applied to discovery, where Rule 10 ("AI does not secretly mutate important research state") bites: the sweep tests every pair, then waits before recording connections and promoting survivors. **Asked for per run, not imposed** — gating every sweep would stop the seeded worked example on a fresh install, and whether an unattended sweep may record is the operator's decision. +34 tests (24 backend, 11 web, one for the inline-subroutine call the worked example alone caught). Mutation-checked 24 of 24 after two survivors were fixed: `pytest.raises(WorkflowError)` passed either way because `AwaitingApproval` subclasses it, and a `Math.max(0, …)` guarding clock skew was dead code, since every negative age is already below the first threshold. |
| D072 | **The Figures screen exported a figure by cloning the live `<svg>` out of the page, and the entire server-side visuals subsystem had no caller at all.** Four routes, a critic, a publication renderer and a lineage edge — none of it reachable. A file came out either way, which is why nobody noticed what the DOM route skips. **LAW 5 was the casualty**: `create_visual` writes a `VISUALIZES` edge from the analysis run to the figure, which is what makes a figure on a slide resolve back to the computation and the dataset underneath — a file assembled in the browser is related to nothing. **The critic was skipped too**: a figure with an unfixed blocking problem must not be published, and cloning the DOM asks nobody, so a truncated axis or a caption claiming causation left through the side door. Also lost: PDF, EPS and TIFF at a stated pixel height, which is what journals ask for, and a filename carrying the figure id rather than `consumption-resistance.svg`, which nobody can trace back a month later. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by D070's reachability guard. The on-screen chart stays as it is — it is good for reading — and *export* now goes through the server: `components/publish.tsx` creates the figure, shows what the critic said, refuses to offer a download for a figure the critic blocked (the server returns 409, so the button would only ever fail), and asks for the format warning **before** fetching the file rather than with it. A pixel height is offered only where one means something; the server refuses one for a vector rather than ignoring it. +17 tests: 12 web, and 5 over HTTP because **the domain was never the problem** — `test_visuals.py` covered the critic and the renderers thoroughly while the four routes themselves had only 404 and 401 cases, so the seam the interface uses was the one nothing exercised. The new ones assert the PDF really starts `%PDF`, the PNG carries its magic bytes and `-600px` in the filename, and the `visualizes` edge exists in `artifact_lineage_edges`. Mutation-checked, 7 of 7 killed. |
| D073 | **The reachability guard was itself blind to nineteen routes, and so was the guard that checks every route answers.** `app.include_router()` does not flatten: this FastAPI version stores the included router as a single `_IncludedRouter` in `app.routes`, with the real routes behind its `original_router`. That object has no `.path`, so both guards — which discover routes by walking `app.routes` and reading `.path` — skipped every route in `interpretation.py`: contradictions, preregistration deviations, the exploration ledger, harvest, exports, library notes, challenges and analysis lineage. **D070's count of 115 routes was wrong; it is 134.** The consequences ran both ways: the audit reported thirteen paths the interface calls as calling nothing, exempted an entire feature area, and `test_every_route_answers.py` never asked those nineteen whether they answer. **Its own guard against silent emptiness passed the whole time** — it asserts the route count is *large*, and sixty is large whether or not nineteen are missing. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found while picking the next thing to build: the interface appeared to call `/projects/{id}/analyses/{id}/lineage`, which no route seemed to serve. It does serve it — 401, not 404. Discovery moved to `tests/apiroutes.py`, which descends into included routers, and both guards use it. | D074 | **Nothing could register a hypothesis, so every result in the system was exploratory by construction.** `POST /projects/{id}/preregistrations` had no caller anywhere, while the interface *did* read `/deviations` and `/deviations/narrative` — a screen reporting departures from registrations that could never be created, whose empty state read "Nothing registered in this project" and was permanent. This is not a cosmetic gap: a pre-registration is what earns a result its exemption from multiple-comparison correction, and the exploration ledger corrects every look at the data together precisely because the one legitimate exemption is a hypothesis registered before anybody looked. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by D073's corrected audit. `components/preregister.tsx`, on the deviations screen and in its empty state. **The predicted direction is required by the form because it is required by the domain** — a prediction with no direction cannot be wrong, and exempting a description would turn the mechanism into a way of laundering exploratory work. The form distinguishes an *unstated* plan from an empty one, because the domain does: `covariates: null` cannot be deviated from, `[]` can. It warns while the form is open that a registration with no method, design or covariates cannot be checked against the analysis that runs — "not comparable" is not "matched" — and it reports the server's own note about what the registration bought rather than restating it. +11 web tests, 8 of 8 mutations killed. |
| D075 | **A sensitivity analysis could be displayed but not performed.** `components/forklineage.tsx` showed where a run came from and what was tried from it, opening with the line that a sensitivity analysis is the relationship between runs rather than any one of them — and that relationship had no screen. `POST /analyses/{id}/fork` had no caller, so the history could not be added to; `GET /projects/{id}/analyses/compare` had no caller, so branches could never be put side by side. **`compare_runs` already answers the only question a set of branches raises** — *"branches disagree on significance, the conclusion depends on an analytical choice and should be reported as such"* — and no researcher could reach that sentence. The panel also returned `null` for a run with no branches, which is right for a description and wrong for an action: it hid the button that creates the first branch exactly where a researcher needs it first. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `components/sensitivity.tsx`. **A fork with nothing changed is a re-run, not a sensitivity test** — it gives an identical result and a comparison of two identical runs reports a stable conclusion, which is flattery rather than evidence, so the form says so. The change offered is the rank-based counterpart (pearson↔spearman, t_test↔mann_whitney, anova↔kruskal_wallis): a fork copies the parent's variables, so only a method taking the same roles can replace another, and role compatibility is necessary but not sufficient — `chi_square` also takes an x and a y and is a test for categorical data, so it is not offered. +21 tests. The HTTP seam had no coverage at all: the domain test built a fork by calling `create_spec` and `create_run` directly, which is how the domain was proved and is not how anybody uses it. **Two things were learned by trying**: fork answers 202, not 200; and a completed run is immutable at the database level — a trigger refuses the update with *"fork it instead of editing it"* — so the disagreement case could not be fabricated. It is now produced by data instead: one outlier gives Pearson p≈8e-15 and Spearman p≈0.55, which is a better test than the one that was refused. Mutation-checked, 10 of 10 killed. |
| D076 | **Nothing could move a finding along its lifecycle, and nothing could challenge one.** `POST /findings/{id}/transition` had no caller, so every finding stayed at whatever state it was created in for ever — while the findings screen opened by saying "a finding must link to supporting and contradicting evidence before it can be promoted past candidate", describing a rule nothing could exercise, and `docs/PHASE_0.md` recorded LAW 3 as enforced by `findings.transition`. It was, and nobody could reach it. **This is LAW 4 again one law along.** `POST /findings/{id}/challenge` had no caller either, so the Challenges panel's empty state — *"No critic has run against it yet. That is not the same as it having survived one."* — was permanently true and unchangeable: the screen displayed an adversarial process nobody could start. `GET /findings/{id}` was unreached too, which is why neither the evidence counts nor the lifecycle history were on screen. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `components/lifecycle.tsx`. **The three states of a robustness check are the design.** The domain distinguishes a check that was never run from one that ran and failed, and refuses validation differently for each — *"cannot validate without these checks"* against *"these checks did not pass"*. A checkbox has two states and would collapse that, defaulting to passed or reporting not-run as failed, so each check is asked as three options and unanswered ones are not sent at all. The screen offers only legal transitions rather than six buttons of which four return 422, which means it holds a copy of `FINDING_PROMOTION` — `tests/test_findings_lifecycle_ui.py` parses the TypeScript and fails when the copies diverge, since that drift is silent in both directions. **Unlike the last three, the backend seam here was already well covered** (`test_api.py`, `test_mvp_end_to_end.py`), so nothing was duplicated. +18 web tests, +9 backend, 9 of 9 mutations killed. The drift guard's own vacuity test earned its place immediately: the parser read `string[]` out of `Record<string, string[]>` and returned an empty map, which is a subset of everything and would have passed every comparison. |
| D077 | **Variable harmonisation was a chain dead at both ends, so every chart in this system is titled `resistance_pct`.** Six routes; the interface called one. It read `/projects/{id}/variables` — approved labels only — and nothing could propose a label or approve one, so the approved set was empty in every project and stayed that way. The figures screen asks for those labels to title its axes (`variables.data?.labels ?? {}`) and always got none. The vocabulary half was worse: a whole subsystem for what a project learns to call things, with a pending queue nothing could add to or decide. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `components/variables.tsx`, a new Variables section beside Sources. Two queues because they are two decisions: a **label** says what a column of this dataset means, an **alias** says a phrase refers to a variable this project already knows — and approving an alias silently resolves that term in everything afterwards, so the panel reports how often it has been used, letting "it improves as you use it" be checked rather than believed. The two decide routes have different shapes (`{approve: bool}` against `{status: str}`) and the tests hold them apart. Review order is the server's — least confident first, which is where a person is actually needed — and a test fails if the screen resorts it. Proposing needs a model and legitimately 503s without one; the server's reason is shown, because "no model provider is configured" is actionable and "could not propose labels" is not. +15 web tests, 10 of 10 mutations killed — **after five of them silently failed to apply**: the shell mangled escaped quotes in the patterns, the substitution never happened, and the green results meant nothing until they were re-run from a file. |
| D078 | **Closed. The reachability audit compared paths, not methods, and eleven routes were hiding behind that.** Callers were found by scanning the interface for `/api/...` strings, and a string carries no verb — so a path whose GET was called and whose POST was not counted as fully reached. The audit now compares `"METHOD /api/path"`. **Most of what it found is one shape: the writing half of a screen that can only read.** `librarynote.tsx` displays a library note and posts nothing, so a note cannot be written; the consistency screen reads the latest sweep and nothing starts one; the board can add a card and not delete one. The mirror image exists too — the compare screen posts a pair and shows the answer while nothing reads a stored assessment back, and the same for locating claims and extracting from a source. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Verbs are read from the call itself (`useApi`, `api.<verb>`, `fetch` with its `method` option), which attributes all but three real call sites. **The three matter, and the design turns on them**: `views.tsx` builds its search path in a `const` with a ternary and passes the variable, so no verb can be read there — guessing one would invent an orphan out of a scan that could not read the call. Those paths are `unresolved` and treated as reached by any method, which is exactly what this file did for every path before, so the change is strictly more detection and no new false positives. Bases built as `const base = \`/api/…\`` are expanded, which is what found the **first wrong ledger entry**: it claimed no journal view existed while `NodeJournal.tsx` both reads and writes the per-object journal through a computed base. Ledger rewritten to 31 method-qualified entries. Mutation-checked, 8 of 8 killed, including the two that matter most — collapsing every route to GET, and collapsing every call to GET. |
| D079 | **Three charts had hover wired to nothing, and the isosurface ignored half the command architecture.** `SetRegions` mounted a tooltip with `const hit = null` above it and `rows={[]}` inside: hovering dimmed the other combinations and reported nothing, on a chart whose entire content is how many items fall in each combination. **The highlight worked, which is what made it look finished**, and the unused `readable` import in both it and `Ribbon` was the evidence of what had been intended. `Ribbon` reported `id: cohort_a` — a raw identifier its own file calls the one thing a label must never be. `Isosurface3D` had `selectRegion` and `withinPolygon` returning `[]` whatever they were given, `focus` and `deselect` as empty functions, and a `select` that returned a target and recorded nothing — so a hand that could rotate it could pick nothing on it while every other chart in the same gallery answered. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Tooltips now report the intersection and its count, saying *none in common* for the empty combinations that are enumerated on purpose, since a bare `0` reads as a missing value rather than as the finding it is; `Ribbon` reports throughput, taking the larger of in and out so a middle node does not double itself. The isosurface selects, and a region keeps facets on the far side while a point does not — a click names one visible place, a lasso encloses an area. **The first fix was the same defect one layer down**: it added a `selected` state, wired every command to it, and painted nothing, so the command stopped being ignored and started being answered invisibly. Caught by mutation testing, not by review. +18 tests where there had been none. |
| D080 | **`insidePolygon` had six copies.** Every 3D chart carried its own point-in-polygon test — five byte-identical, the sixth the same formula rearranged. It also had no test anywhere. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | One copy in `lib/charts/scene3d.ts`, used by all seven, with the first tests it has ever had — concave lassos, self-crossing ones, degenerate ones, and a point on a seam shared by two lassos belonging to exactly one of them. Lint is clean for the first time: the unused imports were the symptom, and the last warning was a stale dependency array in the isosurface's handle that would have had a lasso testing against the shell from before the level moved. |

So the plain-language reading beside every result — the whole answer to "a researcher who does not know statistics can still use this platform" — has never worked. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by asking which domain modules nothing imports, then noticing the web called a path the route table did not contain. **Every reachability check in this repository asked whether a route has a caller; none asked whether a caller has a route**, which is why this survived four rounds of exactly that audit. `test_the_interface_calls_nothing_the_server_does_not_serve` now closes that direction, and fails if the route is removed again. The route answers 409 rather than 404 for an unfinished run — the run exists and has no result yet, which is a different thing from one nobody has heard of — and 502 when the model writes a figure into prose that must not carry one. **That last check is the reason the summary is safe to show, and nothing had ever exercised it**: a paraphrase restating a number can round it or drift from the value printed beside it. +12 backend tests, 4 of 4 mutations killed, verified against the running server: 401 JSON where it was 503 HTML. |

Two routes had been waiting for that view with no caller: `/objects/{id}/impact` and `/objects/{id}/mentions`. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | A panel beside the board — beside rather than over, because covering the arrangement hides the reason somebody is on this screen. Findings resting on it as evidence are counted separately from artifacts derived from it — an artifact can be remade, and a finding that loses its evidence is a claim with nothing left under it. +18 tests, 7 of 7 mutations killed.
