It matters on **undo** — putting the card back would otherwise resurrect a panel the researcher had already dismissed by taking the card off. | low | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by mutation testing, which is the only thing that would have found it: the line reads as obviously necessary. Kept, with the undo path tested, rather than deleted as dead or left as an untested guess. |
| D089 | **Twelve routes never checked who owned the project, and one account could read another's research.** Confirmed against a running app with a second signed-in account, not inferred: `GET /projects/{id}/board`, `/board/available`, `/artifacts`, `/citations/verify` and `/excerpts` all answered **200** with the other account's contents — the whole board, every unplaced object, every report, every excerpt taken from a paper. `POST /artifacts/{id}/presentation` answered **201**: somebody else's report re-cut as a talk, with the slides created inside their project. **The domain looked like it was guarding that one.** `draft_presentation_from_report` refuses a report belonging to a different project — and the route handed it the report's *own* project_id, so the comparison was against itself and could never fail. A check that cannot fail reads exactly like a check. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found while evaluating the presentation route as a feature to build: every neighbouring handler called `scoped_project` and that one did not. A static scan for handlers that take a `project_id` and never scope it returned sixteen; **testing them from a second account is what separated the ten real ones from the six that were fine**, and it also showed which of the failures were leaks (200) and which were only invalid ids (400/422) — those last had to be re-probed with bodies that validate, because a 422 means the ownership check never ran. `tests/test_project_isolation.py` asks every project-taking route the same question and reads the answer as a status code, because a route can be scoped by a dependency, a helper or the domain and only the answer settles it. Removing the twelve checks fails nine of its cases. |
| D090 | **A test that expected the weaker refusal.** `test_a_connection_from_another_project_is_refused` posted to a project that does not exist and asserted 400, which came from the domain noticing the connection belonged elsewhere. With the ownership check in front it is 404 — and its own comment argued for that: *"an identifier is not an authorisation"*. | low | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Updated to 404, and split: the case it used to cover — a connection from another project the caller **does** own, where ownership is not the question — is now its own test, so the domain's refusal stays covered rather than being replaced by the earlier one. |
| D091 | **Five more routes leaked, keyed by an artifact, a source or an alias rather than a project.** The natural completion of D089, which only covered handlers taking a `project_id`: these carry an id that names a *row*, so the check has to look up which project the row belongs to before it can ask anything, and none of them did. Confirmed from a second account: `GET /artifacts/{id}` **200** — the whole report; `POST /artifacts/{id}/render` **200** — somebody else's report written to a file; `POST /artifacts/{id}/check-citations` **200**; `GET /sources/{id}/marks` **200** — everything drawn on their paper; `POST /vocabulary/{id}/decide` **200** — a term approved into their vocabulary, which from then on silently resolves in every paper of that project. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | One helper, `_owning_project`, since all five need the same lookup and answering 404 for a row you do not own is the same rule as `scoped_project`. Each leak proved before the fix and re-proved after by removing the check. |
| D092 | **A guard so this class cannot come back, and the reason it cannot be a scan alone.** `tests/test_routes_are_scoped.py` requires every route to establish ownership by one of the known means or to be written down with what it reaches instead — 32 entries, all of them auth, health, system settings or external search. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | **The entry that makes the point is `DELETE /api/projects/{id}`**, the most destructive route in the product. It calls none of the scoping helpers and is correct: it filters on `owner_user_id` in its own query and finds nothing for anybody else. A scan alone would have called it a hole; the behavioural test in `test_project_isolation.py` proves it is not. That is why the two files exist together — one asks what the code says, the other asks what the server answers, and only the second settles it. `POST /speech/transcribe` is recorded as the one route with no session at all, a documented choice made on the grounds that the API binds to localhost, flagged for re-reading if that ever changes — the product does support more than one account. Mutation-checked, 4 of 4 killed after two survived: the unexplained and stale checks passed because both lists are empty against a healthy API, the same hole the reachability guard had, fixed the same way. |
| D093 | **A finished report could not become slides.** `POST /artifacts/{id}/presentation` had no caller, so a researcher with a report had no way to get a talk out of it except by writing one somewhere else — which is the thing the route exists to prevent. §75: a presentation is the same evidence at a different length, derived from the report so both reference the same analysis runs; re-running an analysis then moves both, rather than leaving two accounts of one result that drift apart. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | On the report screen, and **it opens the talk rather than only making it** — creating a second document and leaving the reader on the first is how a button comes to look broken: the work happened and nothing on screen changed. `onOpenArtifact` is required rather than optional, because an optional callback nobody supplies is the dead surface this codebase keeps producing. Not offered on a talk, since a presentation re-cut from a presentation is a copy. +7 tests where the screen had none, 5 of 5 mutations killed — one only after being retargeted, because `setFailure(err)` appears twice in that file and the first occurrence is a different function. |
| D094 | **A second untyped fixture, and the same two hours of the wrong diagnosis.** The report fixture carried `problems` and no `warnings`; the integrity panel iterates both, so the screen rendered nothing and all seven tests reported that they could not find the button — which was never the problem. | low | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Typed as `Artifact`. Recorded because it is the second time in three commits: an object literal standing in for an API response is a second copy of the contract, and only the compiler keeps the two together. The tell is uniform — every test in the file failing on a *missing element* rather than a wrong value means the render died, not the assertion. |
| D095 | **Four tests were passing over a render that had crashed.** React catches an error thrown during render, unmounts the tree and logs it — and an empty DOM satisfies every `queryBy…` expecting nothing, every `not.toContain`, and every assertion about a call made before the crash. Two board tests and two notebook tests were green over components that had fallen over. **This had already cost four debugging sessions in this repository**, each starting in the wrong place, because a dead render reports itself as whatever the first query asked for: *unable to find role combobox* when the picker was fine, *unable to find the button* when the button was fine. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by instrumenting `console.error` across the whole suite and listing every **passing** test that had logged a render-time TypeError. `tests/setup.ts` now turns that into a failure, with an opt-out list for tests that mean to provoke one — verified against a component written to throw, which passes its own assertion and is failed by the guard. The two board tests broke because pressing a card now opens a panel that fetches two more things; the two notebook tests because creating a note opens it, and the mock answered `{}` for the note. **The second one was hiding behaviour worth asserting** — that a created note is opened rather than left for the researcher to find — which could not have been tested while the step that does it was throwing. |
| D096 | **The cause behind all four: an object literal standing in for an API response is a second copy of the contract.** `problems` where the type says `warnings`; a note with no `body`; an impact payload answered with the board's. Nothing keeps the copy and the contract together unless the fixture is typed. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | The fixtures in the files touched here are typed now, and the render guard catches the rest wherever they are — which is the better half of the fix, since it needs nobody to remember. |
| D097 | **The project's journal could only be read one object at a time.** `GET /projects/{id}/journal` — the stream of everything written, newest first — had no view. `NodeJournal` shows the notes attached to one object, so a researcher returning after a fortnight could check objects one by one and had to remember which ones to check. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | A Journal section beside the Notebook, **reusing `NodeJournal`'s marks and class names rather than inventing a second appearance for the same rows**: they are one table, and two screens styling it differently would teach a reader that the human/model distinction is decorative. Grouped by day, since that is how somebody looks for a thought they half remember. A note attached to nothing says *about the project* rather than leaving the line blank, which reads as a missing value. +14 tests, 6 of 6 mutations killed — the last only after asserting the ◇/◆ mark itself, since `data-kind` is invisible and the mark is what a reader sees before they read anything. |
| D098 | **A test that would have passed or failed depending on the machine's timezone.** The journal groups by the reader's own day, and the fixtures were written as UTC strings: `2026-08-27T22:00Z` is the 27th in London and the 28th in India, so "two days" was one day here and two in CI. | low | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Timestamps are built from local components now, so the fixture means the same thing wherever it runs. Caught on the first run rather than by luck, because the failure was a count that did not match — but it would have been a *green* suite on a UTC machine and a red one on this laptop, which is the worse way round to find it. |
| D099 | **The synthesis screen built the table and stopped.** `POST /projects/{id}/synthesis/key-points` had no caller, so the reading across a set of papers — how many state a design at all, which pairs cannot be compared, where the papers are not independent evidence — was left to the researcher to work out from the matrix. That reading is the part the route exists to do, and it does it **without writing a sentence about the findings**: its own summary is that *"a generated synthesis of five papers is precisely the artifact nobody can check"*, so every point is a count over verified quotations. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Above the table, following this codebase's habit of the reading first and the evidence under it — the matrix is what the counts are counted from. **Two requests, and the second failing must not take the first away**: a researcher who has the comparison keeps it, and is told the reading could not be worked out rather than left with a blank space above a full table, which reads as "these papers had nothing in common". The same care for a genuinely empty result: it says nothing stands out, rather than rendering an empty section under a heading. +6 tests where the screen had none, 5 of 5 mutations killed. |
| D100 | **Figures were saved and then unreachable.** Exporting one creates a record — the spec, the critic's report, and a lineage edge back to the analysis it draws — and nothing listed those records or opened one. The interface downloaded the file and moved on, so a saved figure could be reached only by somebody who had kept its id. `GET /visuals/{id}` and `PATCH /visuals/{id}` both had no caller, **and `visuals` has been indexed on `(project_id, created_at DESC)` since the table was written — for a query nobody had made.** | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Added the missing listing route and a saved-figures list under the live chart, since the list is a record of what has been made rather than the thing a researcher came to that screen to do. **The edit is deliberately narrow**: a title and a caption are how a figure reads, its rows and variables are what it claims, and the domain refuses the second — *"a redraw would leave the old statistics on new data"*. Editing re-runs the critic and reports what it blocked, which is the reason to edit here rather than in the exported file: a caption that overstates the result is exactly what the critic stops, and a caption fixed in a PNG is fixed nowhere. The editor stays open while the critic is still refusing, rather than closing over a change that did not take. +12 tests, 6 of 6 mutations killed. `GET /visuals/{id}` stays in the ledger with a corrected reason: the list carries what the list and its editor need, and nothing yet opens one figure on its own. |
| D101 | **Any unmatched URL answered 500 with an errno.** Found by checking the new listing route against the running server and getting `500 {"error": "PermissionError", "message": "[Errno 1] Operation not permitted"}` — then getting the identical answer from a path that has never existed, which is what showed the route was fine and the **catch-all** was not. `Path.is_file()` swallows the errors that mean *not there* — ENOENT, ENOTDIR — and lets everything else through, so a bundle directory the process cannot traverse raised out of `installed()`, a question that reads like a yes-or-no. A researcher who mistyped a URL got an errno. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | An interface the API cannot read is one it cannot serve, which is the same answer as one that is not installed — and that answer already existed and explains itself: 503, saying so. Guarded in both places, because they fail at different levels: `installed()` on the file, `resolve()` one directory up, where `is_dir()` raises only when the directory *containing* the bundle cannot be traversed. Reachable without a sandbox: a bundle owned by another user, a restrictive umask, a mounted volume with the wrong permissions. Mutation-checked, 3 of 3 killed — the third only after writing the test for the containing directory, since guarding the file left the second call to raise and a guard nothing exercises is a guess. |
| D102 | **The Reports list showed a status that could never say a document had drifted.** `status` and `stale_reason` are columns on `communication_artifacts`, both the reports table and the report header render that status, and **nothing had ever written it** — `POST /projects/{id}/exports/recheck` is the only route that does, and it had no caller. The exports screen was never wrong, which is what hid this: it recomputes on every read, so the one place that could prove drift was the one place that did not need the stored flag. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | A button on the exports screen that records the verdict onto the documents, **not a side effect of loading** — the route is a POST because it writes, and a read that quietly changed a document's status would make opening a report a modification of it. The line beside it says where the recorded verdict shows up, because without that the button looks like it did nothing: the screen it is pressed on already said the same thing, live. +3 tests, 4 of 4 mutations killed. |
| D103 | **The vocabulary queue could be answered and never added to.** `POST /projects/{id}/vocabulary` had no caller, so a project's vocabulary grew only from what the system proposed while reading papers — a researcher who knew their own field's word for something had no way to say so. The decide half was wired up in D077 and this half was not, which left a queue with one end open. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | **The blocker was that no id ever left the server**: an alias points at a canonical variable by id, and `labels()` and `equivalent_columns()` both project the name and drop it while `learned()` only counts them. `vocabulary.variables()` returns them, on the response the vocabulary screen already fetches rather than as a new route. The variable is **chosen, never typed** — a term attached to a variable this project does not have resolves in no paper and looks, from outside, exactly like one that works. A phrase somebody typed is recorded as `researcher` rather than `paper`, because the queue shows where a term came from and those are not the same claim. +6 tests, 5 of 5 mutations killed. |
| D104 | **A project accumulated compatibility verdicts that nobody could see.** Every assessment is stored — verdict, reasoning, what blocked it, what would need harmonising — and `GET /projects/{id}/compatibility` returned them to nobody. A researcher with six datasets asked the same question about the same pair as often as they happened to select it, and never saw that they had asked before. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | **Dated, and described as the answer at that time.** A version id is immutable so the datasets have not moved underneath it, but the verdict depends on harmonisation too, and approving a variable label since then can turn a blocking difference into a shared dimension — presenting an old verdict as current would be the screen asserting something nobody rechecked. The label now travels with the verdict from the server rather than being mapped again in the client: a second copy of that vocabulary is a second thing to keep in step, and the one place this repository did copy such a map needed a drift test to hold the two together. +8 tests, 5 of 5 mutations killed. |
| D105 | **Two of those tests passed against the mutations they were written for.** `waitFor` on an *absence* passes on its first evaluation — before the fetch resolves — so the empty-state test reported success against a screen that had not yet had the chance to render the thing it was checking was not there. And the fallback for a missing dataset was asserted on one side of the pair while the two sides are separate expressions. | low | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Flushed rather than waited for, and both sides asserted. Worth recording as a rule: **`waitFor` proves a thing appeared; it proves nothing about a thing that has not appeared yet.** |
| D106 | **The synthesis screen told a researcher to press a button that was not there yet.** Above Compare it said *"Papers must be read first — use 'Read this paper' if one is missing"*, and that control only appears **after** a comparison has been built and reported the paper missing. So the instruction named something invisible at the moment it was given, and the only way to find an unread paper was to press Compare and be refused. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | The chosen papers are now asked about before comparing, which is what `GET /sources/{id}/extract` exists for — *"what was already read out of this paper. Never runs a model"* — and it had no caller, so a previous reading could not be seen and a paper could be read twice at a model call each. Bounded by the **selection** rather than the project: asking about every paper on arrival would be a query nobody made. Reading one forgets the cached answer for it, or the screen would go on calling it unread. The hint now says what is true without pointing at a control. +5 tests, 4 of 4 mutations killed. |
| D107 | **Choosing a paper re-read it, every time.** The claim test posted to locate claims on every selection — a model call per click, and, the part that matters, a reading that can quietly replace the claims an earlier comparison rested on. `GET /sources/{id}/claims` exists for precisely this and had no caller; its own summary says why the two are separate routes: *"reading the record and re-reading the paper are different acts, and only one of them can change what every downstream comparison rests on."* | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | The record is read first, and a re-read is a visible choice with its consequence beside it — **not that it costs money, but that anything already tested against those claims rested on that reading**. The recorded claims carry the model and prompt version that produced them, and the screen names them, because two readings of one paper can disagree and the difference has to be attributable rather than argued about. A paper nobody has read is still read on selection: there is nothing to show, and reading it is plainly what choosing it meant. +7 tests, 4 of 5 mutations killed — the fifth is the initial value of a flag that no path can observe, since every route to a rendered reading sets it first. |
| D108 | **Reconciling two papers re-read both of them, every time.** `reconcile_papers` called `locate_claims` per side, which always asks a model — two readings per comparison, and the same pair reconciled twice could come back different. That is not only cost: `stored_claims` says so itself — two readings of one paper can disagree, and when they do *"the disagreement has to be attributable rather than argued about"* — so the verdicts could change under a researcher with nothing on screen saying the inputs had. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `claim_test.claims_for` reads the record and only reads the paper when there is none. **Comparing two papers is not a request to re-read them**; re-reading stays deliberate and stays where it belongs, as its own act on the claim-test screen. A recorded reading carries the model and prompt that produced *those* claims rather than the model configured today, and the response now says which sides had to be read. +4 tests, 4 of 4 mutations killed. |
| D109 | **`POST /projects/{id}/reconcile` is not a gap.** It reconciles two *claims*; the screen reconciles two papers, and `/reconcile-papers` already returns every pair of their claims — so the directed question is answered inside the broad one. | low | recorded | Claude (Sarthak) | The ledger reason said only that the interface calls the other route, which reads as an oversight. Building a control for it would recompute something the reader already has, so the entry now says why it stays unbuilt rather than implying somebody forgot. |
| D110 | **A test run killed part-way poisoned the next one.** Most tests sit inside a transaction that is rolled back, but the HTTP ones go through `TestClient` and commit real rows, tidying up in fixtures that only run when a session ends gracefully. So an interrupted run leaves accounts, projects and analyses behind, and the next run inherits them. **Not hypothetical:** an interruption in this session left rows that failed six tests in the file that happened to sign in as the same address, and every one of them pointed at the tests rather than at the wreckage — the usual shape of contaminated state. `conftest` already recovered from a *half-initialised cluster* with some care; nothing handled a database that was merely full. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Every session now truncates the data tables after migrating. **It refuses to run anywhere but the test database**: this clears every table there is, and pointed at a researcher's project directory that is not a cleanup but data loss — so it checks `THROUGHLINE_HOME` against the temp path set at import and raises rather than guessing. One `TRUNCATE ... CASCADE`, so foreign keys never see a half-emptied database, and `schema_migrations` is kept or every migration would re-run against a schema that already has them. +3 tests, 3 of 3 mutations killed — **and the third one proved the point by damaging the test database**: truncating the migration record left the schema in place but unrecorded, and the next run failed on a column that already existed. Repaired by discarding the temp directory, which is exactly what that safety check exists to make unthinkable anywhere else. |
| D111 | **The product did not agree with itself about how many comparison verbs it has.** `compare.tsx` said *"Part I has six verbs. Two are built"*; the README said *"five of the six ... are wired"*; the screen renders **eight** tabs, every one of them a real component. Three counts of one thing, none of them right. | low | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Both corrected against what the screen actually renders: all six of Part I's verbs, plus two this product added — figures, and scan ↔ scan. The ordinary fate of a count kept in prose beside the thing it counts, which is why the comment now says so. |
| D112 | **Chart primitives was the one navigation entry that is not a step in research.** It sat in *Communicate*, between Figures and Notebook, and renders every primitive against illustrative data with **no project involved** — its own summary says the point is that "it renders" should be checkable rather than asserted. | low | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Moved to *This machine*, beside the two pages already filed there for the same reason: does the camera see my hands, can I draw in the air, do the charts draw. A second argument for the move: the Figures screen chooses a chart from the shape of the data and says why, and a gallery invites browsing charts detached from data — the habit §10 warns against — so the two should not sit together reading as alternatives. |
| D113 | **An Ollama cloud model would have been chosen as though it ran here.** Ollama serves models with a `:cloud` tag that execute on ollama.com, and reports them from `/api/tags` beside the local ones. `installed()` listed them identically and the picker offered them identically, so selecting one looked exactly like selecting a local model — while every passage of every paper the system reads would leave the machine. **The README's guarantee is the whole point of the product**: *"nothing leaves the machine unless the researcher connects an external service — and where one can be connected, the interface says so before it is used."* The hosted provider already confirms before use, on the grounds that it is "the one action here that changes where data goes"; an Ollama cloud model changes it just as completely and asked nothing, **because the check was written against the provider rather than against where the model actually runs**. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found while connecting this installation to `glm-5.3-flash:cloud` — the request that would have exercised the gap. Models now report `runs_here`, the choice itself carries the mark, and picking a remote one confirms first with what would be sent. The tag is the signal rather than asking Ollama, because `/api/tags` does not say where a model runs and a wrong guess here only shows up as data already sent. +4 tests, 3 of 3 mutations killed, including the one that matters in the other direction: a false positive would put a warning on a model that never leaves the machine and teach a researcher to dismiss it. |
| D114 | **Ollama answering with an error was reported as Ollama being down.** `urllib.error.HTTPError` is a subclass of `URLError`, so a response carrying a status code was caught by the same branch as a dead socket and reported as *"Ollama is not reachable — start it with `ollama serve`"*. That is false whenever the server answered, and it sends a researcher to fix the one thing that is not wrong. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by pointing this installation at `glm-5.3-flash:cloud`, which answered **402 Payment Required** — and was reported as a stopped server that had in fact been running throughout. §104's rule is the server's own words, never "something went wrong", and Ollama puts its reason in the body: the message now reads *"Ollama answered 402 for 'glm-5.3-flash:cloud': this model requires a subscription or extra usage, upgrade at ..."*, which is something a person can act on. A `:cloud` model adds that the account lives at ollama.com, because a 402 on a local model is a different problem. +5 tests, 4 of 4 mutations killed — **the last only after testing the routing rather than the wording**: every test called the message function directly, which proves nothing about which branch an HTTP error actually lands in, and that branch was the bug. |
| D115 | **A discovery sweep recorded its own denominator and showed none of it.** `discovery_runs` holds how many pairs were considered, how many were dropped and why, how many were tested, and the correction the survivors were judged against — and `GET /discoveries/{id}` returned it to nobody. The screen listed the connections that came out and said nothing about the search that produced them. **In this product that is not cosmetic**: a q-value means nothing without the number of tests it was corrected across, and the same p-value is worth less after forty comparisons than after four. A reader shown only the survivors cannot tell which they are looking at. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | The run id came back from the POST and was being discarded; it is kept, and the sweep now accounts for itself under the results. Everything is read from the run rather than recomputed, so it cannot disagree with the correction that was actually applied. **Exclusions are reported apart from failures** — a column dropped for being constant was never a candidate, and listing the two together would inflate what was tried. A run still going says so instead of showing a half-written count, which would read as a search that tested very little. +11 tests, 5 of 5 mutations killed. |
| D116 | **A script that forgets one environment variable writes to the researcher's real database.** `db.py` resolves its home as `os.environ.get("THROUGHLINE_HOME", Path.home() / ".throughline-os")` — so anything run from the repo without that variable set silently connects to real project data instead of the test cluster. `conftest.py` sets it at import, so the suite is safe; a throwaway script run beside the suite is not, and looks identical while it runs. **Not hypothetical, and not a guess:** while investigating D048 I ran two one-off scripts that way and inserted two users, two projects and two queued workflow runs into `~/.throughline-os`. They were identified by id and removed with the owner's approval, and nothing of theirs was modified — the scripts only inserted. The measurements taken with those scripts were also worthless, because they were reading a different database from the one under test, and that was not visible until the numbers stopped making sense. **A guard was offered and declined for now**, so nothing here is changed: recorded so the next person does not have to rediscover it. The obvious shape would be refusing the fallback unless a caller asks for it explicitly. | medium | fixed | — | `db.py:27`. Reproduced by running any script that imports `throughline_domain.db` with `THROUGHLINE_HOME` unset: it opens the cluster under `~/.throughline-os`. The two-line defence used while finishing D048 was an assert in the script itself that `THROUGHLINE_HOME` equals the temp test home, which is exactly the check the library could make for itself. **Fixed — the guard that was offered and declined, built when asked for.** `data_root()` refuses an unnamed home *only* when it is running out of a source checkout, detected by `scripts/manage.py` and `TASKS.md` sitting above this module; an installed copy in site-packages keeps the silent default, because for a researcher `~/.throughline-os` is the right answer and nothing should stand between them and it. `manage.py` declares `THROUGHLINE_ALLOW_INSTALLED_HOME` once in `main()` — the launcher *is* the product acting on purpose, and `dev`, `doctor` and `backup` all mean the real database — while `THROUGHLINE_HOME` still wins, so `preflight` running the suite is unaffected. **The cost, stated:** starting `uvicorn` or the worker by hand from a checkout now refuses until a home is named. That is the ambiguous case this exists for and the message names the three ways out, but it is a change to a thing that used to work. Proven by breaking it: the guard fired inside the running API mid-edit, because `--reload` re-imported the module in a process started before the flag existed — the refusal was legible and a fresh `./scripts/dev.sh` starts clean with none. 7 tests, both halves mutation-checked. |
| D117 | **The exploration ledger's correction family was identified by a browser tab, and the record it kept was unreachable the moment that tab closed.** `lib/session.ts` minted a UUID into `sessionStorage`. That decision was argued carefully and was half right — `localStorage` really would be wrong, because one family per browser profile grows forever and corrects today's result as the ten-thousandth thing ever tried. The half that was wrong is that a *tab* is not the alternative. It is invisible, so a researcher could not see what was being corrected together and could not tell us it was wrong; two windows split one afternoon into two families, each reporting its looks with more confidence than they had earned; and — the part that decided it — `exploration_tests` is a durable table, so closing the tab discarded only the *identifier* and left every past look in the database with nothing able to reach it. The record of how often the data was questioned is the record this product exists to keep, and it was being orphaned by a browser event. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T088. Reproduced before the fix by recording looks, closing the tab, and observing the rows still present in `exploration_tests` with no route to them from any screen. |
| D118 | **`Binned.tsx` drew no gridlines and no axis ticks at all, in either theme.** It used bare `grid`, `axis`, `tick`, `axis-label` and `chart-note` where every other chart in the codebase uses `chart-grid`, `chart-axis`, `chart-tick`, `chart-axis-label` and `chart-caption`. Those five names are defined nowhere, so the rules never applied — and an SVG `<line>` with no `stroke` is not faint, it is *invisible*. The tick `<text>` fell back to the UA default fill rather than `var(--ink-faint)`, and the chart also missed the high-contrast block that the four real class names already have. Six entries removed from `KNOWN_UNSTYLED`; the guard passes with the shorter list. |

Mutation-tested: restoring each half of the old behaviour fails the corresponding tests (5, 2 and 2). |

| D128 | **The resizable panels stored their layout and never restored it.** `react-resizable-panels` reports layouts as **percentage shares** — `{"rail": 27.143, "workspace": 47.143, "inspector": 25.714}` for a 1400px window — and the validator was checking each value against the *pixel* bounds the panels are configured with (180–380 for the rail). Every layout the library actually wrote failed validation and was silently discarded, so dragging a divider stored a value and the next reload went back to the defaults. **The unit tests could not have caught it**: they built layouts with `writeLayout` in pixels and read them back, which round-trips perfectly while agreeing with nothing the library produces — the suite was green throughout. It took opening the page. | medium | fixed | — | Found while verifying T091, by reading `localStorage` after a keyboard resize and seeing `27.143` where the validator wanted 180–380. `sane()` now validates shape rather than size — every share a finite positive percentage, and the set summing to 100 — because the pixel limits are enforced by `minSize`/`maxSize` on the panels, which is the only place that knows the viewport. Measured with `brew info` on exactly that URL with the line removed, not inferred. The audit's advice is correct for projects whose archives are named `name-1.2.3` and wrong here, which is what makes it dangerous: it looks authoritative and the formula still installs. | medium | fixed | — | The line stays, with a comment saying why and a test that fails if it is removed. | D133 | **Building the interface silently breaks a running dev server, including when a release does it.** `next build` writes its manifests through `.next` regardless of `NEXT_DIST_DIR`, and `next dev` keeps its working files there — so a build pulls them out from under a running server and every route it serves answers 500 with `ENOENT: .next/static/development/_buildManifest.js.tmp.*`. The build reports success; the process that breaks is a different one; and the recovery (`rm -rf apps/web/.next`, restart) is obvious only once you already know. Found the hard way — I ran `build-interface` to size the release tarball while the dev server was up, and the researcher hit the 500 on `/gesture-check`. **`release` inherits it**, because it builds the interface when no export exists, so this is a way of shipping that breaks your own machine. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T098. But nowhere did it say the family had 200 tests in it, or 4. `resolve_block`'s own docstring explains why that matters: the same run in a family of five and a family of five hundred yields different q. So the document put its most important number in front of a reviewer with no way to weigh it, which is the failure the ledger exists to prevent, one step further downstream than anyone had looked. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T106. Found by asking what a researcher actually hands over, rather than what the screens show — the rigour chain (preregister → ledger → deviations → narrative) turned out to be built and reachable, and it stopped at the edge of the artefact. |
| D145 | **Three catalogued globes were drawn as flat grids.** "3D globe", "Population globe" and "Climate globe" were filed under the `surface` primitive, which draws a height field on a rectangular grid — so the catalogue's own measurement showed 253 entries resolving to 15 distinct pictures, and three of them were the wrong *shape* rather than merely undifferentiated styling. A globe drawn as a plane misrepresents the thing it is named after, and no amount of restyling fixes a shape. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T099. | D146 | **The globe's ocean was drawn a third of its true size, with the coastlines outside it.** The disc's radius was taken by projecting `{x:1,y:0,z:0}` and measuring back to the centre. Same shape as D128 and D134: arithmetic that round-trips with itself and agrees with nothing on screen. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T099. `silhouetteRadius` derives the tangent-cone radius `d/√(d²−1)`, which depends on no rotation at all — and that rotation-independence is what the new test asserts, together with the property the formula exists to have: no visible point of the globe may fall outside the outline drawn around it, swept over 25 camera angles. The read returns `""`, lands in whatever `|| fallback` the caller wrote, and paints a plausible wrong colour; it is quieter than the `var()` case the file was written for, which at least drops the declaration. Found by writing the bug: a globe shading gradient whose two stops silently became the same colour, producing exactly the flat fill it was meant to replace. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T099. Reads are now stripped before the definition scan and checked against it separately, so a read can never excuse itself. Mutation-tested by reintroducing `--surface`. |
| D148 | **The workspace had one address for twenty-one screens.** The section lived in `useState<Section>("overview")` with no router, no query parameter and no storage, so four ordinary things did not work: a researcher could not send a colleague the Connections view, a reload returned them to Overview however deep they were, reopening the app did the same, and the browser's Back gesture **left the product** rather than going back one section — the only history entry was the landing page. This is **D128 one level up**: the panel layout was persisted because "dragging a divider stored a value and the next reload went back to the defaults", and the same reload discarded the researcher's *place*, which is the larger half of that complaint. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T108. The URL, not `localStorage` — storage fixes the reload and none of the rest, cannot be linked to or gone back through, and is actively wrong on a second tab where two windows fight over one key. `pushState` per navigation so Back moves between sections, a `popstate` listener so the address bar and the screen cannot disagree, and the section read inside the `useState` initialiser so a deep link paints its own section first rather than flashing Overview. **Partly unverified**: the parsing, the fallbacks and the round trip are tested and mutation-tested, and a deep link was confirmed live to survive without rewrite, but clicking the rail and pressing Back need the API running, and this machine has no PostgreSQL. |
| D149 | **Six of the seven spatial charts could be turned only with a mouse.** All seven listened for `pointerdown`; one listened for a key. Six had no `tabIndex`, so Tab could not reach them, and five had no label, so a screen reader met an unannotated canvas — a blank. This is **D127 one family over**: that defect was lists selecting a row from `onClick` with nothing focusable inside, and its guard asks every component the question rather than the five known files — but it asks about `onClick`, and a drag is not a click, so the whole spatial family sat outside it. **Worse here than it looks**: rotation is not a convenience on these charts, it is the depth cue. Motion parallax is the strongest signal a flat screen has for which mark is in front, and §10 tolerates a third dimension only where depth carries information — so a reader who cannot rotate is looking at one fixed projection of a tangle for ever, which is the picture §10 exists to forbid. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T109. One `useSpatialKeys` hook rather than six copies of the globe's handler, since six copies are six chances for the step sizes to drift and a reader who learns Left turns the network expects Left to turn the volume by as much. Verified live on all seven by pixel hash — the picture changes on the key press, not merely the attribute. |
| D150 | **Adding `tabIndex` made six canvases focusable with no visible focus ring — a defect I introduced while fixing D149.** A focusable element with no focus style is worse than an unfocusable one: a keyboard reader tabs into a chart and nothing says they are there (WCAG 2.4.7). The grab cursor and focus ring existed on `.chart-canvas`, and none of the six carried the class. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T109 in the same pass, with the class supplied by the hook so a rotatable canvas cannot get the keys without the affordance. | D151 | **The network's only depth cue was paint order, which says nothing where marks do not overlap.** A node six units away and one two units away were drawn identically, so a spatial graph read as a flat tangle until it was moved — and `project` returns a `scale` factor documented "for scaling a mark by its distance" that nothing used. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T109 **without** binding depth to size: radius already carries node weight, and a size that means two things means neither. The recorded reason for declining was itself slightly wrong and is corrected in place — it said depth "is already carried by the projection", and the projection carries depth into a mark's *position*, not its size; the conclusion stands on the weight argument alone. Depth went on two channels carrying nothing: aerial perspective with a floor, so no mark fades out of existence, and a halo in the page colour drawn behind each node, which is what actually makes the sort visible — two circles of one colour overlapping read as a figure-of-eight, not as one in front of the other. |
| D152 | **The network layout claimed a frame budget it had never been measured against.** `DEFAULT_LAYOUT` said its pass count was "few enough to run inside a frame budget" for "a few hundred nodes", and that "a layout that took a second would be computed on a background thread". Measured, the all-pairs repulsion is exactly quadratic: 42ms at 300 nodes against a 16.7ms budget, 121ms at 500, and **1.93 seconds at 2000** — synchronous, with the interface frozen throughout, well past the one-second mark the same note said would call for a worker. A claim the data does not support, in a docstring that reads as authoritative reasoning. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T110. The claim is replaced by the measured table. Note the *neighbouring* comment was honest and was right at the time — "quadratic and fine at this scale... a graph large enough for that to hurt needs a spatial index, and building one before anything renders would be optimising a picture nobody has seen". The picture has since been seen. |
| D153 | **Barnes-Hut made the layout slower at every size a person is likely to open.** The first working version allocated thirteen typed arrays per pass — 120 passes over a thousand nodes is tens of megabytes of short-lived garbage — so the asymptotically better algorithm lost to the loop it replaced at 100 (2.8x slower), 300, 500 and 800 nodes. The tests were green throughout; only the benchmark showed it. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T110 by reusing the tree and the force vector across passes, and then by **keeping both algorithms** behind a measured crossover rather than pretending one wins everywhere. A first attempt to find that crossover was wrong and was thrown away: it compared the tree against a stripped all-pairs loop that only accumulated a scalar, which the engine optimised into something the layout never runs, and it reported "pairs win" at every size including 5000. |
| D154 | **The octree lost a body whenever a cell split.** A cell holding one body must push it down into a child when it subdivides; the first version left it recorded in the parent's totals and present in no child, so any traversal that opened that parent walked straight past it. The node repelled nothing, was repelled normally, and drifted into its neighbours — producing a completely plausible layout, which is why it is worth naming: a layout has no right answer to check by eye. | high | fixed | — | Caught before shipping by reading the insertion back, and now guarded by checking the traversal against brute force at theta 0, where the two must agree to nine decimal places. Mutation-tested: restoring the bug fails four tests. |
| D155 | **The catalogue told a reader there was no renderer for the globe, three sections below a working globe.** `standing()` fell through to "no renderer for it yet" whenever `isDrawable` was false — but `isDrawable` asks whether an *example* can be generated, not whether a renderer exists. `GENERATORS.places` is `null` deliberately, because a fabricated choropleth of the world reads as an epidemiological finding rather than as synthetic data, so all four globes reported the strongest promise as the weakest. `builtWithoutAnExample()` already named this third category and the browser did not use it — in a file whose own docstring opens by insisting that "this product can do X", "this product could do X" and "somebody has written X" are three different promises. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T111. Found by the researcher reading the rendered list, not by the suite. |
| D156 | **"Interactive globe" claimed a library the repository ships, and was the fourth globe filed under `surface`.** Its note read "a basemap and a projection, neither of which is here" — `world-atlas` is a bundled dependency, `d3-geo` projects for `Geographic`, and `Globe3D` had been drawing on the same page for two commits. T107 moved three globes off `surface` and missed this one, which is the argument for asking the question of every entry rather than of the three that were noticed. `needs-library` is the most expensive claim in the catalogue to get wrong: a reader deciding whether the tool fits their work takes it at its word and stops looking. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T111 and guarded by `catalogue-tells-the-truth.test.ts`, which checks negative claims against `package.json` rather than against the catalogue's internal consistency — the existing registry tests passed throughout, because neither entry contradicted anything *inside* the catalogue. |

Filed under `surface` as well — a trajectory of atoms is not a height field over a grid, the same mis-primitive as the globes. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T111 to `specialist` / `mesh` / `viewer: molstar`, and the status was **checked rather than assumed**: the viewer reads `.xyz` and `.gro`, both multi-frame, and Mol* parses a multi-model file as a real trajectory. It cannot read the binary formats, so the note now names `.xtc`, `.dcd` and `.trr` explicitly instead of claiming the whole capability. |
| D158 | **All 24 remaining `needs-library` entries gave no reason at all, so the guard written one commit earlier passed over every one of them vacuously.** `catalogue-tells-the-truth` checks a `needs-library` note against `package.json` — and an entry with no note has nothing to check. The strongest negative claim the catalogue makes was being asserted 24 times without saying which library or why, which is unfalsifiable by a test, a reviewer, or the researcher deciding whether the tool does their work. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T112. Every one now states its reason, and the guard requires it — the structural fix, because a claim nobody can check is how the other two got in. Mutation-tested by stripping a note. |
| D159 | **Fifteen entries said "needs a library this codebase does not have" while the library sat in `package.json`.** `@kitware/vtk.js` is a dependency and already draws "CAD model" from `.stl`, `.obj`, `.ply` and `.vtp`. For stress on a mesh the real gap is here, not upstream: the mesh path is `mapper.setInputData(data)` with no scalar range and no legend, so the field would not be readable. Two different admissions were being made as one, and only one of them is a decision somebody else made for the reader. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T112 using `primitive-missing`, a status that already existed, was documented as "the primitive itself is not written yet", and **no entry used**. | D160 | **Five entries claimed a missing library for a plain mesh the installed viewer draws.** Urban 3D map, Human anatomy, Organ model, Brain model and Cellular model are cases where the geometry *is* the content, so the objection recorded in `specialist-vtk.test.tsx` — that an uncoloured mesh shown for "Stress visualization" would be "a placebo with a viewer around it" — does not apply: there is no solution field for the shape to be mistaken for. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T112 to `specialist` / `vtk`. The geomap test that pinned Urban 3D map was **read before being changed** and its reasoning was about *geomap* ("flipping it would be the claim this viewer cannot make"); its title, "leaves the city model to whoever wires a mesh reader", describes vtk.js exactly. It now asserts the guarantee it is entitled to — that geomap does not draw it. |
| D161 | **Every `.vtp` failed to open, with the suite green.** The three geometry readers were called through one branch — `reader.parse(bytes)` — and they do not share an interface: `vtkSTLReader` and `vtkPLYReader` expose `parse`, while `vtkXMLPolyDataReader` inherits `parseAsArrayBuffer` from `XMLReader` and has no `parse` at all. So `.vtp` died on `reader.parse is not a function` while sitting in the viewer's own accepted list, and the `.vti` branch four lines above already called `parseAsArrayBuffer` correctly. **The seam tests could not see it**: they hand the viewer a stand-in reader, and a stand-in has whatever methods it is written with. | high | fixed | — | Found by building a `.vtp` and opening it in the browser, which is the only thing that had not been tried. Guarded now against the real package's module surface rather than against a double — the base `XMLReader` defines `parseAsArrayBuffer` and no `parse` — with a companion test that STL and PLY still take `parse`, so the fix cannot over-reach. |
| D162 | **A `.vtp` carrying a field was coloured by it, wrongly, with no legend.** vtk.js leaves `scalarVisibility` on and defaults a mapper's scalar range to [0, 1]. It reads exactly like a solved field, and there was nothing to check it against; the caption said only "625 points, 1,152 polygons". **Worse than an uncoloured mesh, which was the assumption this rested on.** | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T113. `setScalarVisibility(false)`, so the viewer draws the shape and only the shape, and the caption now names the array and its range and says it is not mapped. `specialist-vtk` already argued the principle for the catalogue — an uncoloured mesh offered as "Stress visualization" would be "a placebo with a viewer around it" — and colouring it wrongly is that same placebo with a coat of paint. |
| D163 | **Two of the four specialist viewers never checked that the modules they name exist, and niivue checked nothing at all.** `specialist-vtk` had made the argument already — "a misspelled vtk.js path is invisible until a researcher opens the viewer: it type-checks, it bundles, and it rejects at run time inside a dynamic import nobody executes in the suite" — and the reasoning was never carried across to the other three. This is the same shape as D161: a failure that only appears when somebody opens a file. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T114 with one guard over all four viewers, mutation-tested on a plausible-but-wrong vtk path and on a renamed niivue package. It deliberately resolves **modules, not methods**: vtk.js generates most setters through macros — `setScalarVisibility` appears nowhere in `Mapper.js`, only `scalarVisibility: true` among its defaults — so a name-grepping guard would report working code as broken, and a guard that is wrong about a correct thing is the kind that gets deleted. |

The array is painted over **its own** range through a shared ramp, with a legend in the DOM. `useLookupTableScalarRange` is the load-bearing line: without it the mapper keeps its default window and samples the transfer function through the wrong one, which is the original [0, 1] defect wearing a lookup table. |
| D166 | **The legend contradicted the sentence directly above it.** The caption read `0 to 240` and the bar read `0` and `239.96400451660156` — one number, formatted in two places, disagreeing in the one spot a reader compares them, and claiming seventeen digits of precision the measurement does not have. Found in the browser; every test passed. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T115. The legend carries written strings rather than raw floats, produced by the same `measured()` the caption uses, so the two cannot drift. Same principle as the ramp: the bar's colours are `FIELD_RAMP` itself, not a second copy of it. |
| D167 | **A file with no field said nothing about it, under a heading that promises one.** `specialist-vtk` had named this risk precisely — a researcher who picks "Stress visualization", opens an STL of a bracket and sees an uncoloured shape has been handed "a placebo with a viewer around it" — and painting the field answers it only for files that carry one. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T115: a mesh with no per-point array now says so, and says what a field would arrive as. | D170 | **The page said "The three below" above six named primitives and seven figures.** A count typed into prose beside a list that is derived — so the list grew and the word did not. | D171 | **"A further 26 are drawn by a specialist library" double-counted seven entries.** "A further" asserts the sets are disjoint and they are not: a CT volume draws as voxels here *and* properly in niivue from a scan; likewise MRI, PET, Point map, Molecular surface and the two reconstructions. 216 + 26 + 4 came to 260 against a catalogue of 253. **Nothing was wrong with the data** — each number was separately correct, which is why no test saw it. The wrong thing was a word implying a partition. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T117 by dropping the claim, and guarded by asserting the property the sentence asserts rather than the numbers it prints. | D172 | **A surface with no observations described observations anyway.** One branch said "No observations are drawn on this surface", and then, unconditionally, "Faint cells have no observation near them — the smoothest part of a fitted surface is usually the part with no data under it." | D174 | **Two tests failed intermittently, and my diagnosis of them was wrong twice.** I reported them as "asserting on elapsed milliseconds". They were exceeding **vitest's 5s default timeout** while the suite competed with a dev server — 942ms and 367ms respectively on an idle machine, seen failing at 5.4s, 6.3s and 27s. | low | fixed | — | Fixed by T118 with explicit 30s timeouts and a note saying what the number is not. Shrinking the input was rejected: the point of a hundred thousand points is that a recursive implementation cannot survive them, so a shorter path is a weaker claim. Verified by running the full suite twice **with the stack up and load climbing to 9.5** — the condition that produced the failures. |
| D175 | **A `.vtp` with its field on the cells was told it had no values.** Only `getPointData` was read, and stress per element is the ordinary way a solver writes an FEA result — so the field was present, the picture plain, and the caption agreed with the picture rather than with the file. The mapper was also left to choose its own scalar mode, which searches for point scalars. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T118: both arrays are read, the mapper is told explicitly which to use, and the caption names whether a value is per point or per cell. Points win when a file carries both — a nodal field interpolates across each cell and an element field paints each facet flat — and the reader is told which they are looking at. Verified with a real cell-data file: 800 elements, flat per-facet shading, "one value per cell". |
| D176 | **The layout froze the interface, and the docstring had said so for weeks.** `DEFAULT_LAYOUT` promised "a layout that took a second would be computed on a background thread"; after Barnes-Hut it still took 1.4s at two thousand nodes and 6s at five thousand, synchronously — unable to scroll, resize or answer a key throughout. Half of a frozen interface is a frozen interface. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T119. A worker above 400 nodes (161ms at five hundred is where a freeze stops being a stutter); below that the round trip costs more than the work. The synchronous path is also the fallback, and it is load-bearing in two ordinary places — the test environment has no `Worker` and neither does a server render. **Verified in the browser** by instrumenting the constructor: Turbopack ships the worker as a `blob:` URL, which is why it never appeared in resource timing and why "no chunk loaded" was the wrong inference. |
| D177 | **My own hook looped for ever against the real caller.** `CatalogueChart` built its example inside render, so every pass handed down a fresh `graph` describing exactly the same thing. The `useMemo` it replaced survived only because a memo recomputes where an effect re-fires. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T119 by keying on a rolling content signature rather than identity, and by memoizing the example — which was rebuilding a 24³ voxel grid and a whole force relaxation on every render for a result that depends only on the entry. |
| D178 | **A hook that decided during render whether a `Worker` exists broke hydration.** The server said no and the browser said yes for the same graph, so the markup disagreed and React reported a hydration failure on a page that otherwise looked fine. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T119: *what* goes across is decided by the data alone, *where* it is computed is decided after mounting. | D180 | **The ledger recorded work as unassessed while it was finished and tested — the §74 failure, repeating.** §54 WORKSPACE LAYOUT and §56 COMMAND PALETTE sat at `unreviewed` with eleven tests apiece behind them, and §81 REDUCED MOTION sat there with sixteen `prefers-reduced-motion` blocks in the stylesheet. | D181 | **§81 was not only unrecorded, it was incompletely implemented.** Writing the missing test found five animated classes with no reduced-motion cover, three of which genuinely moved — a treemap tile sliding to its new rectangle, a progress bar, a state label animating `transform`. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T120. **Reduce, not remove**: each keeps its change and loses its travel, and the state label keeps its colour fade. | D182 | **§73 asked for bibliography export in one line and nothing did it.** Citations were built and *better* than the section asked — entailment checked per claim rather than asserted — while the sentence "Allow bibliography export" had no implementation anywhere. Recorded as the gap in T121, closed here. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T122. BibTeX over the papers *actually cited*, never the whole corpus — exporting everything read would pad a reference list in a way a reviewer notices. |
| D183 | **I recorded §54 WORKSPACE LAYOUT as `built` in error, while fixing this very ledger.** The row cited the resizable shell panels. §54 asks for objects to be arranged on a canvas — alignment guides, magnetic snapping, grouping, frames, layers, spatial zones, *"organize this workspace by experiment"* — and none of it exists: the board is `Board.tsx` plus `CardDetail.tsx`, with no snapping or grouping anywhere. Dragging the rail wider is not arranging a workspace. | high | fixed | — | Corrected to `not-built` by T123. Found by a reader asking whether the navigation rail has requirements behind it, which is a question nothing in the suite asks. The lesson is narrow and sharp: I matched a section by its *title* rather than reading what it demanded, two sessions after writing a guard against exactly that class of mistake. |
| D184 | **My own audit of the navigation rail was wrong in three of its findings, and I published it.** I reported Overview, Sources and Patterns as having no requirement behind them, and suggested trimming two of them. All three are specified — the spec names "Overview mode", and carries 18 mentions of import and 23 of cluster. The method was keyword probing over a run-together specification, and the probes were guesses (`project overview` instead of `overview mode`). | high | fixed | — | Corrected in T123 and here. There is a **category error** underneath the wrong details: a specification describing *capabilities* need not name a *navigation entry*, so "does this rail item have a section" was close to the wrong question. The answerable ones are: is any capability behind the rail unrequested, and is any requested capability unreachable? |
| D185 | **The privacy guarantee was a constant.** `OllamaProvider` accepts a remote host and reported `local=True` whatever it pointed at — so the same backend against `127.0.0.1` and against `https://ai.university.edu` made the identical promise while doing opposite things, and the sentence a researcher reads before pasting unpublished work said *"nothing sent to it leaves the device"* while every prompt did. A guarantee that cannot be false is not one. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T127. Locality is derived from the address and carried through **every** return, because the early ones were the wrong paths: an unreachable remote host reported `local=True` and looked like a laptop that happened to be offline. A machine on the same network counts as hosted. |
| D186 | **An empty AI policy failed open.** `(os.environ.get(...) or "external")` treats an empty string as unset, so `THROUGHLINE_AI_POLICY=""` — an explicit act by an operator — selected the *most permissive* setting. Caught by my own test on the way in, which is the only reason it is a note rather than a shipped defect. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T127: absent means nobody has restricted this deployment, and present-but-unreadable permits nothing. |
| D187 | **A figure was drawn from the first 500 rows and called it a sample.** `_visual_sample` took `.head(limit)`, and research data arrives sorted — by date, by site, by arm — so those rows are the earliest patients, or one hospital, or one condition. That subset was rendered *beneath statistics computed from the whole dataset*, so the picture and the numbers next to it described different populations. The docstring called it "a bounded sample". | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T129: a uniform sample with a fixed seed, so the same figure is the same picture — an unseeded one redraws differently on every open and a researcher cannot tell a redraw from a change in the data. |
| D188 | **Sampling was never communicated, which is §47's closing line verbatim.** The endpoint returned x, y and group with no indication that 500 of two million rows were used. A caption can only say what the endpoint tells it. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T129: `rows_total`, `rows_drawn` and the method travel with the points. Guarded by a test that reads the endpoint body, because the account can be computed correctly and silently never reach the response. |
| D189 | **Twenty-one entries told a reader that depth they were looking at was in their data, when a force simulation had invented it.** `spatial: "inherently"` means, in the catalogue's own words, *"the data has three meaningful dimensions"*. Every one of the 35 network entries takes `needs: "graph"`, and that shape is nodes and edges — **no coordinates anywhere in it** — so all three dimensions come out of `layoutGraph`. The split was arbitrary on its face: "Citation network" and "Dependency graph" have the same primitive, the same data shape and the same renderer, and carried opposite answers with no note either way. This is §10 exactly — a cloud of nodes in depth reads as structure *discovered* and it is structure *arranged*. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T130. All 35 are `framed`, and `spatial-claims.test.ts` generalises the rule past the networks that revealed it: no entry may claim inherent depth over a shape that carries no position. A companion test keeps the correction from over-reaching — a surface over a grid and a volume over voxels are three-dimensional measurements, and marking everything `framed` to be safe would make the field say nothing. |
| D190 | **Six of the eight spatial renderers could never draw a researcher's data.** Measured: `Network3D`, `Isosurface3D`, `Field3D`, `Lines3D`, `Bars3D` and `Surface` are imported only by `app/charts-3d/page.tsx` and `CatalogueChart` — a demonstration page and a synthetic-data browser. Only `Globe3D` (via `mapview`) and `VoxelVolume` (via imaging) sit on a research path. **The root cause was not navigation.** `figures.tsx` imports five 2D charts and no 3D one, and the visualization recommender — the thing that chooses a figure from the shape of an analysis — contained **zero** mentions of a surface or any spatial chart. No analysis could produce one, so no menu arrangement could have connected them. | high | fixed | — | First link built by T131: a fitted response over exactly two continuous predictors, which is the case §10 admits without argument. |
| D191 | **React reports two children with the same key, `continuous`, on every first page load.** Non-unique keys let React duplicate or omit children, so this is a correctness warning rather than noise, even though nothing visibly wrong has been found yet. **What is established, by measurement:** it is client-only — the Next server log has no such warning, so the list renders after data arrives; the key is the string `continuous`, which is a `semantic_type`, not a column name; the example project has exactly three columns with that type (`consumption_ddd`, `resistance_pct`, `gdp_per_capita`) and React warns twice, which is what three duplicates produce; column *names* are unique, so the list is keyed on the type. **Why it resisted a search:** React deduplicates this warning per key for the life of the runtime, so it fires once on the first load and never again — clicking through every section in a fresh tab captures nothing, and a `console.error` hook installed after mount is always too late. **Ruled out by reading the code:** `figures.tsx` (keys by `c.name`), `mapview.tsx` (`c.name`), `speccurve.tsx` (columns come from `Object.keys`, which cannot repeat), `runanalysis.tsx` (roles are `value`/`group`, never `continuous`, checked against `method_variables()`), `Overview` (fixed step labels), `Shell` (group labels and hrefs), the Inspector (no lists). A module-scope `console.error` hook added temporarily to `app/workspace/page.tsx` captured nothing and was reverted. **Not fixed, and recorded rather than dropped**, because a defect somebody has to rediscover costs more than one written down. The next step that would settle it is patching `react-dom`'s warning to print a component stack, or bisecting by commenting out sub-trees. | medium | withdrawn | — | **Withdrawn — it does not reproduce, and the reason it appeared to is the finding.** The console I read it from belonged to a tab that had been open across a sixteen-hour-old dev server, a merge of eleven commits and a restart, and console history accumulates: the warnings were emitted by a stale bundle compiled before that day's work, and I attributed them to whichever page I happened to be looking at. **Checked properly this time.** The one place that catches this warning is a script in `<head>`, because React deduplicates it per key for the life of a runtime — it fires on the first load and never again, so every hook installed after mount is too late. With such a probe injected through `layout.tsx`, all 22 workspace sections plus `/charts-3d` and `/gesture-check` were loaded in full and reported **zero**. The probe was reverted. **What replaces it:** `tests/setup.ts` now fails any test whose render produces a duplicate-key warning, alongside the render-crash guard already there. In a test each render is a fresh runtime, so the warning has nowhere to hide and it names the test that produced it — the hour this cost by hand becomes a line of output. Proven by reproducing the exact shape: keying the column table by `semantic_type`, where three columns share `continuous`, fails immediately. The fixture that missed it first time had a single column, and now has two, because one row cannot collide with anything. |
| D192 | **Two tests were written so that they could not fail, and both were hiding something.** `assert set(OPTIONAL_FORMATS) - set(readable_suffixes()) or True` sat under a comment claiming it checked that the code treats optional formats as optional; it checked nothing, and the contract that holds on every machine — a format is readable exactly when its package is installed — had never been tested. `assert interface.resolve("/workspace") is not None or True` was the precondition of a test about unreadable directories; **repairing it failed immediately**, because that fixture's bundle contained only `index.html` and never `workspace.html`, so `/workspace` did not resolve before the `chmod` either — both assertions after it had been true for a reason unrelated to permissions, and the case the test is named for had never once run. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by scanning, and the scan is the more useful half of this row: it also reported **ten** tests with no `assert` at all, of which **eight were perfectly good** — `signing.verify(manifest, public)  # raises if not`, `raw.decode("ascii")`, a monkeypatched stub that raises when called. "No assertion" is not "cannot fail". So the guard that shipped is deliberately narrower than the scan that found the bugs: `test_no_test_is_written_so_it_cannot_fail.py` and its interface counterpart report only a truthy constant in an assertion or an `or` with one in it — shapes with no judgement call available — because a guard with an 80% false alarm rate gets suppressed rather than obeyed. Both guards hold their own detection open with a test, since a silent scanner and a clean codebase look identical from outside. The interface side was already clean. |

| D059 | **The Windows launcher opened a window and closed it again, with no message.** `Throughline.bat` ran and vanished — the hardest failure to report, and the cause was a comment. `rem` in cmd is **not** an inert comment: the parser still expands `%` substitutions on the line. A `rem` explaining that *"`%~dp` on a URL yields a filesystem path"* contained a bare `%~dp` — a parameter operator with no argument number — which is a **fatal syntax error**, not an ignored one. `%~dp0` is fine; `%~dp` ends the script. **Written by me while fixing D050**, and it survived because no test runs a batch file and CI cannot reach Windows (D034). | high | done | Claude (Fenil) | Diagnosed by running the downloaded `.bat` on real Windows through WSL interop, which printed *"The following usage of the path operator in batch-parameter substitution is invalid"* — invisible on a double-click because the window closes on the error. After the fix, the same file downloaded from the live host installed `1caf2a0`, verified the checksum, applied all 39 migrations and reached package installation. Evidence: `test_no_comment_in_the_batch_file_contains_an_invalid_substitution`, **mutation-checked** by restoring the comment. It requires every `%~` to be followed by an argument number, anywhere in the file, comments included. |
| D060 | **The one-liner cannot be universal, and the page implied it was.** Pasted into PowerShell it fails with *The term 'sh' is not recognized* — a fair thing to try, since the page offered one line and never said which shells it was for. There is no `sh` on a stock Windows and no single literal line can span both families. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | Added `scripts/install.ps1`, so the shape is universal even though the line is not: `curl ... | sh` for macOS/Linux/WSL, `irm ... | iex` for PowerShell — the pair rustup, deno and uv all ship. It is thin by design: it finds a Python and hands over to `install.py`, the one implementation of download-verify-unpack, rather than becoming a third copy of the install sequence. **Driven on real Windows against the live host**: fetched the installer, verified the checksum, installed `1caf2a0`. That run also caught a genuine PowerShell bug — `& $Python` where `$Python` was the array `@('py','-3')` makes PowerShell look for a command literally named *"py -3"*; the arguments have to be splatted. Guessed wrong, measured right. Evidence: `test_both_shell_families_get_a_one_liner`, `test_the_powershell_installer_is_published`, and the User-Agent guard extended to PowerShell's `-UserAgent` idiom. |
