Nothing conflicts textually because the rows are different lines, which is exactly the failure this ledger already recorded once for D010 and D011. An id is how a commit message, a code comment and another row point at a finding, so two meanings for D203 makes every reference to it ambiguous. | high | open | — | Found 2026-09-06. The four on `main` are D026, D027, D028 and D029, each appearing twice in this table — one batch of four rows followed immediately by a second batch of four. The eighteen on the branch are visible in `git diff origin/main origin/study-context-and-the-checks-that-needed-it -- TASKS.md`. **The protocol's rule decides the branch case:** whoever has not landed renumbers, because the rows on `main` are the ones already there — so that branch's D200-D319 shift above `main`'s highest id at merge time. **The four on `main` are the harder half** and are left for the owner to decide: renumbering them now would break any commit or comment that already points at them, so the choice is between living with the ambiguity and renumbering the later batch with a pointer note. Not fixed here because it is not one session's to settle. `.claude/skills/ledger/SKILL.md` already carries the rule that would have prevented both. **Merged and verified 2026-09-06:** CI run 34071130030, dispatched on `main` after the merge, is green on every job — web interface, Windows sandbox, Docker build, macOS and Ubuntu Python. **Resolved on merge, 2026-09-06.** The branch's side was renumbered, as the protocol requires of the side that has not landed: its D194-D319 became **D219-D344** (add 25), and the references inside those rows moved with them. Ids at or below D193 were shared history and did not move. So a note or commit message written on that branch before the merge points at a number 25 below the row it means. The four duplicated ids on `main` — D026, D027, D028, D029 — are **not** renumbered and remain ambiguous: they are both already on `main`, where the protocol's rule has nothing to say, and renumbering either would break every reference that already points at it. |
| D225 | **`preflight` granted itself the exemption it exists to verify, so a security guard failed only when the project checked itself.** `manage.py main()` sets `THROUGHLINE_ALLOW_INSTALLED_HOME` because the program genuinely is the installed product when it runs `dev`, `doctor` or `backup`. `preflight` is a subcommand of that program: it inherited the grant, handed it to pytest, and pytest handed it to the subprocess in `test_a_stray_script_cannot_open_real_data.py` — the D116 guard proving an unnamed home is refused, running with the refusal switched off. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by running `preflight --full`, which I had not done while changing a great deal. The failure mode is the dangerous part: the two tests passed under a bare `pytest` and failed only under `preflight`, which reads as flakiness — and a security guard believed to be flaky is one that gets deleted rather than fixed. **Fixed in two places, because either alone leaves a hole.** Preflight now strips the allowance from every check's environment (`env=env or None` inherited it, allowance and all), so no test runs under an exemption the product grants itself; and the guard strips it in its own `_run`, so it cannot be defeated by whoever started the run. The variable is named once in `manage.py` and imported from `db.py` in the test, because a grant and a strip spelled differently would leave the grant in place and every test still passing. Two new tests pin it; all three mutations fail. |
| D226 | **The README claimed more web tests than exist.** 2647 claimed, 2656 `it(` blocks written — and parametrised blocks only expand upward, so the real count cannot be lower. Mine: I added tests across several rounds and never updated the count. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Corrected to the measured numbers rather than estimated ones — `pytest --collect-only` reports 2487, `vitest run` reports 2907. The backend figure was also stale, understating by 222; the guard tolerates understatement ("overstating is false, not stale") but there is no reason to be wrong in either direction. Mutation-tested by restoring the old figure, which fails. |
| D246 | **The flat-image panel rebuilt every pixel on every frame of a drag — my own defect, from D219.** The window mapping sat inside the frame loop: each dirty frame allocated a canvas, allocated an `ImageData`, and walked every pixel. Panning and zooming both mark the panel dirty, and the loop's dependency array included the pan, so it was also torn down and rebuilt on every pointer move. On a 2048-square micrograph that is four million iterations and two allocations per frame while somebody drags — a freeze, not a slow frame. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | **Nothing in that work depends on where the image sits**: a pan moves the picture and changes no pixel's value. The mapping is now `windowedBytes` in `lib/imaging/raster.ts`, computed when the pixels or the window change and blitted once per frame; the loop reads the pan and zoom from a ref, so moving the image no longer restarts it, and the offscreen buffer is reused rather than reallocated. Found by looking for the one performance defect this codebase has hit before — expensive work in a render path — after many rounds spent only on accuracy. |
| D247 | **The window arithmetic had no test, and it is the calculation a reader's judgement rests on.** Applied wrongly, a faint structure disappears or a flat field looks like signal. It was untestable while it lived inside a frame loop; pulling it out for D246 made it a pure function. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Nine tests: the window centre lands on mid grey, its ends on black and white, values outside it clamp rather than wrap — a wrapped value renders a bright structure dark and a reader takes that for absence — narrowing raises contrast, no pixel is left transparent, and a sixteen-bit image is not rendered black at its own full window, which is the `rgbaFrom` mapping added in D220. **Two of my own mistakes surfaced here.** A colour test expected identity and was simply wrong about the arithmetic: a window of width 256 centred on 128 covers [0, 256], so 240 correctly maps to 239 — the assertion now checks that every channel follows the *same* rule, which is the claim actually being made. And a surviving mutation showed the hand-written clamp was redundant, `Uint8ClampedArray` clamping on assignment; it is gone, with the guarantee recorded where it happens. |
| D248 | **A catalogue entry generated a whole example — a 24³ voxel grid, or a full force relaxation — as a null check, and the browser did it again on every render.** `sharesPictureWith` called `exampleFor(entry)` and used the result for nothing but `if (!data) return []`, and `CatalogueChart` called it unmemoized one line below a memoized `exampleFor` for the same entry. The file's own header records fixing this exact cost for the other call and missed this one. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | `isDrawable` answers the same question by lookup, and the equivalence between them was already pinned by "draws nothing it cannot draw", so the swap needed no new claim. The cost is now pinned relatively rather than in milliseconds — a whole sweep of the catalogue must cost under half of one round of example generation, which is exactly what the old implementation paid for a single sweep. A millisecond bound would say different things on different machines; the old code is the honest baseline. Measured 23ms → 1.7ms for the sweep. Mutation-tested by restoring the old guard. |
| D249 | **The memoization I added was a crash, and the whole 3D suite could not see it.** `CatalogueChart` returns early for an entry nothing can draw, and I put the new `useMemo` below that return — so the two paths through the component call different numbers of hooks. React throws "Rendered more hooks than during the previous render" the moment a reader clicks from an undrawable entry to a drawable one in the same element position, which is exactly what `CatalogueBrowser` does. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Caught by lint under `preflight --full`, not by 3052 tests: every other 3D test renders one entry and stops, so no test had ever crossed the boundary. Hoisted above the early return, and the crossing is now a test in both directions — both fail with the hook back below the return, with the two different React errors. The lesson is the one this session keeps re-learning: the suite is evidence about what it exercises, and a component test that mounts once cannot see a state machine. |
| D250 | **The fitted surface re-projected itself on every render of a real research figure.** `Surface` builds the fit and the observations into one shared cube in a memo keyed on the `grid` and `observations` references. `figures.tsx` — the path a researcher's own analysis takes — constructed both inline in the JSX, so every render handed it a new pair and re-ran the whole projection: every cell spanned, every observation placed, the colour ramp rebuilt. At real grid sizes, not the 3×3 the tests use. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Both are memoized on `points.data` now, and hoisted above the component's early returns so they are not the D249 mistake again. The existing fidelity guard — the browser must take the grid from the server rather than evaluate the fitted response itself — read the JSX branch, which after this change contains only `surfaceGrid`, so it would have passed whatever the memo did; it now reads the memo, and additionally requires the branch to pass both references through rather than build its own. Both halves mutation-tested. |
| D251 | **A missing dependency was hidden by a second defect, and finding the second one is what made the first real.** `Surface`'s projection memo reads `colourBy` throughout — the fourth variable, the legend, the shape-mismatch flag — and did not list it, so changing the colour grid over the same surface kept the old colouring and the old legend: a picture claiming one quantity and painting another, which is the failure the mismatch check in that same memo exists to refuse. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The first test I wrote for it passed with the dependency removed. The reason was `observations = []` in the parameter list — a fresh array evaluated on every render, so the memo never cached and nothing could go stale. One shared `NO_OBSERVATIONS` constant fixes that, and only then does dropping `colourBy` fail. A vacuous test that would have shipped as proof; the coupling between the two fixes is recorded in the test, because reverting the default would silently make it vacuous again rather than fail. Two more of the same class were checked — `labels = {}` in `ResultCard` and `CommandPalette` — and neither feeds a hook, so neither was touched. |
| D252 | **A suppression that suppressed nothing.** `useLayout` deliberately keys its layout memo on a content signature rather than the graph object, and said so in a two-line `eslint-disable-next-line` comment — which covered the comment's own second line and nothing else. ESLint printed both the missing-dependency warning and an "unused directive" for the comment meant to silence it, and had done for as long as the comment existed. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The directive has to be the single line immediately above the dependency array, which is where `exhaustive-deps` reports. The argued reason moved into the block comment above the hook, where it is not load-bearing on ESLint's line arithmetic. Worth recording because the failure mode is invisible in the direction that matters: the warning it was meant to hide stayed, and the noise trained the eye past a lint output that later turned out to be carrying a real crash (D249). |
| D253 | **A wrong dependency array did not fail the suite — it hung it.** `Cartesian` publishes its brushed region from an effect and depends on `linked.select` rather than on `linked`, which is exactly what `exhaustive-deps` asks it to change. Making that change span for ever: `select` built `new Set(ids)` unconditionally, so republishing an identical selection produced a new object, a new context value, another render and another publish. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | A hang is a worse failure than a red test — it stops CI without saying why, and it is the one failure nobody can read from a log — so the cycle is closed in the provider where no caller can reopen it: an equal selection now returns the state it was given and React's bail-out applies. The same mutation that used to spin for ever now fails in 907ms with four red tests, which is a bug report. The narrow dependency stays, because widening it is still wrong behaviourally, and now carries the argued reason and a suppression placed where `exhaustive-deps` actually reports (D252). Two mutations — an unconditional `new Set`, and comparing selections by size alone — are both caught. |
| D254 | **Every way the fragility endpoint can say no produced the same sentence, and it was a claim about the researcher's own method.** The endpoint is careful: 422 when the estimate cannot be converted to a risk ratio honestly, 409 when no analysis has completed or the run recorded no estimate, 404 when the connection is gone — each with a sentence written for a person. The panel folded all of them, plus 5xx and a dropped network, into "this connection's method is not one it can convert to a risk ratio honestly". | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | So a run that had simply not finished was told its *method* was the problem, and a server that was down said the same. The panel now branches on what the server actually said: 422 keeps the sentence written for it, the other refusals carry the server's own words (§104), and anything else is a failure with the retry the rest of the product offers — a refusal must not offer one, because a retry that cannot succeed reads as a system that is merely broken. **The existing test's fixture was unfaithful**: it rejected with a bare `Error`, which no path in the product produces — `api.get` throws `ApiError` for every response it gets — and that is why the collapse had never shown. Fixture corrected, 409 and 500 covered, all three fail on the collapsed condition. |
| D255 | **Three ARIA attributes were set on an element whose role does not support them, so a screen reader was told nothing while the sighted reader watched a list open under the caret.** The notebook's link suggester drives its popup from the `<textarea>` — correctly, because focus must stay with the caret — using `aria-expanded`, `aria-controls` and `aria-activedescendant`. A `<textarea>` is implicitly a `textbox`, and `textbox` supports none of them. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | `role="combobox"` is what makes them mean anything. Two existing tests asserted all three attributes and passed throughout, because an attribute that is present is not an attribute that is honoured — the same shape as the vacuous test caught in D251. The new test queries by role rather than by attribute, so dropping the role while leaving the attributes fails it. Reported by `jsx-a11y` for as long as it existed, in a lint output that had also been carrying a genuine crash (D249); with this the interface lints clean, warnings included, for the first time. |
| D256 | **Two hook warnings judged individually rather than silenced together, and a dead import that turned out to be evidence.** `workspace/page.tsx` omitted `setSection`, which is a permanently stable `useCallback` — so it was added, not suppressed, because a suppression is a claim that needs an argument and adding a stable value costs nothing. `Globe3D` listed `controllerRef`, the ref its handle attaches *to* rather than anything the handle reads; removed. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The unused `Empty` in `cohorts.tsx` was checked before deletion in case it meant the screen had no empty state: it has a bespoke one that names what a subset is and offers the form, which is better than the generic primitive, so the import really was dead. The unused `Failure` in `fragility.tsx` was the opposite — it was unused because the error path had been folded away, which is what led to D254. A dead import is worth reading before it is deleted. |
| D257 | **The section that explains an evidence grade returned nothing when it could not read the checks — the same nothing it shows when every assumption held.** §47 grades evidence from the assumptions a method needed rather than from the p-value, so an association can read r = 0.90 at q = 5e-66 and still be graded weak. The component's own docstring calls that the screen's most confusing moment and says the grade is never shown without the checks that produced it. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | `if (error \|\| !data) return null` made "could not be read" and "nothing was violated" the same empty space, and they are opposite answers: one says the assumptions held, the other says nobody knows. A failed request therefore left the screen looking exactly as it does when a method's assumptions were all satisfied. Now the failure is stated where the explanation would be, with the retry — `!data` without an error stays silent, which is the state before a request exists for a run that does not. Three tests: the failure, the true silence, and a violated assumption still explained; the collapsed condition fails the first. |
| D258 | **A failed request for the excerpt board was turned into an empty board, and the board is only rendered when it has entries — so it vanished.** `.catch(() => setKept([]))`. The comment directly above that line records why the board is loaded from the server at all: as React state it emptied on navigation, which made §205 "a demonstration rather than a place to put anything". A failed load re-created exactly that, silently. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | A researcher who had taken excerpts saw no board and no reason for its absence, and the obvious next move is to take the same excerpt again — so the failure mode is duplicated work on top of a false statement. The error is now kept and said where the board would be, because that is where a reader looks for what they have taken, with a retry that re-runs the load. Two tests, and the fixture needed `disagreements` on the paper — omitted, the result row takes the whole render down before any assertion runs, which is the trap `connectiondetail`'s fixture already records for `assumption_checks`. |
| D259 | **Silence for "this source is not a database" also covered every way the request could fail.** `databasetables.tsx` renders nothing when the tables cannot be listed, and argues for it: the panel sits under every source page and the usual reason is that the source is not a database, where a banner would be noise a reader learns to ignore. The argument is right and covered too much. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The endpoint separates them — 400 for a file it cannot read as a database, 404 for a source not in this project, and neither for a 5xx or a dropped connection, where the tables may well be there and nobody found out. Silence for the first two, stated failure with a retry for the rest. **The existing test's fixture rejected with a bare `Error`**, which no path in the product produces, and is the third fixture this round found to be unfaithful in the same way (D254): `api.get` throws `ApiError` for every response it hears back, so a bare `Error` exercises only the transport case and silently skips every status-dependent branch. |
| D260 | **The link suggester told a researcher a page did not exist when nothing had found out, and the obvious next step splits a note in two.** `notebook.tsx` matched `[[` queries against a list of page titles loaded once, and `.catch(() => setTargets([]))` made a failed load indistinguishable from a notebook with nothing in it. Every query then matched nothing, and the empty state says "Nothing called '<query>' yet — finish the link and it becomes a page waiting to be written." | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | A missing suggestion is a small loss; acting on a false absence writes a second page for a note that already exists, and the two then drift. The failure is remembered and the sentence changes to say the list could not be loaded and that this does not mean no such page exists. Two tests hold both directions, because "nothing called that yet" has to keep meaning that — which it only does while the case where nobody found out reads differently. |
| D261 | **A failed load of a paper's existing marks showed the paper with none of them.** `PaperReader` catches the marks request and empties the overlay, arguing — rightly — that ink which could not be fetched is not worth refusing the page over. The argument covers the banner and not the silence: an empty overlay is exactly what a paper with no marks looks like. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Two concrete hazards follow, not just a false impression: the same annotations get drawn again, and rubbing out cannot reach an existing mark because `marks` no longer holds it to be found. Said through the same non-blocking `role="status"` line the save path already uses — which argues the opposite direction, that a mark believed saved and not saved is the worst outcome. Not knowing what is on the page is the same error. **Tested by reading the source**, which is weaker evidence and is the evidence available: `PaperReader` needs pdf.js and a 2D canvas context and happy-dom provides neither, which is why the rest of that file tests exported geometry. The reason is recorded in the test so a source assertion is not mistaken for a preference. My first version of that test sliced from the front and got an empty string — the effect's cleanup line appears earlier in the file — so both assertions would have been vacuous; the slice now searches the end anchor from the start, and asserts the slice is non-empty. |
| D262 | **"Asking four repositories" — the same sentence, on a second screen, that had already gone wrong once.** The Find papers header said it searched four sources while searching ten, and the repair there was structural: the count is taken from the same capabilities the chips are built from, so adding a connector changes the sentence and the chips together or neither. `datasearch.tsx` had the identical hardcoded count and was left alone. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | It is true today — there are four dataset connectors — which is exactly what the literature header's tests say makes it dangerous: "a hardcoded list is exactly what looks fine in review". Now counted from the list. Where that list's own request failed the sentence drops the number rather than inventing one, because a count guessed there is the same false precision in a new place. Fixed before it rotted rather than after, which is the point of naming a defect class. |
| D263 | **The account list rendered nobody when it could not be read, under a sentence saying it was everybody.** `settings.tsx` maps over `people?.`, and a failed request sets `people` to `null` — which draws no rows directly beneath "Everyone with an account on this installation. | D264 | **The reachability guard walks the import graph, so a feature reached only through a prop nobody passes reads as reachable.** `charts/linked.tsx` is imported by `Cartesian`, which every figure screen renders, and every line of it is dead: no page wraps anything in `<LinkedCharts>` and no chart is ever given a `linkKey`, so the provider, the brushing, the echo counts and the muting reach no researcher. A guard that accuses correct code is one people delete, which costs more than the blind spot; the limitation is recorded in the file rather than papered over. |

Rendered through the parent, the same mutation fails. |
| D266 | **A defect I reported and withdrew: the import note is not discarded.** I read `setImported(… body.note)` beside a cell rendering the word "Imported" and concluded the server's sentence — "<n> rows are being profiled as a dataset of their own" — was being thrown away for a word claiming a completion the server had not claimed. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | It is not. Reverted, and recorded because the reasoning that produced it was the same shape as four real findings this round and was still wrong — the cell and the note are one screen, and reading a component top-to-bottom is what made two lines look like the whole answer. |
| D267 | **A PDF page whose columns could not be told apart was read straight across, silently, under metadata that said the columns had been detected.** `_page_blocks_in_reading_order` reads column by column for one reason, in its own words: a whole-page read interleaves a two-column layout and splices unrelated sentences together, "which destroys the verbatim text every citation depends on". When the block extraction itself raises there is nothing to detect a split with, so the fallback *is* that whole-page read. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The fallback stays — losing the page is worse — but a spliced sentence is indistinguishable from a real one and this product quotes passages verbatim for citation, so it must not be silent. The affected pages are collected, logged the way the table extraction ten lines below already logs its own failure, and recorded on the parse; `"columns": "detected"` was stated unconditionally *including for the pages where detection is exactly what failed*, and now says which happened and names the pages, because "somewhere in this paper" is not something anybody can act on. |
| D268 | **The fact was recorded, stored, and stopped at the database.** `store_paper` has always written the parse metadata to `papers.metadata`, and the source route selected `id, title, page_count, object_id` — every column except that one. So the single fact bearing on whether a quotation from a paper is verbatim reached no researcher, and nothing could tell a paper read cleanly from one read straight across. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Carried end to end rather than left in a column, because recording it where no screen reads it is the same built-and-unreachable defect this session has been closing: the route sends it, the client type declares it, and the source page says it beside the injection-signal notice it mirrors — a fact about the source, shown because it is one, with the pages named and every other page said to be unaffected. **Pinned at all three layers, and the middle one needed its own test**: the web tests mock `api.get` and the parser tests never reach HTTP, so removing the column from the `SELECT` passed both — the same gap as D265, found by looking for it this time rather than by a surviving mutation. |
| D269 | **`columns` is a PyMuPDF fact, and my first API test asserted it unconditionally.** It failed on this machine because Docling read the paper — the structured parser handles layout itself and records no such key. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Fixed by asserting the parser first and the column claim only where it applies. Worth recording because the test would have passed on a machine without Docling installed and failed on one with it, which makes it a report on the machine rather than on the product — and `test_which_parser_read_the_paper.py` exists precisely because that difference had once been invisible. It also bounds the fix honestly: the splicing notice can only ever appear for PyMuPDF-read papers, and whether Docling has an equivalent failure is not something I have evidence about either way. |
| D270 | **D222 has been closed for some time and I kept reporting it as open.** The `whose.ts` wiring no longer overrides anything: the exemption it contradicted is gone from the reachability guard, the module is imported by `SpatialControl`, and the guard passes. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The remaining caveat is not a defect but a documented state: the gesture chain is driven in tests by a stubbed tracker, and T019 already records that MediaPipe is deliberately not wired, so no camera has met any of it. Recorded because a stale open item repeated across several reports is its own small false claim, and I made it four times. |
| D271 | **The consistency checker ranked lifecycle stages against a list that had drifted from the state machine, and both directions of the drift produced false verdicts.** `_STAGE_ORDER` listed `observed` and `deprecated`, which no transition in `CONNECTION_PROMOTION` can produce, and omitted `rejected`, a terminal state every connection can reach. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The phantom rung put `exploratory` and `validated` — **one legal promotion apart** — two apart, so comparing them returned F8 "different lifecycle stage": two adjacent results reported as a hypothesis being read against a conclusion. `rejected` ranked -1, below `candidate`, so two rejected results that agreed returned F9 "consistent, but both exploratory", whose caveat says neither has been through robustness checks — a rejected result has been through them and been thrown out. The ladder is now the four real strength stages; `conflicted` and `rejected` are named as deliberately off it, because a disagreement is not a *strength* of evidence and `conflicted` had been ranked above `replicated`, reading a disagreement as the strongest evidence in the system. A test asserts the ladder and the state machine describe the same statuses, so neither can move alone. |
| D272 | **The test that would have caught it demonstrated adjacency using a stage that cannot exist.** `test_adjacent_lifecycle_stages_are_still_comparable` paired `exploratory` with `observed` — and `observed` is producible by no transition — so the one test aimed at this property was asserting it on a value the product cannot reach. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | It passed throughout the drift, and it would have gone on passing after the fix for a *different* reason: `observed` is now unranked, so F8 is skipped rather than not-triggered. Repaired to `validated` and `replicated`, which are adjacent and both real. This is the fourth unfaithful fixture this session — after three `Error`-instead-of-`ApiError` mocks — and the same shape each time: a test whose inputs the product cannot produce proves nothing about the product, while looking like coverage of exactly the thing that is broken. |
| D273 | **The silence that let it drift, closed at runtime as well as in the guard.** `rejected` was added to the state machine and ranked -1 here for as long as it existed, with nothing said. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | `_stage_rank` now warns when a status is on neither the ladder nor the list of statuses deliberately off it. The guard test catches the two lists disagreeing in this repository; the log catches a database written by a version that had a status this one has never heard of, which no test of two constants can see. It also makes `_OFF_THE_LADDER` load-bearing in production rather than a constant only a test reads — which would have been the same reached-only-by-a-test shape recorded in D264. |
| D274 | **The note that leaves the building had plain-language wording for a vocabulary that is not the finding lifecycle.** `library_note` renders a finding for somebody's reference manager — "a reader in Zotero has no glossary", in its own words. Its lifecycle dictionary carried `confirmed`, `refuted`, `superseded` and `retracted`, none of which any finding can hold: `refuted` belongs to `ClaimStatus` and `superseded` to artifact staleness. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Meanwhile `replicated`, `conflicted` and `deprecated` — three states findings really do reach — had no wording at all, so the strongest state in the system exported as "replicated — an internal status with no plain-language equivalent recorded". The fallback is honest, which is why nothing looked broken; it was simply firing for half the real vocabulary while four entries could never fire at all. Both directions are now asserted against `FindingLifecycle`. |
| D275 | **The same drift in the field this product is most careful about, and one of the wrong keys was a near-miss for a real one.** `CausalStatus` has six values. The note described `associational` — the real value is `association_only` — and `causal_refuted`, which is not a value at all, while `temporally_consistent`, `possible_causal` and `insufficient_evidence` had no wording. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Those three are exactly the qualified readings the note exists to convey, and they left for a colleague's reference manager as "Recorded as possible_causal, which has no plain-language equivalent here" — the association-is-not-causation distinction this product is built around, dropped at the one point where it travels to someone with no access to the record. `associational` is the reason it survived review: it reads correct at a glance. Each new sentence says what was established and stops — `temporally_consistent` rules out one alternative and establishes nothing by itself; `insufficient_evidence` is stated as not being a finding of no effect. Understating costs a reader strength they were owed; overstating is the failure the product exists against. Asserted against the enum in both directions, both mutation-tested. |
| D276 | **Three vocabularies checked and found sound, recorded so they are not re-investigated.** `critic._CAUSAL_LANGUAGE`'s allowance of `{causal_supported, possible_causal}` is a policy set rather than a coverage map — both values are real and the subset is argued, so it is not drift. `consistency._STAGE_ORDER` and the connection state machine now agree (D271). The dataset-connector status map builds itself from `DATASET_CONNECTORS`. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Recorded because two of the three look exactly like the pair that was broken, and the difference — a *policy* subset versus a map that must be total — is checkable rather than a matter of taste. The distinguishing question: does a missing key change what a reader is told, or only what a rule permits? Only the first kind needs to be exhaustive. |
| D277 | **The person receiving the citation was told more about causality than the person who made the finding.** `causal_status` has been on every finding since the beginning — `evidence_graph` sends it with `SELECT *` — and exactly one screen in the whole client read it: the findings *list*, as a bare token, "causal status: possible causal". The finding a researcher opens to decide what it establishes said nothing about cause at all, while the library note exported to somebody else's reference manager carried a full sentence about it (D275). | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Association is not causation is the claim this product is built on, and it was strongest in the artifact that leaves the building and absent from the screen where the judgement is made. Now shown on the finding, above the limitations — cause qualifies what the finding *is*, the caveats qualify what it *covers*, and read in the other order the causal sentence looks like one more caveat. Always present, including "not assessed", because that is the case a silent screen most invites a reader to assume away. |
| D278 | **The sentence is served, not written in the client, and that was the design decision worth making carefully.** Rendering it in TypeScript would have been quicker and would have created a third copy of a vocabulary that had already drifted twice in two places. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | `library_note.causal_sentence` is now public and is the single source: the exported note and the researcher's screen read the same dictionary, and a backend test asserts the payload's sentence *is* `library_note.causal_sentence(...)` rather than merely resembling it — so the two cannot diverge without failing. This also satisfies §104 the way D254 and D268 did: the server's own words reach the researcher. The client keeps a presence guard on the field, which the backend test pins by asserting it is always sent, including for an unassessed finding. |
| D279 | **A fifth unfaithful fixture, in the test file for the sibling feature.** `a-finding-says-what-it-does-not-establish.test.tsx` built its graph with `causal_status: "associational"` — the same near-miss that had been sitting in the library note's vocabulary for a status really called `association_only`. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Corrected. Recorded because the count now matters more than any one instance: three `Error`-instead-of-`ApiError` mocks, an `observed` lifecycle no transition can produce, and now this. Every one of them was a value the product cannot generate, sitting in a test that looked like coverage of the thing that was broken. The near-miss spellings are the dangerous kind — `associational` and `observed` both read as correct at a glance, which is why review never caught either. |
| D280 | **The unfaithful fixture became a guard, and the guard's first act was to catch a miss of mine.** Five instances had been found by hand in one session; the sixth was found in the first run of the scan — a second `causal_status: "associational"` in the same file whose other copy I had corrected an hour earlier, in the list fixture rather than the graph fixture. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The check reads literal assignments to the two fields whose vocabularies are closed enums and compares them against the enums. **Validated by reintroducing both known instances** — `lifecycle_status="observed"` and `causal_status="associational"` — and confirming it names each with its file and line, rather than by trusting that it passes today. Deliberately-unrecognised values are exempted by name with an argument, and a second test fails if an exemption stops describing a real fixture, so the list cannot quietly become permission to ignore anything. |
| D281 | **What that guard deliberately does not do, written down so it is not mistaken for more than it is.** `lifecycle_status` names two vocabularies — findings and connections have separate state machines — and the field alone does not say which object a fixture describes. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | So it checks the union, which costs it the ability to notice a *finding* fixture using `rejected`, a real connection status, and keeps it free of false accusations — the failure mode that gets a guard deleted, and the one my own prop-reachability scan hit twice in D264. It reads string literals only, so a status built any other way is invisible, and it says nothing about the shape of a mocked exception, which is the `ApiError` half of this class and needs a different instrument. It would have caught three of the five known instances. Stated in the file, because a narrow guard described as a general one is its own false claim. |
| D282 | **The mocked-exception half of the fixture class, closed with the instrument D281 said was missing — and the honest result is that there was nothing left to fix.** Every component that branches on an error's status was found and read: four of them, and the two I had not already touched — `approvals.tsx` on 409 and `analyses.tsx` on 409 — were both already tested with a real `ApiError` carrying that status. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | So the bare-`Error` mocks that remain are all in tests that only assert a banner appears or a message is shown, where either kind of error behaves identically; the three that mattered were the three already fixed. Reported as a negative result rather than turned into work: a sweep that finds nothing is worth the same as one that finds something, provided the search was real, and the four call sites are named so the next person does not repeat it. |
| D283 | **A guard so the gap cannot reopen: a screen that tells statuses apart must be exercised with those statuses.** It reads literal three-digit comparisons on an error and requires the tests that import that component to construct an `ApiError` with each one. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | **Matched by import, not by filename** — test files here are named for the behaviour they pin, so `fragility.tsx` is tested by `how-fragile-is-this.test.tsx`, and a name-based mapping would have found nothing while looking thorough. A first assertion fails if the mapping ever resolves to nothing, because a guard whose scan comes back empty passes for the wrong reason. **Validated by restoring the D254 state** — the 422 fixture back to a bare `Error` — and confirming it names the file and the status, rather than by trusting a green run. Its limits are in the file: a branch on a status held in a constant is invisible to it. |
| D284 | **The read me understated the product by fifteen and seventeen, in the document somebody reads before anything else.** It claimed the registry names 238 visualizations and draws 199; it names 253 and draws 216. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | This is the defect the catalogue *page* was already repaired for, in the one place with no test. `catalogue-draws.test.tsx` opens by recording it — a headline number was an assertion in a data file with nothing behind it, and the repair was to *derive* the number rather than correct it — and the page derives it now, while this paragraph stayed typed out and drifted exactly as the page's had. The per-primitive breakdown had drifted too, so it is now the measured drawable count per primitive rather than a historical delta I cannot re-derive. Corrected **and** guarded: a read me cannot derive anything at runtime, so a test does it, checking both figures and that the remainder adds up — a paragraph can be right twice and still not add up. Validated by restoring 238/199 and watching all three claims fail. |
| D285 | **D070's own row overstated the current state by more than twice, and three of its four examples were out of date.** It says thirty-seven server routes cannot be reached from the interface; the ledger in `test_routes_are_reachable.py` holds 17. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The board gained its delete, the visuals subsystem gained `savedfigures.tsx` and is down to one unreached route rather than four with no page at all, and workflow approvals are reachable — `approvals.tsx` calls them and handles the 409. | D286 | **Two things examined this round and deliberately not changed.** The library-note POST writes into Zotero and no screen calls it — but `librarynote.test.tsx` asserts the absence of any write control and the panel says why: the path has never run against a real library, and a reference library has no undo. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | An argued, tested decision, not a gap, and wiring it would have overridden a recorded one. The GET is called, so the note itself does reach the researcher — my first reading of the ledger entry had that backwards for a minute. Also checked and left: the remaining bare-`Error` mocks, all in tests that assert only that a banner appears. | D287 | **Two test files guarded on `numpy` and `pandas` with `importorskip`, which are hard dependencies of three packages.** A machine missing either cannot run this product at all — `research-domain`, `ingestion` and `visual-spec` all declare them — and the suite would have reported green while the numeric stack the whole analysis layer rests on was unusable. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Imported directly instead, so a missing one fails the run, which is the true answer. The `cv2` skip beside it is left alone and is correct: the digitise pack really is optional, and the difference between the two is the whole point. Same defect as the product ones this session — a failure rendered indistinguishable from a normal state — except in the instrument, where it is worse, because the instrument is what would otherwise have told you. 24 tests pass in 10s across both files. |
| D288 | **Two consecutive `preflight --full` runs failed on different random sets of tests, and neither was a regression.** Six files the first time, seven the second, none overlapping, each frozen for around 1,013,000 ms against a 5,000 ms timeout. All thirteen pass in isolation — 21 in 823 ms, then 362 in 3.9 s. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The cause is memory: `vm_stat` showed **67 MB free** and 1 GB of 2 GB swap in use, so `load average 16` with no process above 5% CPU meant workers blocked on paging rather than computing. Not a code fault and not one I can fix from the repository — Chrome alone held ~1.3 GB. **Recorded because the failure is indistinguishable from a real regression at a glance**, which is this project's whole subject: a run that says FAILED on seven unrelated files is telling the truth about the machine and a lie about the code. The check is `uptime` plus `vm_stat`, then rerun the named files alone. |
| D289 | **My own waiters caused the first of those two failures.** I had been waiting on background tasks with `until grep -q "EXIT=" "$f"; do :; done`, which spins a full core for the whole wait; several alive at once starved the run. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | An empty loop body never yields. `sleep` inside the until-loop is the sanctioned form, and polling the task's output file with a read costs nothing at all. Also cleared an orphaned `pytest` holding the database for 1 h 38 m — the same orphan hazard as earlier in this session, and the reason two test files appeared to take 15 minutes when they take 10 seconds. Recorded as a defect rather than a note because it produced a false red run that I nearly reported as one. |
| D290 | **My own guard from D284 would have failed on a rewrap.** It asserted the read me's remainder sentence against the line breaks as they fall today — `The\nremaining 37 need somebody else's reader` — so reflowing the paragraph would have turned it red for a reason unrelated to whether the claim is true. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | And the paragraph invites exactly that edit: correcting a number changes its width, which is how it gets rewrapped in the first place. Whitespace is collapsed before matching now, so all four assertions survive reflowing and still fail on a wrong number — re-validated by setting the count to 999. Found by re-reading my own work rather than by a failure, which is the only way this one surfaces: a guard that cries wolf is silenced the first time somebody is in a hurry, and it would have been silenced for being right about nothing. |
| D291 | **Five areas swept this round and found sound, recorded so they are not re-swept.** The model layer: `DEFAULT_MODEL` is a current identifier, `max_tokens` is documented correctly as bounding thinking and answer together, the untrusted-data fence is present in *both* providers, `strip_reasoning` is applied in both, and `NullProvider` refuses loudly rather than degrading — its docstring states the principle this session has spent itself applying. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Ollama's cloud-model hazard is already handled: `:cloud` tags are detected per model, a remote host is covered separately, and the docstring quotes the README guarantee it protects. The workers' two broad handlers are argued, and the one that swallows logs, with the deadlock reasoning written out. `docs/REQUIREMENTS.md`'s summary table is accurate — my first count made all four statuses look one too high, which was my grep counting the legend rows, not a drift. **Four of the five needed the instrument checked before the finding**, which is the same lesson as D281 and D264. |
| D292 | **The log redaction matched whole key names, so it dropped `api_key` and kept `zotero_api_key`.** `_REDACT` is compared with `key.lower() in _REDACT` — exact membership — and a compound name is the ordinary shape: the Zotero route already takes a field called `api_key`, which becomes `zotero_api_key` the moment anybody logs which service it belonged to. `access_token`, `refresh_token`, `client_secret` and `user_passphrase` all passed through in the clear. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Matched as substrings now, with `credential`, `passphrase` and `private_key` added. **Latent rather than live, and said so**: `observability.log` — the only thing that fills structured context — is called by nothing today, so no secret has been leaking; this is the first caller's defect, which is exactly when it is cheap. The existing tests passed throughout because every fixture used the one spelling that already worked (`password`, `token`, `session`, `content`) — the unfaithful-fixture pattern again, in the security guard. |
| D293 | **A withheld field now says it was withheld, which extends a recorded decision rather than reversing it.** `test_secrets_are_dropped_from_log_context` argues that dropping beats masking because "a masked value still records that one existed and how long it was". | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The length is the part that argument protects, and a key name carries neither the value nor its size — so `redacted: ["api_key"]` lists names only, never values, and is absent entirely when nothing was dropped, because an empty list would read as "something was hidden here". The reason it is needed is my own change: a substring rule deliberately over-reaches — it catches `content_type` along with `content` — and an operator who watches a field vanish with no explanation cannot tell that from a field nobody sent, so the over-match could never be noticed or argued with. Both halves mutation-tested; the tension with the recorded decision is written into the code rather than left for somebody to discover. |
| D294 | **A defect I reported to myself and withdrew before acting.** Eleven `extra={...}` call sites in the scientific runtime looked like structured logging the formatter silently discards — it reads only `record.context`, and I confirmed empirically that direct `extra` keys produce nothing. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | They are not logging calls. `extra` is a field of `StatisticalResult`, and `methods.py` imports no logger at all. Caught by reading one call site in context after the empirical test had already "confirmed" the behaviour — the test was correct about the formatter and irrelevant to the claim. Recorded because the check that saved it was one line of context, and because a true measurement of the wrong thing is the most convincing kind of false finding. |
| D295 | **A comparability verdict never said what it rested on, and the one number that says so was computed and shown to nobody.** `assess` returns `evidence` — `Math.min` of the two acquisitions' evidence strength — which is the honest measure of how much of the verdict came from file headers rather than somebody's recollection. It was returned in every assessment, asserted in one unit test, and read by no screen. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Each scan's own figure sits on its caption, so the screen showed "100% read from the file" beside "20%" with **DIRECTLY COMPARABLE** between them and left the researcher to work out that a comparison is only as good as its weaker side — which is the reasoning this screen exists to do for them. Now stated on the verdict itself, above the harmonisation steps because it qualifies the verdict rather than the work remaining, and **stated at 100% too**: a qualification that appears only when things are bad teaches a reader that its absence means nothing, when its absence would mean everything was read. Same shape as D236's limitations and D277's causal reading — a field that qualifies a claim, carried the whole way and shown to no one. |
| D296 | **Four checks on the comparability engine that came back sound, recorded so they are not repeated.** `compareAxis` returns `null` only for an axis that does not apply, never for a missing value — a missing value becomes a `not_stated` finding, so the "nothing known is not nothing wrong" guard sees it. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The `appliesTo` predicates fail safe in the right direction: `!bothCT` is true whenever a modality is unknown, so an unreadable header *keeps* the MRI axes rather than silently dropping them — the inversion would have been an unknown fact quietly removing a decisive axis and falling through to "directly comparable". A domain mismatch is refused before any axis is read, with the reason that comparing a micrograph on a CT's axes would report missing metadata as if it were a fact about the pair. And the Zotero key travels in a header, so no error message can carry it. Recorded because three of the four are the exact shapes this session has found broken elsewhere, and they are right here. |
| D297 | **The sweep I proposed after D295 found nothing, and the honest answer is not to build the guard I was about to build.** Three instances — D236's limitations, D277's causal reading, D295's evidence — looked like a habit of computing a qualification and forgetting to show it, so I swept every place that could repeat it. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The API-side baseline in `test_the_interface_and_the_api_agree.py` already names its qualification-shaped unread fields with arguments — `evidence_strength` on the *summary row*, where D236 established that a caveat has nowhere to sit. `Assessment`'s remaining unread fields are redundant rather than withheld: `blocking` builds the verdict prose inside the module, and the agreeing axes reach the panel through `findings`. Three instances, not a tendency. Recorded because I had already described it as a possible habit, and the evidence says otherwise. |
| D298 | **Three false positives in one scan, all from scoping the consumers wrongly — the third time this session.** `Field.dropped`, `Field.invalid`, `Bars.invalid`, `Paths.breaks` and `Paths.degenerate` are counts of data left out of a picture, and my scan reported that no screen reads them. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Every one is read by a `describe*` helper colocated in `lib/` and rendered into the chart's caption — with better prose than I was about to write: "the field is denser than it looks", "the line stops rather than crossing ground nothing was recorded on". The scan looked for readers in `components/` and `app/` only. **I had the fix half-written before checking `describeField`**, and it would have produced a second copy of a sentence already on screen — which is exactly how D266 happened with the import note. The pattern across all three scanner failures this session is the same: the instrument was right about what it measured and wrong about what that meant. |
| D299 | **A comment that had quietly stopped being true.** `Assessment.shared` is documented as "Axes that agree, named for the panel" and no panel reads it — no production code does; one test does. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | True of an earlier panel, and the current one renders `findings` instead, because a table listing only problems leaves a reader unable to tell "checked and fine" from "never looked at". The field is kept — a test asserting which axes agreed without walking the findings is a reasonable use — and the comment now says what is actually so, including that it is not displayed. Verified with the consumer scope that caught out D298: lib, components and app, not components alone. |
| D300 | **A row in the comparability panel contradicted its own note, and only the rendered page showed it.** The axes table printed `sequence weighting: not recorded | T2` with the note "Not recorded on both scans" beside it — the note is written whenever *either* side is silent, so it denied a value printed two columns to its left. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | A reader who trusts the note concludes the sequence is unknown on a scan where it is recorded; one who notices the disagreement decides the panel is unreliable. The two states are also different facts: a value present on one scan can be sought on the other, while a value absent from both is a question about the files rather than about this pair. Now "Recorded on one scan only, so the two cannot be compared on it" and "Not recorded on either scan". Both mutation-tested. |
| D301 | **Found by serving the static export and reading the page — the instrument this project's own record says keeps working, and the one I had not used all session.** D122's note ends "found by reading the rendered page rather than by the suite, which is now the sixth time"; this is the seventh. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | 3,092 web tests, including twenty-seven on this exact module, were green over a sentence that any reader of the panel would question — because every one of them asserts on values and verdicts, and none reads a note against the row it sits in. The defect is not in what the code computes; it is in what the words next to the number say, and that is the class a rendered page shows and an assertion does not. `apps/web/out` is already built by preflight, so serving it is one command and needs no backend for the client-side screens. |
| D302 | **The workspace showed a JavaScript parser error as its failure, directly above its own correct explanation of what was wrong.** With no API running, the panel printed "Unexpected token '<', \"<!DOCTYPE \"... is not valid JSON" — and underneath it, the note "The API is not answering. Start it with `./scripts/dev.sh`". `request` called `JSON.parse` unguarded, so a body that is not JSON threw a `SyntaxError` past every handler in the product. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | It sends a researcher to debug JSON when nothing is serving the address, and `Failure`'s whole job — "turn anything that was thrown into a sentence a person can act on" — cannot do it with a parser's message. The thrown error now says nothing is serving the API there, and names the command, which is what the panel below it already knew. |
| D303 | **The same parse ran before the status was read, so §104 was skipped for the failures that need it most.** A 502 from a proxy or a 500 rendered as an HTML error page threw on the parse and never became an `ApiError` — no status, no server text, and every screen that branches on a status saw nothing to branch on. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Status first, parse guarded, and a non-JSON error body carried through as its own words rather than reduced to a number — which is exactly what `requestBytes` does **ten lines below in the same file**, with the comment "A non-JSON error body is still better than a status code alone". The repair existed; it had been made in the function that serves two endpoints and not in the one every screen uses. Same shape as D262, where a count was made structural in one place and left typed out in its sibling. |
| D304 | **`lib/api.ts` had no test of its own.** Every screen depends on it and it was exercised only through components, which is why an unguarded parse in the first ten lines survived 3,094 tests. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Seven tests now cover the answers it can receive: a non-JSON success, a non-JSON error at 502, a JSON error's detail, a JSON success, and an empty body treated as nothing rather than as a failure. Four of the seven fail against the old code. The two that already passed are there deliberately — a repair to an error path is the easiest place to break the ordinary one, and nothing else asserted that a plain successful request still parses. |
| D305 | **My own repair for D302 made one case worse, and the page said so within a minute.** Copying `requestBytes`' "a non-JSON error body is still better than a status code alone", I sliced 300 characters of the body into the message — so the workspace, hitting a 404, showed `<!DOCTYPE HTML> <html lang="en"> <head> <meta charset="utf-8"> <title>Error response</title>…` in the error box. That is less use than the parser error it replaced. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The judgement was sound for the function I took it from: `requestBytes` serves two endpoints that return short plain-text errors. Markup is never quoted now; a short single-line body still is, because a gateway saying `upstream connect error` is worth having; the status is in the sentence as well as the object, so a screen that branches on it still can. I had written a test asserting the wrong behaviour — "carries the body's own words" — and it passed. Corrected, and the markup case added. |
| D306 | **Reading the page caught my regression on the same screen that produced the original defect, before it left the session.** The suite was green on the wrong behaviour, because the test I wrote encoded my mistaken judgement. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Worth recording as the strongest argument yet for the instrument: a test written by the person who misunderstood the requirement will agree with them, and the rendered page will not. The loop is cheap — `apps/web/out` is built by preflight, `python3 -m http.server` serves it, and no backend is needed for the client-side screens or, as it turns out, for the failure paths that matter most when there is no backend. |

It matters more here than most copy because the block's own docstring says the measurements "are quoted because they are the only part of this anybody can check without a hand, and because 'more stable' on its own is the kind of claim that turns out to be false". A number a reader cannot parse is no more checkable than no number. Read off the rendered page; no test named the string. |
| D308 | **The database guard from D116 works, and it stopped me.** Starting the API from the checkout with no `THROUGHLINE_HOME` refuses outright — "Refusing to guess which database to open… the old behaviour was to open the real installation at ~/.throughline-os and write to a researcher's projects" — and names the three ways out, scratch first and the real one last and explicitly. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Recorded because a guard is only proven by the moment it fires on somebody who was not expecting it. I took the scratch path; `~/.throughline-os` is untouched. The message earns the space it takes: it says what would have happened, not just that something was refused, which is the difference between a guard people route around and one they thank. |
| D309 | **The live health report was checked against the machine it describes, and every claim holds.** 200 while degraded, database critical and ok, each non-critical check naming its own `impact`. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The workers check says "No worker has reported in recently. The model check reported `ok` with `qwen2.5:7b-instruct`, which I verified against Ollama's own tag list rather than taking on trust: it is running and that model is installed. Unauthenticated requests are refused across a sample of seven endpoints — 401 where the endpoint exists, 404 where the path does not — so nothing reads data without an account. |
| D310 | **The rendered-page instrument reaches its boundary at the sign-in wall, and I stopped there rather than route around it.** A fresh scratch database opens on "Set up this machine", which asks for a name, an email and a password. | medium | **open** | Claude (Sarthak) | Creating an account and entering a password are things I do not do, and a local scratch database does not change that — the exception is the sort I would be inventing for my own convenience. So the data-driven screens are unread: source detail, findings, connections, the analysis and validation screens, which is where most of this session's code-level defects actually lived. The stack is running against `/tmp/tl-scratch` on ports 8080 and 3000; one account created by hand at `http://127.0.0.1:3000/workspace` opens all of it. |
| D311 | **I was about to cap the test workers in the committed config, and measuring said don't.** Two `preflight --full` runs had failed on random sets of tests frozen for ~1,013,000 ms, and this machine is 8 cores with 8 GB — so "vitest's default of about seven happy-dom workers is over-subscribed on memory" was a plausible diagnosis, and I offered to pin a lower number. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Timed instead, under the same ~110 MB-free conditions that produced the failures: **default 26.63s, four workers 26.41s, two workers 46.99s — all 3,103 passing**. The default is not the problem and never was; parallelism saturates around four and costs nothing above it. The real causes were the ones already recorded in D288 and D289 — my own busy-wait loops spinning cores, an orphaned `pytest` holding the database for 1h38m, and Chrome at peak — all transient, all gone. A permanent config change to work around a transient fault, in a project whose whole subject is claims that outlive their evidence, would have been the wrong repair for the right observation. |
| D312 | **My own workaround was costing half the speed of the thing it was working around.** I ran `--maxWorkers=2` every round after the failures, and it makes the suite take 47s instead of 26s. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Dropped. Recorded because the shape is worth remembering: a workaround adopted during a real incident outlives the incident silently, since it keeps working and nobody re-measures. The check is cheap — one timed run against the unmodified default — and it is the same discipline as re-validating a guard rather than trusting that it still bites. |
| D313 | **Every validation error in the product rendered as `[object Object]`, including on the first screen a researcher ever sees.** A hand-raised `HTTPException` carries a string detail; a *validation* failure carries FastAPI's list of objects — `{"detail":[{"loc":["body","password"],"msg":"String should have at least 12 characters"}]}` — and `lib/api.ts` did `String(detail)` on it. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | So setting up a new machine and mistyping the password reported itself as `[object Object]`, with the field name and the rule both present in the response and both discarded at the last step. §104 exists for exactly this. Now every field is named with what the server said about it — every field, not the first, because a form with two bad entries that reports one teaches somebody to fix and resubmit twice. Found against the running API rather than by reading: the shape only appears when a real FastAPI validator rejects a real body, and no fixture in the suite had that shape. |

IT PROTECTS AN ENTIRE RESEARCH CORPUS" is enforced by the API, which answers 422 with `min_length: 12` — the claim on the screen and the rule in the code are the same rule. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Worth recording because that is the pairing this session has repeatedly found broken elsewhere — a screen promising what nothing enforces — and here it is right. Probed with a deliberately invalid password so that nothing could be created; `POST /api/auth/accounts` is the add-a-colleague route and requires auth, while `/api/auth/setup` is the first-run one, which is itself the correct split. |

Found by taking the actual response from the running API and putting it through the client's reader, rather than by re-reading the code I had just written — the fixture I invented had the right *shape* and one entry, so it never showed what two look like together. |

Naming half the omission is the same failure one level down. Now names every side that lacks it, and both when both do. " dlg-danger" : ""}`} `` was the one shape it could not see. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Not invisible danger, which is what I first assumed: a destructive dialog is marked twice and visibly, by the alert icon in its header and `btn-danger` on the confirm button, both of which have rules. Nor is the worker's housekeeping, which deletes only rows already past `expires_at` and runs at most hourly on the idle path. Both were plausible enough to check and neither survives. **Left open rather than guessed at**: earlier calls in the same test authenticate fine, so whatever happens, happens between the validate POST and the next GET, and I cannot reproduce it. Under `pytest -n auto` the files would delete each other's users mid-request, and the symptom would be a 401 arriving where a list was expected, in whichever test happened to be running — which is the shape D337 is chasing, though I am not claiming it as that cause, because nothing parallel is installed. A field leaves the unread set for two opposite reasons: a screen started reading it, or the API did not send it. Only the first makes a baseline entry stale. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The API sets `passage_count` on a source *only once its paper row exists*, so a worker still parsing leaves it out — and the guard turned that into a verdict about the client. This is the signature defect of this repository, inside the guard written to catch it. D239 had already fixed the same shape one grain up, skipping a declaration that did not answer with the note that "a guard that fails for reasons unrelated to the thing it guards is one people learn to ignore"; that tolerance covered a missing endpoint and not a missing field. Only a field the API actually sent can now count. |

So the arithmetic is tested directly instead of hoping to reproduce the timing: the field the screen now reads is stale, the field the API did not send is not, a declaration that never answered is not, and a field still unread is not. Mutation-checked by removing the intersection, which fails exactly the second of those and nothing else. |

Nothing made the same check of `usedClasses()`: a moved directory or a glob that stopped matching would return no classes, `offenders` would be empty, and the guard would pass having examined nothing. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | A silent scan and a clean one are identical from the outside, which is the failure this file exists to prevent one level up — its own comment says "a guard for a silent failure is worth exactly as much as its ability to fail".
