A mutation that is not confirmed to have landed is not a mutation, and the third attempt — printing that it applied, then grepping for the marker — failed the assertion exactly as it should. |
| D343 | **Audit of every mutation this session, prompted by D342: the evidence holds, and the reason is worth stating.** A mutation reported as *caught* is self-proving — the test could not have failed if the source were unchanged — and every mutation I offered as evidence produced a failure naming the file and the assertion. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The ambiguous case is the opposite one, a mutation that *passes*, and there were exactly two: the drift test in D327, where the pass was confirmed independently by the tremor fixture's own amplitude being under the threshold, and the `dlg` assertion in D342, where I investigated instead of accepting and found the assertion weak for a different reason. So no earlier verification is retracted. **The practice changes anyway**: applying a mutation and running it is not evidence that it applied, and the check is one line — print on success, grep for the marker before running. Two of my substitutions this round silently matched nothing, and both times a green result briefly looked like a finding. |
| D344 | **The verdict layer read carefully and found sound, with the numbers rather than an impression.** 72 outcomes, 55 of whose templates require facts; a verdict that cannot phrase itself renders `[left not recorded]` instead of raising, on the stated grounds that "suppressing it would hide a real adjudication behind a formatting bug". | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | That recovery path calls `.format()` a second time, so a template using a format spec would raise from inside the handler — `_tokens` uses `string.Formatter().parse()`, which yields the field name without the spec, so it does not, and no template uses one anyway. Both halves checked rather than assumed. Of the 22 `Verdict(...)` constructions in the packages, 15 have a literal `facts` dict an AST walk can read, and every one supplies what its template needs. **The seven it cannot read are not evidence of anything**, which is why the scan reports what it read alongside what it found — the same distinction D339 turned on. |
| D244 | **The findings list presented a claim with three stated caveats identically to one with none.** `limitations` is sent with every finding in the list and the `Finding` type did not name it, so the qualification appeared only after the reader had opened the finding they were deciding about — which is the wrong order for a list whose purpose is choosing what to open. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The count rather than the text: three sentences of qualification per row would bury the titles the list exists to show. Nothing is shown when a finding states none — "0 stated limits" on every unqualified claim would be noise, and worse, would read as a positive assurance that there are none to state. This is the list half of D236, which fixed the detail view; the baseline entry said a summary row had nowhere for a caveat to sit, and a count fits where the text does not. |
| D245 | **Five statistical and honesty implementations audited this round and found correct.** Recorded because a later reader should not have to re-derive them, and because the audit is the work even when nothing is wrong. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | **E-value**: `RR + sqrt(RR·(RR−1))` with inversion for protective effects and 1 at the null — VanderWeele and Ding as stated. **d to log RR**: `D_TO_LOG_RR = 0.91`, VanderWeele's approximation, correct. **Benjamini-Hochberg**: sorted ascending, walked from the largest downward with a running minimum, so q-values are monotone and ties share one — the textbook step-up, and the family is the tests that produced a finite p-value rather than the runs attempted, which the docstring records as a past defect. **`all(checks.values())`**: `all({})` is `True`, so an empty check set would report "All robustness checks passed" vacuously — unreachable here, because all six checks are assigned unconditionally with no early return between them. **`checks["outliers"] = True`**: a hardcoded pass, and deliberately so — outliers are reported, never removed, and the sensitivity check below is the one that can fail; a rule failing whenever extreme values exist would refuse almost every real dataset and teach the researcher to ignore the verdict. |
| D242 | **A failed analysis was undiagnosable: the traceback was stored and shown to nobody.** The screen printed `error`, which is the runtime's fallback string "Analysis failed" whenever it has nothing better to say. What the sandbox actually wrote to stderr is captured to `logs` (capped at 8000 characters) on every run, and the `AnalysisRun` type never named it. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | A failure a researcher cannot diagnose is one they retry blindly or abandon, and neither is a use of their time — the explanation was in the database the whole while. Now offered on a failed run as "What the sandbox reported", **folded away rather than printed**: a stack trace above the fold makes an ordinary failure look like a crash in the product, and the sentence above it is what most readers need first. Not shown for a run that completed, where stderr from a successful fit is noise dressed as a problem. Six tests; three mutations — dropping it, printing it open, and showing it on success — each fail. Found in the D235 baseline, and removing the entry made that guard's staleness check confirm the fix. |
| D243 | **Three suspected defects checked this round and found sound, recorded so they are not re-investigated.** | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | `uncertainty_representation` has no interface reference because critiques are rendered generically by their `detail` text — the check identifier is never meant to appear, so "zero references" is not evidence here even though it was in D232 and D233. The image-comparison empty state is correctly conditional: it says "Nothing found by the checks that ran — not the same as nothing being there" when a comparison did not run, and asserts absence only when all of them did. `Vocabulary.pending_aliases` is a count whose underlying list (`pending`) is displayed, so the unread count is derivable rather than lost. Two of the three looked exactly like the defects found earlier in the session; the difference was checkable in each case and worth checking. |
| D241 | **The onboarding promised "Files never leave this machine" while the settings screen warned that every passage of every paper would be sent to a remote model.** Both sentences are in this application. They cannot both be true, and the absolute one is on the screen a researcher reads while deciding whether to hand the tool their data. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Choosing a model that does not run here sends passages of every paper the system reads to that service — `settings.tsx` says exactly that before asking permission, and the model list marks a remote one as "a remote service — data leaves this machine". The onboarding hint said the opposite, without condition. Now: "They stay on this machine; nothing is sent anywhere unless you choose a model that runs elsewhere" — true in both configurations, and it names the one decision that changes it, because a qualified claim a reader cannot act on is no better than a false one. **The other privacy claims were checked and are sound**: the paper reader and the imaging comparison read files in the browser and never upload them, so "never uploaded" is true there. Found by scanning user-facing copy for absolute claims and testing each against the code, which is the second signature defect of this codebase after the unreachable one. Added to the existing `overview-steps.test.tsx`; the unconditional wording and an unactionable qualification both fail as mutations. |
| D239 | **The contract guard I added was inventing findings, and one of them nearly sent me to "fix" correct code.** Its field parser read declarations line by line. `Report` is declared on one line — `{ finding_id: string; challenges: Challenge[]; note: string }` — so it saw the first field and reported `challenges` and `note` as fields the interface never names. Both are named. `AnalysisRun` was likewise accused of omitting fifteen fields it declares. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Replaced with a depth-aware walk that splits on top-level `;` as well as newlines, so a one-line type and a nested generic both read correctly — `_required` had the same blind spot and now shares the walk, which means the *original* guard was under-reporting too. Three false entries left the baseline. **A second flaw was flakiness**: the analysis-run endpoint answers only once the worker has finished, so the staleness check failed on timing whenever it did not, and a guard that fails for reasons unrelated to the thing it guards is one people learn to ignore. It now treats an endpoint that did not answer as saying nothing either way. |
| D240 | **The vocabulary counted what it accepted and stayed silent about what it refused.** `rejected_aliases` is sent on every request and was named by nothing, so the panel read "N variables · N approved terms · resolved a term N times" and never said that suggestions had been turned down. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | This product treats a refusal as an answer everywhere else — five comparability verdicts, a capability that states what it withholds, a sandbox that lists what it does not enforce, a globe that counts the countries facing away — and a vocabulary reporting only its approvals was the one asymmetry it argues against. Now "N approved · N refused", shown at zero as well, because a count that appears only when non-zero teaches a reader that its absence means nothing when it means none. Added to the existing `variables.test.tsx` rather than a new file. `pending_aliases` stays in the baseline: it counts the `pending` list the panel already renders in full. |
| D237 | **A retracted source did not say so on its own page.** Harvesting marks a source withdrawn rather than deleting it — deleting would destroy the reference *and* the evidence of withdrawal — and `withdrawals.py` frames the question as "what of mine is now standing on something withdrawn". The API sends `withdrawn_at` and `withdrawn_reason` on every source; the `Source` type declared neither. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | So the harvest report announced a retraction once, at harvest time, to whoever happened to be looking, and the one page about that source — the page somebody opens while deciding whether to rest a finding on it — showed its trust level, its ingestion status and nothing at all. Now said first, above the status chip and above the injection-signal notice, because a retraction outranks both: it changes what every finding resting on the source is worth. The reason is given, and so is why the source is still listed — a reader seeing a retracted source in place would otherwise assume the system failed to remove it. Found by the reverse contract check from D235, minutes after it existed. |
| D238 | **My own guard was wrong twice before it was right, and both corrections matter more than the guard.** The first version keyed unread fields by type *name*, so `Source` in `lib/api.ts` and a narrow local `Source` in `variables.tsx` merged into one entry — a baseline that named fields which are in fact declared, and which would have hidden a real omission behind a broad entry from another file. Now keyed by name *and* declaring file. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The second correction was a test of mine, not the product. Asserting that the withdrawal notice appears above the status chip failed three ways in a row: `container.textContent` and `container.innerHTML` returned empty while `querySelector` on that same container found the element; a document-scoped version passed only because an earlier test had left a matching node behind; and an apparently blank render turned out to be an unflushed state update rather than a defect — I nearly reported "the source page renders nothing when ingestion failed", which is false. The ordering is now asserted against the order the JSX is written in, which is weaker evidence and is evidence, with the reason recorded in the test. |
| D235 | **The interface/API contract guard only ever asked one of the two questions.** `test_the_interface_and_the_api_agree.py` checks that every field a screen *requires* is still sent, which catches a rename or a deletion. It cannot see the opposite: a field the API sends that no type names. That is where D232, D233 and D234 all came from — the sandbox's honest limits, the readable dataset formats and a run's warnings each arrived on every request and reached no screen, and every test stayed green because a field the client cannot name is one nothing can miss. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | **The existing guard was extended rather than duplicated** — the fourth time this session I have nearly rebuilt something that was already there, and the first time I checked before writing a line. Measured first: seventeen types carry unread fields. Most are housekeeping — identifiers, timestamps, ownership columns a list does not display — so a hard "every field must be declared" would have cried wolf and been deleted. It is a **ratchet** instead: the current set is recorded with reasons, a *new* unread field fails, and a second test fails if the baseline names something that is no longer unread, so the list cannot quietly become permission to ignore anything. Both directions mutation-tested. |
| D236 | **A finding's own caveats were sent on every request and shown to nobody.** `graphs.evidence_graph` returns `limitations` at the top level, deliberately beside the note explaining that no contradicting evidence "is not the same as none existing" — the whole response is shaped around what a reader should not conclude, and that field was the one part of the shape the TypeScript type never named. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by the new reverse check within minutes of adding it, which is the argument for the guard. A finding is the research claim this product exists to produce; its limitations are the caveats on that claim, and they were being dropped between the database and the screen. Now declared and shown as "What this finding does not establish", **beside the claim rather than beneath the evidence** — a caveat that appears after the supporting and contradicting evidence arrives once the reader has already formed a view. Five tests; the drop and the misplacement both fail as mutations. The list endpoints still carry it unread and are named in the baseline: a summary row has nowhere for a caveat to sit, and the detail view is where a claim is weighed. |
| D234 | **A run said the method was wrong and the screen showed the coefficient anyway.** The analysis view rendered `limitations` and not `warnings`. Both are produced by the runtime, both are stored — `warnings` in its own column and again inside the result — and both arrive at the client on every request. Only one was ever shown. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | **The half that was dropped is the half that matters more.** `methods.py` appends "Normality is violated. Spearman correlation is the appropriate alternative." when a Pearson or a t-test fails its normality check, and `entrypoint.py` captures whatever the statistics library said during the fit — a `ConvergenceWarning` means the optimiser never settled and the estimate may be meaningless. So a researcher could run a Pearson correlation on non-normal data, be told in writing by the system that Spearman was the right test, never see it, and publish the coefficient. "An interesting pattern is not a discovery" is the first sentence of this product's own workspace; a result whose method the system knows is wrong, kept quiet, is the worst available version of that. Now shown **above the estimate**, because a warning changes how the number should be read while a limitation qualifies a result that stands — the two are kept apart rather than merged, and a mutation that merges them fails. Read from both places the same value is written to, deduplicated, so an older run carrying only one still says what it knew. `warnings` was also missing from the `AnalysisRun` type while `SELECT r.*` returned it — the same root cause as D233: a field the client cannot name is a field it cannot show. Nine tests; three mutations each fail. |
| D233 | **The upload dialog greyed out the primary data formats of three disciplines.** "Add sources" carried `accept=".pdf,.docx,.txt,.md,.csv,.tsv,.xlsx,.json"` while ingestion reads fifteen dataset formats and reports them on every capabilities request. Eleven were missing: `.db .dta .geojson .por .sas7bdat .sav .sqlite .sqlite3 .xls .xlsm .xpt` — Stata, SPSS, SAS, SQLite and older Excel. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | **The silence is what makes this expensive.** `accept` hides files rather than rejecting them, so a social scientist with a `.sav`, an epidemiologist with a `.dta` or an economist with a `.sas7bdat` opened the dialog, saw their own data greyed out, had no error to read and no reason to doubt the screen — and concluded the product could not read their work. It reads all of it. **The root cause was the type**: `Capabilities` never declared `formats`, so a field the server has always sent was one the client could not name, and the literal survived because nothing could replace it. Now built from `formats.readable` plus the document formats, which are not datasets and have never been in that field. **When the server has not answered, the picker offers everything**: a wrong filter hides a researcher's data with no error, while no filter lets ingestion give a real answer — undefined is the honest state, and a mutation that guesses a fallback list fails. The eleven formats that need an extra installed are named beneath the button rather than offered, since selecting one would fail at ingestion with nothing to read. Eight tests; three mutations each fail. |
| D232 | **The analysis sandbox reported its own limits honestly, and the interface showed none of them.** `policy_report()` composes an unusually candid account — what is enforced, what is only best-effort, what is not attempted — and its docstring notes that every value there was once a hardcoded `True` beside a real `platform.system()`, "the one shape of dishonesty this file exists to prevent". It is stored with every run and served on every capabilities request. Nothing displayed it: `Capabilities.analysis.isolation` was typed `Record<string, unknown>`, an opaque bag no component could render. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | A researcher deciding whether to run an analysis over data they were trusted with could not learn that the filesystem isolation is **not kernel-level**, or that blocking network egress is `python_level_only` — attempted in Python, which a determined library can step around. The form told them "this runs in the sandbox" and offered no way to ask what that meant. Now disclosed exactly there, collapsed so a t-test on a laptop is not interrupted and one click away when it matters. **Ordered limits-first on purpose**: eleven enforced controls listed above two missing ones would read as reassurance, and the missing ones are the only reason to open it. The two `SandboxPolicy` types were merged into one — the live report and the copy stored with each run come from the same function, and the stored-run type described three of its seven fields. Every field is optional so a run recorded by an older version still parses rather than failing to render. Eight tests; three mutations (drop the panel, reassurance first, best-effort shown as enforced) each fail. |
| D230 | **The globe was the one rotatable chart no seam could address.** Six charts on `/charts-3d` are collected by `SpatialControl` through `controllerRef` and `alsoControls`; `Globe3D` accepted no such prop and exposed no `VisualizationController`, so a researcher who turned hand tracking on could rotate every figure on the page except the globe, with nothing saying why. §189's premise for that page is that the *chart* decides which one a hand is addressing — a chart with no controller was not losing that competition, it was not in it. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | `Globe3D` now implements the interface and the page passes it a ref. The methods that do not apply are answered honestly rather than plausibly: `pan` does nothing because a sphere has no pan and quietly remapping it to rotation would mean a sideways hand sometimes turned the earth and sometimes did not, depending on which chart it was over; `hover`, `select` and `withinPolygon` return nothing because this globe carries no marks — `GENERATORS.places` is null on purpose, since a value invented for a real country reads as a fact about the world. **Found by asking why the globe could not be covered by a test**, which is the same question that revealed it could not be reached by a hand. Verified live: one arrow press moves the hidden-country count from 34 to 35 — a semantic observable computed during the render, rather than a pixel hash, which for a sphere is nearly constant under rotation and is part of why earlier pixel readings misled me. |
| D231 | **"Every chart turns by the same amount" was true and unguarded.** `Surface`, `Volume` and `Globe3D` hand-roll the key handler instead of using `useSpatialKeys`, so the step (12°) and the zoom (1.15) exist as literals in four places — exactly the drift the hook's own docstring says it exists to prevent, since a reader who learns Left turns the network expects Left to turn the surface by as much. | low | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | `spatial-charts-actually-turn.test.tsx` now presses a key on all nine rotatable charts and asserts they move the same axis by the same amount — derived by asking `rotateCamera` what `KEY_STEP` does, not written down, because `viewState` reports internal units and a hard-coded 12 fails against correct code. It also drives each controller directly, which caught a gap the keyboard tests could not: removing the globe's restoration of its own framing on reset broke nothing, because `Home` goes through the key handler and a hand goes through the controller — a hand asking to reset would have framed the earth wrongly, silently. A completeness guard fails if a new chart promises the keys and is not in the table, or accepts no controller. |
| D229 | **"The arrow keys turn the chart" was asserted about the text of the files, never about their behaviour.** `spatial-charts-answer-keys.test.ts` reads each spatial chart's source and checks a key handler is present, the canvas is focusable and a label exists — all true, all necessary, and none of it presses a key. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The gap matters more here than usual: every one of these canvases tells a screen reader "Arrow keys rotate, plus and minus zoom, Home resets the view", which is an instruction rather than a description — a reader who cannot see the picture is being told exactly how to operate it. And rotation is the depth cue, not a convenience (§10), so a reader who cannot turn the chart sees one fixed projection of a tangle for ever. `spatial-charts-actually-turn.test.tsx` now presses keys on all six charts and reads the camera back through each chart's own controller: it turns, it turns back, Home restores, and keys it does not own are left to the page — a chart that swallowed Tab would trap a keyboard reader, which is the more frightening failure. Thirty assertions; three mutations (dead handler, dead Home, swallow-everything) each fail. **The behaviour was correct all along** — I suspected it was broken from browser measurements that turned out to be unreliable, and the value here is the test that was missing, not a fix. Its limit is stated in the file: it checks the camera moved, not that the canvas repainted, because happy-dom has no 2D context. |
| D227 | **The catalogue denied the existence of a renderer that was drawing on the same page.** Clicking "3D globe" said "No renderer here draws a globe yet, so 3D globe cannot be shown" — a few hundred pixels below a rotatable earth with coastlines, a graticule and a count of the countries facing away. The sentence was not stale; it was false, and falsifiable by scrolling up. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | The cause is a real distinction that had collapsed into one branch: `RENDERED` means "can be drawn *and* an example can be invented for it", and `globe` fails only the second half — a globe is drawn from coastlines, which are a fact about the world rather than something to synthesize. A third sentence now says which of the two is missing, and `RENDERER_NEEDS_REAL_DATA` names the case. The headline count is deliberately unchanged: it promises "pick one and it is drawn below", and a globe still is not. 33 `mesh` entries genuinely have no renderer and keep the original wording, because trading a false sentence for a vague one would be no better. | D228 | **Blender availability was computed on every capabilities request and displayed nowhere.** `availability()` composes a "withheld" sentence and an install hint precisely so a researcher can be told; `settings.tsx` typed the response as `{ packs }` and dropped the rest. | medium | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | Surfaced as its own row, deliberately apart from the feature packs: those are Python distributions this application installs on a button press, and Blender is a separate desktop program of several hundred megabytes that cannot be installed from here and cannot run in a browser — there is no production build of Blender for the web. A button that appeared to install it and then failed would be worse than the honest sentence. The row leads with what still works, because nothing here needs it: every chart draws in the browser, and a 3D figure exports as geometry any tool can open. **A regression was introduced and caught by ten existing tests**: the row was gated on `blender !== null`, which is true for `undefined`, so any response without the field — an older backend — rendered the row with no state and threw while reading `.available`, taking the whole settings screen down over an optional capability. Pinned by a test; the mutation fails. |
| D222 | **`lib/spatial/whose.ts` was written to fix a live defect, tested, and wired to nothing.** §32: the tracker reports hands, not people, and `SpatialControl` took `frame.hands[0]` unconditionally — correct exactly while one person is in frame, which a laboratory is not. MediaPipe promises no ordering between frames, so a colleague leaning in could take a drag mid-gesture and the object would follow a stranger. The fix existed in the repository the whole time, imported only by its own test file — and **not by oversight**: `tests/nothing-is-built-and-unreachable.test.ts` named it a deliberate exception, arguing that wiring it without hardware is how the gesture layer accrues code that has never met a hand. This entry overrides that decision; D223 records the correction. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | **Wired in, but not the way it first looked.** The naive integration — treat two hands as ambiguous and hold — breaks the deliberate two-handed zoom, which the machine already reasons about separately (§17: "two hands in frame is not two hands in use"), and would then explain the break with a message about somebody walking past: a fix that breaks a working feature and misdescribes the damage. The reconciliation lives in a new `followFrame`, which takes the machine's own answer as a parameter rather than guessing at gesture state from hand positions. The hold is a third outcome distinct from "no hand", because releasing a grab is itself an action and doing it because somebody walked behind the desk is the accidental action §87 counts. **Three of my own tests were vacuous and had to be corrected before they proved anything:** a component test fed open hands and never reached the two-handed path, passing with the guard deleted; two unit fixtures put a hand exactly where the tracked one was, which continuity resolved immediately, so the branch under test was never entered. The component cannot reach a zoom at all — happy-dom gives a `<video>` no width, so the machine never leaves "tracking lost", which is why the existing spatial tests assert no machine states — so the guarantee moved to a plain function where it can be checked. All three mutations now fail. **What remains unverified is exactly what that allowlist entry was about:** no camera has met this code. The decision logic is checked; how often a real room produces a hold, and whether the message flickers, are not, and cannot be checked here. |

Not attempted alongside the work that found it, because a half-connected selection is worse than none — a researcher would see it work in one view and silently not in another. |
| D221 | **A third of the evidence model was unreachable: nothing could state a fact the file did not record.** The engine has always had three origins — read from a header, stated by a person, unknown — with `evidenceStrength` counting a stated fact for half a read one and `text()` rendering it as "stated, not read from the file". The only call to `declared()` in the whole application was a hard-coded synthetic scan. So every comparison of real files stopped at "cannot be judged" the moment one axis was silent. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | **`components/imaging/Declare.tsx`, driven entirely by the profile**, so a new discipline gets the screen for free and the component never learns what a channel or a contrast phase is. Closed vocabularies are offered as lists rather than free fields — the same argument `Modality` is a union rather than a string, since an open field lets "MR", "MRI" and "mri" compare as three — and the genuinely open axes (channel, plotted quantity, units) gained a `text` match that folds case and trims, so "DAPI" and "dapi" are one channel. **Only silent axes are offered, and a statement cannot overwrite a header fact**: that is not a guard against a slip but the distinction the engine rests on, since afterwards nothing could tell which verdicts rested on which. Mutation-tested — allowing the overwrite, carrying a statement as `header`, and offering already-read axes are each caught. **Measured in a browser:** two OME-TIFFs go from "cannot be judged" to "can be compared directly" when preparation is stated, evidence moves 83% → 92% (half a point, not a whole one), and the verdict says "stated, not read from the file". **Also removed `domainForFile`, which was dead** — its only caller went when the picker bug was fixed, and a test still pinned it, which made it look covered. While it existed it invited exactly the mistake that was made: an extension says what a file is, never what it is of. `extensions` now drives the picker's `accept` instead, so the list has one definition. A CSS guard test caught the new panel shipping unstyled. | 
| D220 | **TIFF was declined by name, which meant the only file that records the acquisition was the one being refused.** TIFF is the working format of light microscopy and OME-TIFF is where the objective, channel, acquisition mode and exposure live — the axes the microscopy profile decides on. A microscopist could open a PNG export and be told, correctly, that nothing about the acquisition was recorded. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | **`lib/imaging/tiff.ts` reads classic TIFF at 8, 16 and 32 bits** — uncompressed, LZW (with the horizontal predictor), PackBits and Deflate, single- and multi-strip — plus OME-XML for the microscopy axes. **Every fixture in `tests/fixtures/tiff.ts` was written by Pillow, not by this repository**, because a decoder tested against its own encoder proves the two agree rather than that either is right: a shared misreading of LZW's early code width would cancel out and pass. Mutation-tested against exactly that class — removing the early change, ignoring the predictor, reading 16-bit with the wrong byte order, and dropping strips after the first are each caught. **Tiled TIFF is refused by name rather than decoded**, and that is an evidence decision, not an effort one: nothing available could write a tiled file to verify the path against, and an unverified decoder would produce a plausible micrograph rather than an obvious failure. JPEG-in-TIFF and BigTIFF are likewise named and declined. **Measured payoff:** the same image opened as PNG scores 0% of the acquisition read from the file; as OME-TIFF it scores 83%, keeps its full 16-bit range instead of being halved by a canvas, and can now be refused on a channel mismatch — a verdict the PNG path could never reach. It still reports "cannot be judged" when preparation is unrecorded, which is correct: OME has no field for fixed against live, and that decides what is present to be imaged. **A botched edit deleted ~165 lines of `CaseWorkspace.tsx` mid-task** — a `];` search ran past a `] as const;` into the JSX. Recovered in full, comments included, from `sourcesContent` in the `.next` SSR source map; the replacement was redone with an exact-block match and a hard assertion. | 
| D219 | **Compare was built for one discipline and read as the platform's only one.** The reasoning under `CaseCompare` was never medical — record where each fact came from, refuse when two acquisitions do not share a measurement, say why — but the axes, the tolerances, the prose and the readers all were, and everything rendered through a voxel volume. A microscopist with two images of one section had nowhere to put an objective, no verdict that spoke about one, and no panel that could draw a plane. | high | **done** | Claude (Sarthak) | **Axes, rules and prose moved into a domain profile** (`lib/imaging/domain.ts`): radiology, microscopy, general images and plotted figures, each with its own tiers — `foundational` (different physical quantities, no remedy), `visibility` (decides what is present at all) and `harmonizable` (correctable, and the advice says how). The engine, `query.ts` and the workspace copy now read the profile and switch on no domain id anywhere. **Radiology is unchanged, and the evidence is that its 25 comparability and 21 query tests were not touched and still pass.** A 2D path was added end to end: PNG/JPEG header parsing that reads what the container actually states rather than what the canvas returns, and `RasterPanel`, a flat-image viewer on the same shared window as the volumes. **Two real defects were found only by running it.** The panel announced every view change from a ref written during render, so the shared window — the thing that makes a side-by-side honest — was one step stale everywhere; it now reports after the state commits. And the discipline picker was inert, because the ingest consulted the file extension first and every `.png` is claimed by the general-image profile, so choosing "Microscopy" scored evidence against axes nobody had been asked about. Both are pinned by tests that fail when the fix is removed. TIFF is declined by name rather than accepted and shown blank. | 
| D345 | **The first release was built and tagged, and it cannot be published from this machine.** `v0.2.0` is tagged on `599922e` and `python scripts/manage.py release` wrote `dist/` — a 20 MB tarball, its checksum, the four launchers, `install.sh`, `_headers`, `latest.json` and the Homebrew formula — with all 74 release, packaging and landing-page guards passing. Two things stop it being a release a stranger can use. **Unsigned:** `keys/release.pub` is committed but the private half is not on this machine, so `latest.json` carries no signature and an installed copy cannot verify the update it is offered; the checksum beside the tarball is not a substitute, since anyone who can replace one can replace the other. **Unpublished:** the download host is the landing page's own origin (`throughline-research.pages.dev`), and this repository holds no deploy credential, no `wrangler.toml` and no publish workflow — `frontend/README.md` documents the upload as a manual step. | high | fixed | Claude (Fenil) | Built 2026-09-07 from the tagged commit. To finish it: set `THROUGHLINE_RELEASE_KEY` to a file holding the private half and re-run `python scripts/manage.py release` so the manifest is signed, then upload `dist/` and `frontend/dist/index.html` to the Pages project. The stale untagged tarball from an earlier build was removed from `dist/` so the upload cannot carry two artifacts. Related: T086 would automate exactly this on a tag push and is now `open` rather than `blocked`. **Published 2026-09-07 — both halves closed.** Signed with the key whose public half is `keys/release.pub`, and uploaded to the Pages project from `dist-upload/`, which is the folder this repository does not document and which took three wrong answers to find: the release command writes `dist/`, the landing page builds `frontend/dist/`, and the thing actually deployed is a third directory, gitignored, holding the page and the release files together. Verified against the live site rather than the build: every published path answers 200, `/install.sh` is served `text/plain` so it can be read before it is piped, `/latest.json` verifies against the shipped public key, and the 19 MB tarball downloaded from the site hashes to the value the signed manifest names — so a stranger gets exactly what was signed. **Left undone:** `frontend/README.md` names `dist/` as what the host must serve and does not mention `dist-upload/` at all, which is the trap that caught this session; T086 would remove the question by publishing on a tag push. |

| D346 | **No italic font face is loaded anywhere, and the editorial italic is the reference design's central typographic device.** Every one of the four homepage frames sets its second line in italic serif -- *A connected whole.*, *Your questions take it further.*, *Keep asking why.*, *The evidence stays connected.* -- and `apps/web/app/fonts.css` declares Inter, Source Serif 4 and JetBrains Mono at `font-style: normal` only. `public/fonts/` holds six `.woff2` files and none is an italic cut. So the browser synthesises the italic by shearing the upright, which is exactly what spec 05 forbids: *do not silently rely on synthetic italic or a remote font CDN*. | medium | fixed | Claude (Fenil) | Confirmed by reading `apps/web/app/fonts.css` (three `@font-face` families, six declarations, every one `font-style: normal`) and listing `apps/web/public/fonts/*.woff2`. The fix is a licensed Source Serif 4 italic subset committed alongside the existing files with its OFL notice. **That makes it platform-shaped work under `CLAUDE.md`** -- a committed binary fixture -- so it needs a CI dispatch before it lands, not just local vitest. **Re-measured 2026-09-12 and closed:** `app/fonts.css` declares both italic faces (`source-serif-4-italic-latin.woff2` and its `-ext`), committed on `feat/ring-entrance` under T143. The row had gone stale — the fix landed and nobody came back to the ledger, which is the failure mode [[D218]] warns about in the other direction. |

| D355 | **The package's light master splits gold and blue; this app's light theme has one blue accent for both.** Spec 08 marks research focus and selected navigation in gold and reserves blue for data selection, which is how a reader tells *where I am* from *what I picked*. `globals.css` light defined a single `--accent: #2563EB` used for both, and its dark theme used amber for everything. **Half of this is now measured as fixed (2026-09-12): light `--accent` is `#8A6A1F`, gold, and every screenshot of the workspace shows gold selection.** The blue that remains is scoped to `prefers-contrast: more`, which is deliberate. What is still open is the *split*: navigation and data selection share one `--accent`, so there is no colour that says *what I picked* as distinct from *where I am*. The new section row therefore uses `--accent-soft`, which is already the app's selected fill and already contrast-checked, and follows the theme rather than the package. | medium | open | -- | Found under T144. Closing it means repainting the light palette so navigation and data selection are separably coloured, and re-running `accent-contrast.test.ts` against the new pairs -- a palette change across the whole workspace, not a nav change, which is why it is recorded rather than done inside T144. The reasoning is also written where somebody will meet it, in the `.sectionbar-item[aria-current]` rule. |

Nothing renders any of them. | low | open | -- | Found under T144, and left deliberately rather than swept up with the conversion: the rules are scattered across a file whose dark block and media-query mirror must stay byte-identical (`css-tokens.test.ts`), and deleting forty declarations by eye at the end of a long change is how a rule that was doing something quietly goes with them. `css-classes.test.ts` only checks the other direction -- every class in the markup has a rule -- so no guard reports this, which is why it needs a row. |
| D376 | **A control that did nothing, and a green suite could not tell.** *(Recorded as D354 on `feat/ring-entrance`; renumbered on rebase because `main` had already allocated D354 to a different finding — [[D351]] again.)* The entrance's *Pause background motion* stopped the renderer's ambient loop and changed nothing else, because the page's scroll controller pushes progress on every animation frame whether or not the reader moved, and every push re-rendered the scene. A paused ring still drew sixty times a second at a fixed camera. Nothing in vitest could have caught it -- happy-dom runs no animation frames and paints nothing. The general shape is worth keeping: a pause control is only as good as the thing that keeps pushing work at it, and the layer that pushes is usually somebody else's file. |

| D357 | **The project graph never said which of its two kinds of edge a row was.** `graphs.knowledge_graph` unions `research_edges` (asserted, may be wrong) with `artifact_lineage_edges` (recorded fact) precisely to keep both on one canvas, and selected no column naming the arm. Two readers were already branching on `edge_kind`: `graphview.tsx` weights a lineage edge at 0.8 and a semantic one at its confidence, and read `undefined` for every edge, so every edge took the semantic weight and lineage never pulled harder — the distinction the UNION exists to preserve was lost between the query and the screen, silently, for as long as the union has existed. `notebook.py` sends its own `edge_kind`, which is why the note graph was unaffected and nobody noticed. Found while building the river, which draws recorded lineage solid and related context dotted and could not have done it. | medium | fixed | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/ring-entrance` | `'semantic' AS edge_kind` and `'lineage' AS edge_kind` on the two arms. `tests/test_the_graph_says_which_edges_are_recorded.py` — 3 tests, one per arm plus one asserting they stay apart in a single payload; removing either literal fails it. |
| D358 | **The Sources list handed over no research-object id, which is the handle every provenance route takes.** The journal, a note and a version history are all addressed by research-object id, so a screen holding only a source id could reach none of them — this is the cause under [[D213]], which records the same dead end for the finding, source and analysis details. Found while building the reasoning master, whose left column hangs the researcher's own notes on the paper being read. **A source reaches its node by two roads and the first fix only looked finished:** a paper's object points back at the source (`research_objects.source_id`), a dataset's is named by the dataset row (`datasets.object_id`), and covering only the first returned null for every dataset — which reads as *not ingested yet* and is wrong. | medium | fixed | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/ring-entrance` | Both roads queried once each, oldest object first so a source with several resolves the same way on every request; null when profiling has not made one yet, and the key always present so a caller can tell that from *not reported*. Three tests in `tests/test_the_sources_list_says_where_it_came_from.py`, one per road plus the honest null. |

| D359 | **An unclosed rule in `globals.css` had been swallowing every rule written after it for five days.** `.acct-theme-option[data-state="checked"]` lost its closing brace in the 2026-09-06 merge, so the browser read the next comment and the next selector as more of that rule's body and discarded **116 class rules** — `.case-declare`, `.withdrawn`, `.run-logs` and everything since, including both master layouts built on top of them. On `main` as well as here. **Why nothing caught it:** `css-classes.test.ts` asks whether a class *appears* in a stylesheet, and it does — the text is there, inside another rule's body; and the suite renders into happy-dom, which applies no CSS at all, so every component test passed against a page that would have been unstyled in a browser. Found by screenshotting the running app, which was the only thing that was ever going to find it. | high | fixed | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/ring-entrance` | Brace closed; `apps/web/tests/every-stylesheet-parses.test.ts` parses all five sheets, skipping comments and quoted strings so a brace inside either does not raise a false alarm, and four tests pin the scanner itself against the real shape of the defect. |
| D360 | **The river reported a project as having no connections while the navigation beside it counted six.** Both were reading truthfully from different places: `knowledge_graph` returns `research_objects`, a connection is a row in `connections` with a lifecycle of its own and only sometimes an object beside it, and `citation` was missing from the stage vocabulary so the worked example's two citations sat under *Recorded, not placed*. A column that reports a project as empty of the thing it holds six of is the quiet kind of lie this repository keeps removing. The state vocabulary in `lib/river.ts` was the evidence it was always meant to include them — validated, exploratory, rejected and candidate are a connection's lifecycle, not an object's status. | medium | fixed | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/ring-entrance` | Connections come from their own route and are placed in their column with their lifecycle and their producing run printed; `citation` added to Sources. The Validation column now says validation reports are recorded against the connection they checked rather than that nothing happened, because *this view cannot reach them* and *the project has never validated anything* are different facts. Two new tests in `the-river-keeps-every-branch.test.tsx`; found by screenshotting the worked example. |
| D361 | **`POST /api/sources/{id}/claims` answered 500 to every call for a month.** The route took a `force` query parameter and handed it to `claim_test.locate_claims`, which has never had one, so the call raised `TypeError` before reaching any of the function's logic — a researcher who chose a paper got an error envelope where its claims should have been. In since 2026-08-10. It was redundant by construction too: reading the record and re-reading the paper are separate acts on separate routes, and a POST here *is* the re-read. **Why nothing caught it:** every test of this area calls `claim_test.locate_claims` directly, the interface's tests mock the API, the reachability guard asks whether a path is *served* rather than whether it can be *called*, and nothing ever passed the parameter. | high | fixed | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/ring-entrance` | Parameter removed from route and call. `tests/test_reading_a_paper_reaches_the_reader.py` calls the route over HTTP against a real database, using a source with no indexed passages so the model is never consulted — the failure lived in the call, not in the reading. Mutation-checked: restoring `force=` fails both tests. |

| D368 | **The homepage proved what was inside with a column of screenshots, which proves the wrong thing.** A stack of UI captures asks a stranger to squint at somebody else's interface and take the claim on trust, and in a year when every comparable site is scroll-driven it reads as the most the developer could manage. It also missed the product's actual sentence: what Throughline sells is not a set of screens, it is that a finding keeps the line back to what it was built from — and a line cannot be photographed. | high | fixed | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/ring-entrance` | `components/entrance/Trace.tsx`: four stops on one stroke — finding, validation, analysis, source — scrubbed by their own stretch of scroll, with the stroke growing and each stop arriving and then staying. Values are the reference's own worked example, so the vocabulary a visitor learns (F-008, VR-008, AN-014, SRC-014) is the workspace's. Six committed screenshots deleted with it. **Found on the way:** the scroll loop compared the chapters' clamped progress to decide whether to do work, so everything below the runway was frozen — the camera stopped travelling and the chain never advanced. It compares the scroll position now, and the chapters' own work keeps its own guard. |

| D369 | **The workspace had a journey and showed a sentence about it.** `lib/loop.ts` knows the six steps and which are done; the strip spent its line on "Step 4 of 6 · Try to destroy what survived", which names a position and shows no path. Above it sit five navigation groups and twenty-three sections, so a researcher opening the product meets a tool rack and no route through it — the owner's "we have functions but no journey". | high | fixed | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/ring-entrance` | The strip carries the whole loop as a spine: six ticks on the line it already spent, done / here / ahead told apart by mark AND label rather than by colour, each tick going to that step's own screen. Only the current step spells its name, because six labels would push the band to two rows on every screen. The screen-reader text says done and not-yet and stays silent on *here*, which `aria-current` already announces — saying both put "you are here" on the line twice. Recorded in the button-vocabulary allowlist: six ticks with `.btn` chrome would stop reading as one sequence. **Not the whole answer** — the spine shows the route, it does not reorganise the twenty-three sections around it. |

Upload your own dataset and you got a profile and a dead end: six screens, six buttons, each able to fail on its own. Nobody walks six, including the people who built it. | high | fixed | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/ring-entrance` | The pipeline is `_advance_project`, used by `example.assemble` and by a new `project.advance`; `POST /api/projects/{id}/advance` queues it, and the Overview offers it as one press. **Verified end to end on two datasets nobody had seen**: a 180-row harvest frame and a 150-row soil frame, each uploaded, advanced with one call, and each producing analyses, connections and a finding in about eight seconds — with the planted relationships found and the noise variable left weak. |

| D374 | **The cockpit's right column held one sentence where §09 asks for the run's whole context.** UI_02 wants "Context/Notes, linked connection/source/data, validation controls and full provenance" there; it had a generic "an analysis is one run" and a fold about the installation. So a researcher reading a result could not see the connection it produced, whether anything had tried to destroy it, or what it was computed from — and all three lived in other sections. | high | fixed | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/ring-entrance` | `AnalysisContext`: linked objects, the validation state, and the provenance chain with a way into the river focused on this run. **Validation is a door, not a copy** — choosing which columns to adjust for is a claim about the science, made against the dataset's own schema, and the Connections screen already lists every candidate column with its type, spread and missingness. A compact second picker would be a second implementation of a decision and the two would drift. |
| D375 | **Twenty-three sections is not a product, it is a directory of one.** Every capability was built, and then filed as a peer of every other: *Search sources*, *Find papers*, *Find data* and *Read a figure* sat beside *Sources* as though each were a peer of the library, when every one of them ends in a source landing in it. *Chart primitives* was filed under **This machine**, beside the hand-tracking pages, because it needs no project — so "what could I draw this as", a question you have while making a figure, was a property of the installation. *Patterns* and *Embedding space* were sections, and both answer a question about this project's analyses. *Journal* and *Activity* were two entries answering one question about the same project. The cost is not the length of the rail: it is that the thing a researcher wants is always in another section, and finding it requires already knowing that *Patterns* does not mean the patterns in your data. | high | fixed | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/ring-entrance` | Fifteen sections in six groups named for the package's surfaces — the project, Evidence, Analysis, Lineage, Communicate, this machine. The eight absorbed ones are views of the screen that owns them, chosen by a tab and carried in the address, so each is still linkable, still reloadable and still reachable by Back. **Nothing was deleted**: `MOVED_TO` resolves every old `?section=` to the screen that absorbed it *and the view within it*, carrying the `item` across — deleting them instead would have made an old link fall back to Overview, which is the silent wrong answer that teaches a researcher the feature is gone. `rail-follows-the-work`'s ordering constraints hold through the rewrite, and its length floor is now a floor against a dead scan rather than a count that every future fold must edit. |
| D377 | **The Record explained where notes come from and gave no way to write one.** Its empty state read "notes written beside an analysis, a figure or a finding appear here" — true, and a dead end: a researcher who had written nothing was reading a screen that described a feature it gave them no route to. A thought that belongs to the project rather than to one object had no door at all from here. | medium | fixed | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/ring-entrance` | The Record now offers the Notebook, empty or not — "where do I write" is not a question that stops being asked once the first note exists. **A composer here was the other option and is the wrong one**, for the reason [[D374]] gives about validation: they are the same `notes` table (`journal.recent` filters on the project, not on `note_kind`, so a notebook page, a daily page and an annotation all arrive here), and two implementations of writing would drift over links, titles and daily pages. Three tests, including one holding that no button appears when there is nowhere for it to go — a door that leads nowhere reads as broken rather than absent. |
| D378 | **Pressing the product's own recommended control changed nothing a reader could see.** Walked on the running stack, not reasoned about: the loop's strip said *Validate yield_t_ha × fertiliser_kg*, that press opened the connection with the robustness card on screen, and pressing **Validate** ran the suite, which honestly reported *Did not pass: confounder_adjustment*. The card was then pixel for pixel what it had been before. The report renders about a thousand pixels below, at the foot of the column, and the card directly beneath the button went on saying the connection had not survived a validation run — true, and indistinguishable from nothing having been tried. **And the default press could never have passed**: naming no confounders records `confounder_adjustment` as not tested, which is a fail, while the line beside the button said only "No adjustment — the report will say so". | high | fixed | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/ring-entrance` | The verdict is now one line inside the card that ran it — the pill, the check that decided it, and a way down to the evidence already on the page — and the line before the press says the outcome that is knowable before it. Neither is a second copy: the pill's own word is stripped from the server's summary, and the sentence the fold used to hide ("naming no confounders is recorded as not tested") was removed from it, because it is now said out loud beside the button. | D381 | **The loop's sixth step could not be reached by following the loop.** `communicate` is one of the six the strip shows on every screen, and `graphs._recommendation` never returned it — no rung did. What is left is a finding that survived promotion, which is what a report is written from. **`conflicted` and `deprecated` are excluded**: offering to draft a report from a withdrawn finding would be the worst suggestion the product could make. Found on the way: `counts` carried no `reports` key at all, so the condition asking for one was always true — the way a rung fires forever and nobody notices, because the sentence still reads sensibly. Confirmed in the browser: *Step 6 of 6 · Communicate it · Draft a report →*, landing on Reports. |
| D382 | **One honest negative pinned the whole loop on step 4.** A connection that fails validation stays exploratory, deliberately — failing a robustness check is information, not a verdict — and the ladder counted every exploratory connection as *awaiting robustness validation*. So a project with three validated connections and one that did not survive adjustment (p = 0.054 controlling for rainfall, which is a result) read *1 exploratory connection is awaiting robustness validation* forever, and steps 5 and 6 were never offered. Most real projects contain at least one such association, so this was the journey's dead end rather than an edge case. | high | fixed | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/ring-entrance` | The rung counts connections nothing has been *run against*, not ones that have not survived. It is [[D354]]'s conflation of a status with a verdict, on the check that gates the rest of the loop. | high | fixed | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/ring-entrance` | An unfitted model is recorded `not_tested`, with a detail saying it could not be fitted and naming the columns asked for; it still does not pass, because an unfitted model has established nothing. The picker now offers only columns the model can use and **names the ones it cannot**, because a column that silently vanishes is indistinguishable from one the dataset does not have. |

Fixed in ddbae9c. `POST /api/projects/{id}/sources/{id}/full-text` fetches the address the import recorded (never one the request brings) through the guarded fetcher, attaches it to the same source and queues ingestion; Find papers offers *Add and read the full text*; the claims refusal on a citation names that way out. An imported paper is fetched, read to ready and searchable under its own id. Five mutations killed. Evidence: `./scripts/preflight.sh --full`, 2975 passed, 11 skipped (web 3714), locally — not dispatched. Not `verified`. 
| D411 | **Importing a found dataset sends the record page, not the file.** `datasearch.tsx` posts `record.url` — a repository landing page served as HTML — though the result lists the tabular files inside it. Fixed in ddbae9c. Two defects: the screen read `files_listed`, `usability.unknown` and `unchecked`, which the server had never sent — a real result blanked the screen (the tests' hand-made records hid it); and *Add* posted the landing page. Files now carry `url` and `readable`; the screen imports the file (one button per readable file). Dryad/Figshare files are unknown, not absent. Contract tests tie both network searches to their screens' types. Six mutations killed. Not checked against live Zenodo — it answered 504. Evidence: `./scripts/preflight.sh --full`, 2975 passed, 11 skipped (web 3714), locally — not dispatched. Not `verified`.

The recommendation for readable papers and no data names the paper route; the claim test's waiting state reads the paper, shows its claims and offers *Find data for this claim*, which opens Find data with the claim's constructs filled in (not searched). Seven mutations killed. The whole topic-first route (search → claim → data → finding) is joined, not automated. | D414 | **Six canvas-heavy web test files fail under full-suite contention, on `main` as much as anywhere.** `the-catalogue-is-a-door`, `chart-conformance`, `case-compare-screen`, `object-history-on-details`, `scene3d` and `embeddingspace` fail with `STACK_TRACE_ERROR` or a `waitFor` timeout when the suite runs whole, and pass every time in isolation. Measured on 2026-09-16 with `--maxWorkers=4`, three paired runs of the four worst files, a clean `origin/main` worktree against the T188 branch: **main 15, 11, 17 failures; the branch 15, 8, 10** — indistinguishable, and main marginally worse. Run alone the same file passes on both and the branch is the faster (14.7s against 18.7s), so this is contention, not any one change. (Two earlier single samples read 0 and 1 on main against 11 and 6 on the branch, which looked like a regression and was not; three paired runs is the smallest honest sample here.) Full-suite runs now take ~620s against ~77s a day earlier, with **386s of that in setup** — it began biting when `main` grew four test files on 2026-09-16. A green local run is now a coin toss, and cloud CI will go red for reasons nobody changed. Fix is one of: raise `testTimeout` for these files, give them their own vitest project with fewer workers, or find what makes their setup so expensive. | medium | open | Claude (Fenil) | Measured on this machine only. Not yet seen on cloud CI, which has not run since the Actions spending limit was reached. |
